                       i
Statement of Contribution

Chapter 3 is adapted from a jointly authored manuscript, Punt, A., Hanser, T., Werner,
S., and Morris, G.M., NoiseInject: A Benchmarking Framework to Study Noise Robust-
ness in QSAR Models, which will be released as a preprint and submitted to the Journal
of Cheminformatics. Most of the figures, tables and text in that chapter are identical or
similar to the manuscript. I implemented the NoiseInject framework, conducted all ex-
periments, and drafted the manuscript. T.H., S.W., and G.M. supervised the project,
contributed to the experimental design, and revised the manuscript. NoiseInject is
available at https://github.com/adpunt/noise_inject.

Chapters 5 to 7 describe the adaptive hybrid representation and its use under label
noise. We intend to submit this work as two manuscripts with G.M., S.W., and T.H.
as co-authors: one on the adaptive hybrid representation (Chapters 5 and 6) and one
on detecting and handling label noise (Chapter 7). I conceived the adaptive hybrid
search and designed the system, conducted all experiments, and wrote these chapters.
The code was implemented with Claude (Anthropic) within that design, as described
in the Statement on the Use of Generative AI. G.M., S.W., and T.H. supervised the
work. The code for the adaptive hybrid search is available at https://github.com/
adpunt/KIRBy.

Acknowledgments

First and foremost, I would like to thank my supervisors, Prof. Garrett M. Morris
at the University of Oxford, and Dr. Thierry Hanser and Stéphane Werner at Lhasa
Limited, for their guidance and support throughout my doctoral research.
   I would like to extend my deepest gratitude to Garrett, who has supported me in
every aspect of my DPhil. I am grateful for his patience, his belief in me, and the
encouragement he has always given my ideas. His mentorship has been instrumental
in shaping this work and my growth as a researcher, and I have become a much better
scientist because of his guidance. I feel incredibly lucky to have been his student.
   I would also like to thank Thierry for his valuable input into my research and for
always sharing his technical expertise so generously. I am especially grateful that he
continued to attend our meetings and to help me with this thesis after he left Lhasa
and stepped down from his formal role as my supervisor. That commitment went far
beyond what was asked of him. I would also like to thank Stéphane, who likewise came
back to help with my work after leaving his role at Lhasa. I am grateful to Lhasa
Limited for supporting this project, and to Lhasa Limited and the UK EPSRC Centre

Summary of Research Contributions                                                     227




                                           viii
Abstract

This thesis investigates label noise in quantitative structure–activity relationship
(QSAR) models, whose labels carry measurement error, differences between laborato-
ries and assay limits. Most studies of label noise in QSAR assume Gaussian noise and
study representations or models in isolation. We show that the kind of noise matters,
and that noise can be handled once the features suit the data.
   First, we introduced NoiseInject, an open-source package for benchmarking noise
robustness. Across 13 base models, six representations and seven types of label noise,
model family decided robustness more than the representation did; random forests were
the most consistent model. Systematic noise, such as labels clipped at an assay limit,
did the most damage. Predicted uncertainty rose with noise but, outside censoring,
did not identify the corrupted labels.
   Next, we traced why published methods for learning with noisy labels did poorly on
molecular data. Within one dataset, most features of a standard representation barely
vary or do not relate to the label, yet those that vary still add to the distance between
molecules. Distance-based methods therefore inherit the problem.
   We then built the adaptive hybrid representation, which selects features from several
representations for each dataset and model. On every small-molecule dataset, though
not on the reactions, it predicted better than the best single representation, and its
distances tracked activity more closely. A newly published embedding can be added
as one more representation.
   Last, we compared single representations and hybrids under label noise. Noise
was handled, mostly by methods built or adapted for molecular data. Which method
worked depended on the task, the data, the model and the noise, but on small molecules
the hybrid stayed ahead in almost every condition.
   The noise in a molecular dataset should therefore be modeled before a method is
chosen, and the features adapted to each dataset.




                                           ix
Statement on the Use of Generative
AI

I declare that my use of generative AI in this thesis complies with the University
of Oxford’s policy on generative AI in research and summative assessment. It also
complies with the guidance of the Mathematical, Physical and Life Sciences Division
for postgraduate research students and of the Department of Statistics.
   The research questions, methods, experimental design, interpretation of results and
conclusions are my own, except where a citation or the Statement of Contribution says
otherwise. All written text in this thesis is my own.
   I used Claude (Anthropic), through the Claude web application and Claude Code,
from February 2026 to October 2026. The models were Claude Opus 4.5, 4.6, 4.7, 4.8,
5 and 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5 and 5.5, and Claude Fable 5 and 5.1. Before that, I used
ChatGPT (OpenAI), with GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, from December 2025 to January
2026.
   I used Claude and ChatGPT to discuss research ideas I had already formed, for
example by asking how a proposed method might fail or what related methods existed.
The contributions of this thesis are my own. They include the adaptive hybrid search
and the design of its search space (Chapter 5), and the cliff-aware gap, a score for finding
noisy labels (Chapter 7). I did not adopt any AI output as a research contribution.
   I wrote the original Python and Rust data pipelines by hand. I then used Claude,
mostly through Claude Code, to extend them and to implement methods from my
own specifications, including the adaptive hybrid search and the noise mitigation ex-
periments. I also used it to write unit tests and to debug. I reviewed and tested all
AI-assisted code, and checked it against the methods described in this thesis. I take full
responsibility for the correctness of all code. Before February 2026 I used ChatGPT
for the same purposes.
   I used Claude to help write plotting scripts. Every plot in this thesis was made
by code run on the recorded experimental outputs, and that code is available to the


                                             x
examiners. I did not use generative AI to make a figure from a prompt, or to generate
or change any data or numerical result. I drew all schematic diagrams by hand in
Lucidchart. For some of them I asked generative AI for feedback on layout and clarity.
   I used generative AI to help structure my writing, for example to propose outlines
for chapters and sections and to point out gaps in an argument. In some cases I asked
for a draft passage as a guide to structure, and then rewrote it in my own words. No
AI-generated text appears verbatim or closely paraphrased in this thesis. I also asked
for comments on the clarity of passages I had written.
   I used Claude and ChatGPT to suggest search terms and candidate references. I
checked every cited reference against its original source, and discarded any suggested
reference I could not verify.
   Generative AI did not produce text, data or figures in this thesis, so it is not cited
as a source in the main text. All code used to produce the results and figures is
available at https://github.com/adpunt/noise_inject and https://github.com/
adpunt/KIRBy, with the full development history of each codebase.

                                        xx
Chapter 1

Introduction

New avenues in the drug discovery pipeline have opened up thanks to advances in large
dataset collection, analysis, and computation. Therapeutic targets are identified, po-
tential drug candidates are screened and optimized, and the most promising of these go
on to pre-clinical and clinical trials [1]. However, attrition is high, and roughly 90% of
drugs that enter clinical trials are never approved, often due to poor efficacy or toxicity
[2]. Computational methods can remove poor candidates early on in the drug discovery
pipeline, long before clinical trials. This is done through virtual screening, which allows
for a large-scale analysis of libraries of compounds [3]. One standard screening method
is the prediction of bioactivities and pharmacological properties of such candidates.
There is a long history of using Quantitative Structure-Activity Relationship (QSAR)
models to make these predictions, to act as a filter for compounds with undesirable
properties, and thus reduce the time and cost associated with experimental screening
[4]. QSAR models aim to predict the relationship between a molecule’s structural and
chemical features as encoded by a particular molecular representation and a desired
experimental molecular property [4] of interest. A QSAR model requires a molecular
representation and some form of machine learning (ML) model. An ideal molecular
representation will allow a model to learn only the relevant information about each
molecule with respect to the target property. Representations are either ‘static’ hand-
crafted descriptors or circular fingerprints that encode structural and physicochemical
features [5–8]; or learned representations, where the encoding is derived from a predic-
tive model, often taken from the activations of the final layer of a neural network, or a
latent space embedding [4, 9].
   QSAR models are of course, sensitive to the quality of the input data on which they
are trained. Experimental noise can arise from measurement errors, variability under
experimental conditions, varying provenance of the data, intrinsic biological, chemical


                                            1
variability, and human error. Even the very measures of model performance may be
flawed if test and validation sets are laden with noise. Kolmar and Grulke [10] showed
that error in the test labels can make a model look worse than it is: a model trained
on noisy data can predict the noise-free values more accurately than an evaluation on
the noisy test set indicates. There is also a limit to how much noise the model can
tolerate; Jorner et al. [11] argued that once their model’s error approached the exper-
imental error, more training data would not be expected to improve accuracy much.
In the context of activity prediction, the inherent variability within experimental se-
tups and assays, batch effects, measurement errors, and biological variability introduce
additional layers of complexity [10, 12, 13]. All of this increases the likelihood of en-
countering noise in the data. Even the molecular representations in the training data
themselves may be incorrect or misleading [14, 15], and chemical datasets contain too
few repeat measurements of each compound to characterize the distribution the label is
drawn from. Thus, models treat a single label as the population mean [10]. Here pIC50
and pKi are the negative base-10 logarithms of the IC50 and Ki in molar units, so a
difference of one log unit is a tenfold difference in concentration. Repeat measurements
of the same compound-target pair have a standard deviation of about 0.68 log units
for pIC50 and 0.54 log units for pKi [12, 13], which is about a 4.8-fold and a 3.5-fold
difference in concentration. Restricting to measurements from a single assay lowers
the mean disagreement from 0.50 to 0.27 log units [16], or from about a 3.2-fold to a
1.9-fold difference.
   This thesis addresses the challenge of conducting molecular property prediction-
when the underlying experimental data, or “labels” are noisy. Most work on noise in
QSAR modeling assumes Gaussian label noise and evaluates either representations or
model architectures in isolation. Cortés-Ciriano et al. [17] examined 12 algorithms
across multiple noise levels but used fixed representations, and studies that compare
representations typically use clean data. No work has systematically compared the
relative importance of representation versus architecture choice for label noise robust-
ness, asked whether probabilistic methods justify their computational overhead under
noise, or tested whether findings generalize across different types of injected noise. This
thesis fills that gap. We will also discuss noise mitigation, using the results of our noise
robustness study. We explore why many noise mitigation techniques underperform
in cheminformatics data and methods to address this problem in the form of novel
molecular representations.
   The first half of this thesis therefore asks four questions. First, how does the choice
of molecular representation and model architecture explain the variance in predictive

                                             2
performance, and does that division change when you instead consider how much per-
formance a QSAR model is able to retain under increasing label noise? Second, what
does the choice of model provide under label noise? Within this question we compare
the effects of deterministic and probabilistic modeling. Third, does the type of label
noise matter, or just the quantity of it? We explore different techniques of generating
and adding label noise to both clean data, as well as data with inherent experimental
noise. Fourth, what is the impact of artificial label noise on a model’s uncertainty esti-
mation? Does the estimated uncertainty rise with increased label noise and can these
uncertainty values point to which labels are corrupted? Together, these questions aim
to identify what, if anything, makes a QSAR model robust to noise. To answer them
we introduce NoiseInject, an open-source Python package for benchmarking noise ro-
bustness and uncertainty quantification (Section 2.6.1).
   In an early study with Gaussian noise added to QM9 labels, the configurations
of published methods for noisy labels that kept the least performance all compared
molecules by a distance. We hypothesized that the distance was at fault. On a given
dataset, most features of a general-purpose representation either barely vary or do not
relate to the property. Yet each one that varies adds a term to the distance, so to-
gether they dominate it (Chapter 4). We propose instead to compute distances over
a hybrid representation: a fixed number of task-relevant features selected from several
representations. A first version showed that the selected features work as ordinary
model inputs too, so the question became how to choose them (Chapter 5). Chapter 6
tests whether distance in the hybrid tracks the label better, and Chapter 7 returns to
noisy labels, comparing the single representations with the hybrid. The same prob-
lem appears in antibody representations, where the features that matter are few and
buried in representations of the whole sequence. For this reason we test the method
on antibodies, and on chemical reactions.
   The second half asks whether the representation itself can be adapted to the task:

  1. How can the best set of features be selected for a given dataset and model, and
     does the method carry over from small molecules to antibodies and reactions?

  2. Does distance in a hybrid representation track the label better than distance in
     the best single representation, and do methods built on nearest neighbors work
     better in it?

  3. Can noisy labels be found and corrected, and do hybrid representations stay
     ahead of single representations through that correction?


                                            3
  4. Does weighting features by their importance help in building hybrid representa-
      tions, in model training and in finding noisy labels?


1.1     Outline of the thesis and contributions
Chapter 2 introduces molecular representations, model families, molecular similarity
and activity cliffs, and the sources of noise in molecular data. It defines the seven noise
conditions used in Chapter 3 and the three groups of methods for noisy labels (noise
detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training), and closes with artificial noise
injection, and evaluation metrics.
   Chapter 3 looks into what makes a QSAR model robust to label noise. The pri-
mary prediction target is the HOMO–LUMO gap in QM9, whose computed labels
carry negligible measurement noise. The same experiments were also run on three
assay datasets: LogD, Caco-2 and hERG Ki . We compare seven different types of
label noise, referred to as noise conditions. We matched the quantity of noise to the
training set label spread for all but one of the noise conditions. We measure noise
robustness as the proportion of predictive performance retained as artificial label noise
increases. We found that model family was the main factor in noise robustness, and
choice of molecular representation matters more for predictive performance than noise
robustness. Strong predictive performance on clean labels does not imply robustness.
LightGBM and XGBoost predicted well on clean labels but lost the most with increas-
ing label noise, while random forests were the most consistent model across datasets,
representations, and noise conditions. We replicated systematic noise by giving every
scaffold family its own offset and by clipping labels at a made-up assay limit. Models
struggled the most when training on this systematic noise. Label noise raised mean
predicted uncertainty for the probabilistic models which estimate it; however, only the
BNNs with a variance head, the heteroscedastic GP and the heteroscedastic VBLL
networks correctly attributed the rise in uncertainty to the data rather than to the
model itself, though not on every dataset and representation.
   Chapter 4 takes a turn to look at another topic: molecular similarity. The natural
next step to Chapter 3 is noise mitigation. However, a small sweep of twelve published
noise-handling methods, four that remove molecules and retrain and eight that change
the training, showed disappointing results. None of the methods that incorporated
the concept of molecular similarity showed consistently promising results. On a given
dataset, standard representations carry many features that do not vary, and some map
different molecules to the same vector. Similarity computed over these representations

                                            4
inherits both of these problems, and methods that rely on similarity suffer as a result.
The chapter ends with what a representation would need to mitigate these problems,
which Chapter 5 builds.
   Chapter 5 presents the adaptive hybrid representation. The adaptive hybrid search
customizes the settings of a feature selection process to a given dataset, model, and
budget. It takes in several base representations and selects the most important features
for that task by a fixed budget. This produces a tailor-made feature set, referred to
as the hybrid representation. The actual output of the hybrid search is not the set
of features, but rather the settings that define the feature selection method used to
generate that hybrid representation.
   We compare the hybrid with each base representation, with an ensemble of one
model per representation, and with principal components, recursive feature elimination
and random features of the same size. We read the features it selects, and test the
method on antibodies and reactions, and on data held at separate sites. The hybrid
predicted better than the best single representation on every small-molecule dataset,
with both downstream models. On antibodies the gain depended on the dataset: on
two of them the best single representation was one we built to read only the regions or
mutations that vary. On most antibody datasets, the large expression scan included,
an ensemble of one model per representation did better. On the reactions the hybrid
gained nothing.
   Chapter 6 asks whether a hybrid representation repairs the failures of similarity
described in Chapter 4. We build hybrids whose features are chosen for distance,
ranking each candidate by how well each molecule’s five nearest neighbors predict its
label. On every small-molecule dataset, distance in the hybrid tracked label differences
more closely, and the five nearest neighbors predicted better. On the antibody datasets
and the reactions it tracked the label more closely as well, but prediction from neighbors
was worse on several of them. The benefit of a hybrid has to be measured on each
dataset.
   Chapter 7 returns to label noise, with the single representations and the hybrid side
by side. We test noise detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training on injected
noise and on the labels as delivered. Corrections worked best when a known flag, such
as an assay limit or a scaffold family, said which labels were wrong. On small molecules
the hybrid stayed ahead of the single representation in almost every condition, but not
always with the network on hERG Ki . The best method changed between the two.
   Chapter 8 answers the research questions. It argues that the noise in a dataset
should be modeled before a method is chosen. No single method fits every dataset,

                                            5
and simple methods are often as good as complex ones. The chapter ends with the
contributions, limitations and future work.
   The software contributions are NoiseInject and the KIRBy repository, which im-
plements the search for hybrid representations and the experiments (Section S.8 of the
Additional files).


1.2     Collaborations
This work was supervised by Garrett Morris and co-supervised by Thierry Hanser and
Stéphane Werner at Lhasa Limited, which runs the federated learning platform Effiris
[18]. Lhasa also supplied the hERG data used in Chapters 4 to 7; in Chapter 5, two
of its sets and the public ChEMBL hERG Ki set are treated as data held at separate
sites, as in federated learning [18, 19].
   The Oxford Protein Informatics Group (OPIG) pointed us to the antibody and
protein datasets used in Chapters 5 to 7; all datasets are listed in Section S.7 of the
Additional files.
   The adaptive hybrid representation, its software and the experiments in Chapters 4
to 7 are the author’s own work.




                                            6
Chapter 2

Molecular representations, models and
label noise

2.1     Overview
Chapter 1 set out the questions this thesis asks; this chapter gives the background they
rest on. We first describe the molecular representations and models we use. We then
cover molecular similarity and activity cliffs. Label noise follows, with its sources in
bioactivity data and the ways we inject it, and then the methods for learning with noisy
labels. The chapter closes with our NoiseInject package and how results are evaluated.


2.2     Molecular representations in QSAR
The success of machine learning hinges on the quality of input data. Although the
quality can refer to noise, which the majority of our work is centered around, in this
case we are talking about the type of input. Molecular representations provide differ-
ent features with which QSAR models and noise detection techniques can be learned.
However, a potential bottleneck on larger datasets should be noted; the more com-
plex the data structure, the greater the computational cost. For smaller datasets, the
number of parameters is limited by the size of the training set, to prevent overfitting.
   One of the most common and intuitive molecular representations is the Simplified
Molecular-Input Line-Entry System (SMILES) string, which consists of a sequence of
ASCII characters generated by a fixed set of rules to specify a given chemical identity
[20]. A common way to use SMILES strings as inputs to QSAR models is to tokenize
and one-hot-encode them rather than to use the strings themselves [21]. The strings are
tokenized into atomic branches and symbols and mapped to indices of a vocabulary, and

                                           7
finally converted into binary vectors using one-hot encodings [21]. This representation,
referred to as one-hot-encoded SMILES, allows models to process molecular structures
as bit-vector-based fingerprints or as sequences of vectors. Sequence-based models such
as recurrent neural networks and transformers have proven to be particularly successful
in capturing text semantics within a sequence. When applied to raw or one-hot-encoded
SMILES strings, these models can effectively capture the structure of the molecule in
its textual representation [21].
   Another common representation is the molecular fingerprint, a vector containing
information about the properties of the molecule in question. In a typical binary finger-
print, each bit accounts for the presence or absence of a given chemical feature [22], and
in the case of Morgan fingerprints, a given substructure. One common fingerprint is
the Molecular ACCess System (MACCS) key, a substructure key-based fingerprint that
encodes predefined atom symbols and properties, as well as bond types [23]. Another is
the Extended Connectivity Fingerprint (ECFP), or Morgan fingerprint, which encodes
local neighborhoods around each atom and bond connectivity in molecules [23]. These
existing molecular representations are well established and have been quite success-
ful when coupled with QSAR models [4, 24]. They are relatively simple, and as the
bits can be mapped back to one or more substructures, they are human-interpretable,
and furthermore easy-to-compute. However, hashed fingerprints are subject to infor-
mation loss due to bit collisions, which happens when two different substructures are
encoded at the same bit position [22]. They are also based on a fixed procedure and
are not trainable or adaptable to a particular dataset. Alternative representations that
capture different structural, chemical, or physical features are able to overcome these
limitations.
   Recent advances have led to the development of new molecular fingerprints. One
such method is the Sort & Slice (SNS) technique, an alternative to hash-based fold-
ing for pooling ECFP substructures [7]. Sort & Slice begins by sorting all circular
substructures in the training set according to their frequency of occurrence. It then
“slices” away all but the N most frequent substructures, which are subsequently used to
generate an N -dimensional, bit-collision-free binary fingerprint. This approach solves
the ambiguity problem of hashed fingerprints, where multiple substructures might be
encoded at the same bit position, making it very interpretable [7]. Sort & Slice fin-
gerprints outperform ECFP of diameter four (ECFP4) with standard hash-based fold-
ing methods across various molecular property prediction tasks. These experiments
consisted of different data splitting techniques, machine learning models, fingerprint
diameters, fingerprint dimensions, and atomic invariants [7]. Furthermore, Sort & Slice

                                            8
outperformed advanced substructure selection schemes such as filtered fingerprints and
mutual information maximization [7].
   Neural fingerprints are a category of learned representations that overcome the
fixed, hard-coded nature of traditional fingerprints. Unlike ECFP or MACCS keys,
which rely on predefined hashing schemes or substructure catalogs, neural fingerprints
train on molecular data to capture chemical information [25, 26]. These representa-
tions are the result of deep learning architectures that have learned dense, continuous
embeddings which can be used for downstream tasks.
   Mol2vec, which adapts the Word2vec algorithm from NLP to molecular substruc-
tures [25], is a common neural fingerprint. Mol2vec treats molecules like “sentences”
and circular substructures derived from Morgan fingerprints like “words”. The model
learns vector embeddings for each substructure through unsupervised training, such
that chemically similar substructures are mapped to nearby points in the embedding
space [25]. The embeddings of all present substructures are summed to obtain a full
representation. This approach produces 300-dimensional dense vectors that avoid the
sparsity and bit collisions inherent in hashed fingerprints [25]. Mol2vec has performed
strongly on various QSAR benchmarks, matching or exceeding Morgan fingerprints on
solubility and bioactivity prediction tasks [25].
   Transformer-based architectures have also been applied to molecular representation
learning, similarly stemming from NLP ideas. MolBERT applies a bidirectional trans-
former to learn molecular representations from SMILES strings through self-supervised
pre-training [27]. The model has domain-relevant auxiliary adaptations, such as the
prediction of calculated physicochemical properties alongside the masked token pre-
diction, to improve the chemical fidelity of the learned embeddings [27]. MolBERT
outperformed count-based ECFP4 (ECFC4), RDKit descriptors and the CDDD neu-
ral representation on virtual screening and QSAR benchmarks [27]. ChemBERTa
is another transformer-based approach which pre-trains masked language models on
SMILES data from PubChem [26]. ChemBERTa uses a byte-pair-encoding tokenizer
by default and compares it with a SMILES-specific tokenizer [26]. Attention visual-
ization shows neurons selective for chemically relevant functional groups and aromatic
rings, and others that track bracket closures in the SMILES string [26]. ChemBERTa-
2, pre-trained on up to 77 million PubChem compounds, is competitive with existing
architectures on MoleculeNet [28].
   Neural fingerprints provide several advantages over traditional fingerprints. They
produce dense continuous vectors that avoid sparseness and information loss from bit
collisions [25]. They can also be pre-trained once on large unlabeled datasets and

                                            9
then fine-tuned for specific downstream tasks, opening the door to transfer learning
[26, 27]. However, pre-training them is computationally expensive, and they are less
directly interpretable than fingerprints, although attention visualization offers some
insight [26].
    Beyond fingerprints, physicochemical-descriptor vectors (PDVs) are a class of
continuous-value molecular descriptors that encode structural and chemical properties
of molecules [29]. Unlike binary fingerprints or graph-based representations, PDVs
aggregate atom and bond-level quantities into fixed-length feature vectors that
capture a wide variety of information about a molecule and its structure [30]. PDVs
typically consist of hundreds of properties including constitutional descriptors such as
molecular weight, atom and bond counts, and heteroatom ratios; topological indices
encoding molecular connectivity patterns; physicochemical properties such as logP
(lipophilicity), molar refractivity, and topological polar surface area; and electronic
descriptors derived from partial charges and polarizabilities [29, 31].      The RDKit
cheminformatics toolkit provides a comprehensive suite of more than 200 molecular
descriptors that cover these categories [32]. Many PDV descriptors, such as logP,
molecular weight and partial charges, have a direct physicochemical meaning, although
many of the thousands available have no clear interpretation [31]. They are also less
computationally expensive than some 3D methods or neural fingerprints, with many
descriptors simply requiring a 2D molecular structure without conformer generation
or extensive pre-training. This efficiency makes PDVs particularly appropriate for
high-throughput applications. Descriptors based on 3D structure can also be used
[30].
    However, PDVs have limitations. The dimensionality of PDV feature spaces can
lead to problems with multicollinearity [29]. This can be mitigated by feature selection
or dimensionality reduction techniques [33, 34]. Additionally, PDVs encode molecular
properties as aggregated global features, which may not capture fine-grained structural
details such as specific substructure arrangements or local chemical environments, in-
formation important for particular endpoints.
    One of the more intuitive representations is the molecular graph. These graphs can
be categorized into 2D and 3D graphs. 2D graphs rely on adjacency matrices that
encode topological atom- and bond-level features but leave out geometric properties
such as bond angles or torsion, meaning they may fail to capture stereochemistry. 3D
graphs incorporate these spatial features, but are often limited by remaining in a single
conformation [35]. A standard molecular graph is constructed such that each molecule
is represented as G = (V, E) with node features xi and edge features eij . In one common

                                           10
featurization, nodes encode atom type, chirality, hybridization and aromaticity, and
edges encode bond type, ring membership, direction and stereochemistry [35].
   There are also alternative molecular graph representations such as graphs with
quantum 3D embeddings, weight alignment graphs, association graphs containing pairs
of molecules, and geometric interaction graph neural networks that incorporate 3D
structures and physical interactions [36–38]. Du et al. [39] developed hybrid 2D and
3D graphs, combining 3D data, guaranteeing precision and completeness by providing
unabridged information, and 2D data, providing chemical intuitions for guidance, in a
zipper-like arrangement. Voxel-based 3D representations, in which each atom occupies
one grid cell, are too sparse for convolutional networks to perform well; Kuzminykh
et al. [40] instead spread each atom over nearby voxels with a wave transform.
   These graph representations do not highlight specific substructures or specific ar-
rangements of atoms and bonds within each molecule. The substructures contain
functional groups that are related to a range of chemical properties and are poten-
tially involved in downstream tasks related to the function of molecules. Bouritsas
et al. [41] developed graph substructure networks, a topologically aware message pass-
ing scheme based on substructure encoding. The method involves encoding structural
roles based on the appearance of specific substructures and using them in message
passing updates. Since message-passing graph neural networks (GNNs) are at most as
powerful as the Weisfeiler–Lehman test, they cannot isolate sufficient features from the
graph structure [41]. Graph substructure networks, on the other hand, capture these
substructure-related features. Encoders pretrained on unlabeled molecules, whether
graph-based [9] or transformers on SMILES [42], can also produce embeddings that
capture structural features.


2.3     Models for molecular property prediction
There are a wide variety of model architectures that can be used for QSAR modeling.
Starting off with simpler and more explainable models, we have tree-based methods.
Decision trees are non-parametric supervised learning algorithms that perform recursive
binary partitioning of the feature space [43]. A decision tree builds a predictive model
by learning a series of if-then rules that recursively split the data based on given feature
values, creating a tree where internal nodes represent decision rules, branches represent
the results of those decisions, and leaf nodes contain predicted values or class labels [43].
Each recursive split is chosen to maximize homogeneity within the resulting partitions.
For classification, Gini impurity is commonly used and for regression, mean squared

                                             11
error reduction [43].
   There are several properties that make decision trees a good foundation for QSAR
modeling. First, they handle non-linear relationships between molecular descriptors
and target properties without requiring explicit feature engineering or transformation
[43]. They also do not make assumptions about the underlying distribution of the
data, making them robust to violations of parametric assumptions that can limit linear
regression or other classical methods [43].
   Decision trees handle data in a “contributory” rather than “consensus” manner. In
consensus methods such as linear regression or neural networks, all training points
fit one shared set of parameters, so a training point can affect predictions far from
it in the feature space. Tree-based methods make predictions based on the subset of
training samples that fall into the same leaf node as the test point [43]. This mechanism
essentially quarantines outliers in their respective leaf nodes, limiting their impact on
predictions for samples in other regions or chemical domains.
   Individual decision trees tend to have high variance and often overfit, particularly
at large depths [43]. Small modifications made to the training data can result in large
differences in the structure of the tree, resulting in unstable predictions [44]. Ensemble
methods are used to counteract this instability, aggregating predictions from many
trees. Random forests (RFs) aggregate predictions from trees trained on bootstrap
samples of data with random subsets of features at each split [45]. Bootstrapping
ensures that each tree is trained on a slightly different subset of data, which effectively
reduces the influence of any individual sample [45]. This is especially beneficial with
noisy samples. The selection of random features at every node decorrelates the trees,
which lowers the generalization error of the ensemble [45]. The aggregation of trees
further prevents overfitting to noise by smoothing out prediction errors from individual
noisy labels.
   Neural networks (NNs), a very popular family of models, can also be used for
QSAR. An NN is a system of interconnected nodes which learn by passing information
through its layers. There are weights and biases attached to the nodes and various
other parameters which the model optimizes to minimize the difference between the
predicted output and the actual target value. During training, the network parameters
are updated using backpropagation, in which gradients of the loss function with respect
to each weight are propagated backward through the network [46]. The structure of
these networks and the hyperparameters used depend on the type of NN. In QSAR,
one of the most commonly used architectures is the multilayer perceptron (MLP),
an NN containing fully-connected layers with non-linear activation functions which

                                              12
transforms molecular feature vectors (i.e., fingerprints, descriptors) into a property or
activity prediction [47].
   A typical MLP for molecular property prediction has a few fully connected hid-
den layers with rectified linear unit (ReLU) activations and dropout regularization to
prevent overfitting. Training is performed using backpropagation with gradient de-
scent optimizers such as Adam, to minimize a loss function (mean squared error for
regression, cross-entropy for classification) on batches of training data.
   However, NN performance on QSAR-related tasks has been very mixed. NNs have
a long history in QSAR [48], and tuned deep networks have outperformed shallow
methods such as random forests and support vector machines across diverse activity
classes [49]. Their complexity and use of gradients make them prone to label mem-
orization [50], which in the context of noise is problematic, as it means that models
may memorize the noise themselves. Although models that are memorizing labels
may initially appear to have strong predictive performance, they cannot generalize
to out-of-distribution samples properly and often provide inaccurate uncertainty esti-
mates. Various approaches have been proposed to improve neural network robustness
to label noise, including noise-robust loss functions [51], confident learning to identify
mislabeled examples [52], and regularization techniques such as mixup [53] or label
smoothing [54].
   All the models up to this point have assumed a vector-based input. Molecular
graphs, on the other hand, require different types of predictive modeling. Two stan-
dard ML methods on molecular graphs include Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs)
and Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs). GCNs apply the concept of convolution to
graph-structured data. Instead of operating on grid-based data, such as images, GCNs
aggregate information from neighbors of a node to update its representation [55]. In
contrast, GINs address the limitations in GCNs’ ability to differentiate between non-
isomorphic graphs. Unlike GCNs, which normalize and weight the sum of neighboring
node features, GINs use a simple sum followed by a trainable MLP, giving the model
flexibility to learn complex transformations of node features. The GIN model’s de-
sign is informed by the Weisfeiler–Lehman test for graph isomorphism, enabling it to
distinguish between molecular graphs that are structurally similar but differ in subtle
ways [21, 56]. GIN features have been competitive with or better than fingerprints
and descriptors at classifying pairs of molecules as activity cliffs (ACs; Section 2.4),
where small variations in molecular structure can lead to significant changes in chem-
ical or biological activity [57]. Descriptor-based models still predicted the potency of
cliff molecules better than deep learning methods [58].

                                           13
2.3.1       Tree ensembles
Gradient boosting methods such as eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) sequentially
fit trees to the residuals of previous iterations, progressively reducing the prediction
error [59–61]. We used a handful of gradient-boosted tree ensembles in this thesis
including Light Gradient-Boosting Machine (LightGBM) [62], and Natural Gradient
Boosting (NGBoost) [63].


2.3.2       Support vector machines
Support vector regression fits a function that is as flat as possible while keeping training
errors small [64, 65]. It uses the ϵ-insensitive loss. Under this loss, errors smaller than
ϵ cost nothing and larger errors cost their size minus ϵ. A constant C trades flatness
against tolerance of large errors [65, 66]. The features enter the model only through the
kernel. With the radial basis function (RBF) kernel, every feature therefore enters the
Euclidean distance between two molecules. In Chapter 3 the continuous representations
are standardized and the binary fingerprints are used as built. A wrong label has
limited effect. An error inside the ϵ tube changes nothing, and outside it the loss grows
only linearly. The weight of each molecule is also capped at C [65]. Under simulated
experimental error, the RBF-kernel SVM degraded smoothly and linearly [17]. Its main
limitation is computational cost. Training operates on a kernel matrix that grows with
the square of the number of training molecules [67].


2.3.3       Gaussian processes
We employed Gaussian process (GP) models using the Gauche framework [68], which
is optimized for cheminformatics tasks. Standard kernels for GPs are not optimized
for the chemical landscape. Gauche defines such kernels for strings, fingerprints, and
graphs that operate on a range of widely used molecular representations [68]. We used
the Tanimoto kernel [69, 70], which is defined for binary vectors x, x′ ∈ {0, 1}d for
d ≥ 1 as:

                                                          ⟨x, x′ ⟩
                     kTanimoto (x, x′ ) := σf2 ·                            ,         (2.1)
                                                   ∥x∥2 + ∥x′ ∥2 − ⟨x, x′ ⟩
   where ∥ · ∥ represents the Euclidean norm [68].
   The RBF kernel is defined for x, x′ ∈ Rd as:

                                                            ∥x − x′ ∥2

2.3.4    Bayesian neural networks
Standard feed-forward networks are deterministic; however, they can be transformed
into probabilistic models (Figure 2.1).




Figure 2.1: One node of a deterministic and of a Bayesian neural network. The deter-
ministic node (bottom left) applies a fixed weight W and returns one prediction ŷ. The
Bayesian node (bottom right) draws its weight from N (µ, σ 2 ), so repeated predictions
ŷ1 , . . . , ŷN form a histogram.

   Unlike standard deterministic ML methods, Bayesian methods model distribu-
tions over predictions using Bayes’ rule, allowing for the quantification of uncertainty
within parameters and labels. Bayesian methods treat the model parameters as ran-
dom variables with distributions. Uncertainty can also be estimated from other non-
deterministic methods, including deep ensembles [71] and bootstrap modeling [72].



                                          15
2.4     Molecular similarity and activity cliffs
Much of cheminformatics rests on the similar property principle: structurally similar
molecules tend to have similar properties, including biological activity [58, 73]. Nearest-
neighbor prediction and GP kernels rely on it, and so do many noise detection methods.
Yet similarity is defined only relative to a representation and a coefficient [74]. Two
molecules that are near-identical under a substructure fingerprint may be far apart
under a PDV. A computed similarity value is easily over-interpreted [74].
   The most common measure for binary fingerprints is the Tanimoto coefficient [75].
For two molecules with fingerprints x, x′ ∈ {0, 1}d ,
                                             Pd      ′
                                             j=1 xj xj
                             ′
                      T (x, x ) = Pd        Pd    ′
                                                       Pd       ′
                                                                  ,                   (2.3)
                                   j=1 xj +  j=1 xj −   j=1 xj xj


which is the number of bits set in both molecules divided by the number of bits set in
either. Continuous representations
                       qP          are instead compared with a distance, most often
the Euclidean distance     j=1 (xj − xj ) . Both measures are sums over the features,
                           d          ′ 2

and neither weighs a feature by its relation to the property. A bit that is off in both
molecules contributes nothing to the Tanimoto coefficient, whereas every bit set in
either molecule counts; in the Euclidean distance, every feature that differs between
the two molecules counts. Chapter 4 returns to this point (Equation 4.3).
   Distance-based reasoning relies on the assumption that the data embeddings con-
tain quality information. When the labels are corrupted or the important features are
scarce, similarity scores over-rely on compounding false or useless information, and the
assumption that similar molecules have similar properties breaks down. Bajusz et al.
[75] found that the Tanimoto, Dice and Cosine coefficients and the Soergel distance
rank molecules almost identically on binary fingerprints. We take this to mean that
changing the formula cannot compensate for a weak representation. This problem is
further complicated by activity cliffs (defined below). These molecules, which poten-
tially contain the most important information for model prediction, can be confused
with noise. Another barrier to distance-based reasoning is persistent noise across la-
bels. The task of trying to determine if a molecule’s value matches that of its neighbors
becomes a lot harder when all of the neighbors have some form of noise as well.
   There are many downstream tasks which rely on the concept of similarity. The
second half of this thesis relies on many such tasks, and as such focuses on identifying
representations that agree with this principle.
   An activity cliff is a pair of structurally similar molecules whose potency differs


                                            16
by a large amount because of a small chemical change [76, 77]. Whether a pair is
a cliff depends on the similarity measure, and there is no widely accepted definition
[58]. The MoleculeACE benchmark [58] uses three measures: the Tanimoto coefficient
on ECFPs, the Tanimoto coefficient on scaffold fingerprints and a scaled Levenshtein
distance on SMILES strings. A pair forms a cliff if its similarity exceeds 90% under
at least one of them and the two molecules differ tenfold or more in Ki or EC50 [58].
Across the thirty MoleculeACE targets, molecules in at least one cliff pair make up
between 7% and 52% of a dataset [58].
   As the number of dimensions grows, the distances to the nearest and to the furthest
point become increasingly similar [78]. Molecular representations are routinely high-
dimensional and sparse. When many of their features carry no information about the
property, distances computed over them are dominated by those features.


2.5     Noise in molecular data
Real-world cheminformatics datasets contain label noise arising from multiple sources
across the drug discovery pipeline. Although noise is often modeled as being random,
or drawn from a Gaussian distribution, other varieties of noise must be considered when
working with experimental data. To properly model experimental noise, we must first
understand where the noise is coming from.
   One key issue with QSAR models is the standard ML assumption that labels rep-
resent true values, as most chemical datasets lack sufficient replications to provide a
robust statistical representation of the underlying distributions [10]. A single mea-
surement, or even multiple measurements for a given label, will not guarantee that
this label accurately reflects the population mean [10]. Most ML models rely on the
assumption that the training data is, in fact, an accurate reflection of the population.
Therefore, they treat these potentially noisy labels as point values rather than distri-
butions, which opens the door to overfitting to noise. However, Bayesian methods such
as Gaussian processes (GPs) do not make this assumption, and neither does conformal
prediction [10].
   Studies involving noise, and specifically the quantification of noise robustness, typ-
ically approach label noise by adding independent, zero-mean, Gaussian error to the
labels. However, there’s evidence to suggest that noise from experimental assays takes
on different forms. Kruger and Overington [79] rejected normality of the differences
in binding affinity between orthologous targets by Anderson–Darling test, found them
approximately Laplace, and found that differences between assays of the same ligand

                                           17
and target closely resemble them. Kalliokoski et al. [13] examined the widest disagree-
ments among 16,844 pairs of repeat pIC50 measurements and found that in most cases,
the wide gap between repeat values was due to annotation error rather than to an
imprecise assay.
   Although numerous studies have explored the effects of noise on ML algorithms for
molecular property prediction, comparisons between different molecular representations
remain limited. Kolmar and Grulke [10] examined the predictive limits of QSAR in the
presence of noise, showing that by learning the underlying trends rather than fitting
to noise, the models can achieve predictions more accurate than those of their training
data. They assumed experimental noise could be modeled as Gaussian-distributed,
using the Central Limit Theorem to argue that aggregated experimental measurements
from multiple independent error sources exhibit a Gaussian distribution. However, Heid
et al. [80] distinguish random noise, which affects all molecules alike, from systematic
noise that differs between regions of chemical space. Using synthetic noise applied
only to nitrogen-containing molecules, they showed that mean-variance estimation can
detect it.
   Beyond just the distribution, the assumptions of independence and zero mean may
fail as well [81]. Bentz et al. [82] examined P-glycoprotein transport measurements
from 23 laboratories and found that, among the laboratories using cell lines, 62% of
the variance in log digoxin efflux ratio was due to differences between laboratories.
When you pool data from different sources, each source has the potential to carry a
shared offset. These sources usually contain families of related compounds, breaking
the independence assumption. The zero-mean assumption is not assured either, espe-
cially when true values fall above or below an assay’s detection limit, called censoring.
Svensson et al. [83] count censored labels in 15 industrial assays, of which 13 carry cen-
sored labels and eight sit between a quarter and two thirds censored. Chapter 3 tests
noise conditions that vary the distribution, the correlation, and the bias of injected
error separately, at a matched quantity of noise for every condition except censoring.
   Label noise enters bioactivity data at every step from the assay plate to the
database. Table 2.1 lists these sources, with examples of each and the kind of error
each tends to produce. The kind of error describes how the error is distributed across
molecules, which is the property that the noise conditions in Section 2.5.1 vary.
   Although the general assumption in much of the noise analysis and mitigation lit-
erature is that noise, specifically label noise, will have a negative effect on the model,
this is not necessarily the case. There are multiple instances of using noise as a method
of regularization. In linear regression, appending observations of pure noise shrinks

Furthermore, adding artificial noise drawn from a Gaussian distribution to the acti-
vation layers of a neural network is considered another form of regularization [96].
These studies indicate that the presence of noise may act as a form of regularization in
some contexts. Neither corrupts the labels of real observations, so whether label noise
regularizes QSAR and quantitative structure–property relationship (QSPR) models
remains open.


2.5.1      Artificial noise injection
Our objective is to evaluate the robustness of QSAR models against label noise. To do
so, we “inject” artificial noise into the labels during training while test data remain free
of noise. Validation labels are given noise of the same type and quantity as the training
labels, drawn independently. This artificial noise simulates real-world experimental
noise. The clean target label yi is replaced by a noisy label ỹi = yi + ϵi , where ϵi


                                             19
is the artificial noise injected at the index i, the value of which is determined by a
specified noise strategy. As we increased the quantity of noise, we measured how much
predictive performance fell.
     We explored seven different ways of adding noise, which we refer to as noise con-
ditions and which are illustrated in Figure 2.2 and summarized in Table 2.2. Three
conditions vary the shape of the distribution from which the error is drawn, and three
concentrate the error on some molecules or share it within Bemis–Murcko scaffold
families. The seventh condition, censoring, clips labels at a limit.
     Molecules that share a Bemis–Murcko scaffold, the ring systems and linking chains
left once all other side chains are removed [97], form a scaffold family.
     For a given label, every condition except censoring delivers the same expected
quantity of noise at a set noise level. These levels are defined as a fraction of the
standard deviation of clean training labels. The noise added to the label of molecule i
