2.5.2       Learning with noisy labels
In this thesis, methods for learning with noisy labels (LNL) fall into three groups: noise
detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training (Table 2.3). The groups follow
from what selects the molecules and what is done to them. Song et al. [51] sort deep
learning methods for noisy labels differently, into five groups by the part of training
they change: robust architectures, robust regularization, robust loss functions, loss


                                                 22
adjustment and sample selection. Figure 2.3 shows how a noise mitigation method is
evaluated.

Table 2.3: Terms used throughout this thesis for methods that handle noisy labels.
A noise mitigation method is named by both halves: what selects the molecules, and
what is then done to them. The group follows from the two.

      What selects the molecules
      Detector               A score inferred from the features and the labels,
                             saying how wrong each label looks. The score is
                             that molecule’s suspicion score.
      Known flag             Already recorded in the data, with nothing inferred:
                             a label at the assay limit, how many times a molecule
                             was measured, which source reported it, which scaf-
                             fold family it belongs to.
      Every molecule         No selection. The method acts on all the labels.
      What is done to them
      Delete                 The molecule leaves the training set.
      Down-weight            The molecules and labels are kept and each molecule
                             carries a weight.
      Move the label         The label shifts toward a target by some distance.
                             The target is a neighbors’ mean, a predicted value,
                             a group mean or an assay limit.
      The groups
      Noise detection       A detector alone, with nothing done afterwards.
      Noise mitigation      A detector, a known flag or every molecule, and then
                            one of the three actions.
      Noise-robust training Nothing is selected and the data is untouched. The
                            loss, the kernel, a noise term, the training loop or
                            the model changes.


   No single method is expected to win. Averaged over all problems, no learning
algorithm outperforms another [101]. Neighbor-based cleaning relies on the similar
property principle. It dates back to editing a training set by its nearest neighbors
[102]. However, activity cliffs break that principle, and QSAR models frequently fail
to predict cliffs [57]. Chapter 4 tests the principle, and Chapter 7 tests neighbor-based
noise mitigation with the adaptive hybrid representation as the feature space.


2.6     Implementation of the noise conditions
The noise conditions are implemented twice: NoiseInject, an open-source Python pack-
age, injects the noise on the three assay datasets of Chapter 3 and computes the ro-

                                           23
Figure 2.3: Evaluation of a noise mitigation method, with QM9 as the example. A
random forest trained on noisy training labels is compared with one trained on labels
corrected by a Bayesian model; the test labels stay clean, and both forests are scored
on them by R2 (higher is better).


bustness and uncertainty metrics. QM9 uses a separate Python and Rust pipeline with
its own Rust implementation of the same conditions. An automated check confirms
that the two deliver the same noise statistics on the same labels, groups and seeds.


2.6.1     The NoiseInject package
NoiseInject (version 0.3.0) evaluates model robustness and uncertainty calibration un-
der artificial label noise, for both regression and classification. It operates on arrays of
labels and on the predictions of any externally trained model, which makes it model-
agnostic, and its core depends only on NumPy, pandas, SciPy and scikit-learn. Results
are returned as pandas data frames with one row per noise level and one aggregate
summary row. The software is available under an MIT license and archived on Zenodo
[103].


Regression noise

The regression injector sets a noise shape (Gaussian, Student-t or Laplace) and a tar-
geting rule (uniform, grouped-wider, grouped-shifted, outlier or censoring) separately.
Each named condition is one combination of the two with its parameters, and the
name is recorded with every result. The requested noise τ is the noise level times the
standard deviation of the clean training labels. The injector solves for the scale τ /G in
closed form (Equation 2.4), and a level of zero returns the labels unchanged. The af-
fected molecules are selected by a separately seeded random number generator, so they

                                            24
are the same at every noise level while the noise draws change. Under grouped-wider,
whole scaffold families are added in random order until they cover as close as possible
to 20% of the training molecules.
   Censoring is parameterized by the fraction f of labels clipped, with its limit at the
(1 − f ) quantile of the clean training labels. On the training split the censored set is
the k = round(f n) largest labels; on any other split it is whichever molecules lie past
the training limit.
   Each injection also returns a record of the target and delivered root-mean-square
noise, the factor G, the solved scale, the mean of ϵ, the fraction of molecules affected,
the group sizes and the random seeds. The delivered noise is checked against a tolerance
band, and a draw outside it raises a warning (Additional file 1).
   A second function returns the noise scale each molecule receives, without drawing
noise; we apply it to held-out molecules. Under uniform and grouped-shifted targeting
every molecule gets the same scale, so the package flags these scales as degenerate
instead of reporting a rank correlation of zero. A separate injector sizes the noise
against a unit supplied by the user; in Chapter 7 this unit is the standard deviation
of a model’s out-of-fold residuals on the clean training labels. It either adds Gaussian
noise or shifts 2%, 5% or 10% of labels. The Gaussian noise has variance the level times
the square of the unit. Here the level scales the variance, not the standard deviation as
in Equation 2.4. Each shift is the level times the unit, with a random sign or upward
only. It can also add Gaussian noise whose standard deviation follows a per-molecule
covariate or a hidden log-normal draw, scaled to the same mean variance.


Classification noise

The package provides six label-flipping strategies: uniform flips, flip rates set by class
frequency, asymmetric flip rates for binary tasks, a random flip rate for each sample, a
flip rate for each class, and flips directed by a confusion matrix. Because the realized flip
rate usually differs from the nominal probability, a binary search finds the probability
that changes a target fraction of labels.


Robustness metrics

For regression, the package computes R2 , the root mean squared error (RMSE) and
the mean absolute error at every noise level. For classification, it computes accuracy,
together with macro- and weighted-averaged precision, recall and F1 score and a per-
class breakdown. Robustness is read from the retention curve ret(x) = m(x)/m(0),


                                             25
where x is the noise level. Here m is a metric bounded above by 1 for which higher
values are better: R2 for regression, and accuracy, F1, precision or recall for classi-
fication. The primary summary is the normalized area under this curve, AUCnorm
(Equation 3.2 in Chapter 3), evaluated by the trapezoid rule. It is the mean fraction of
clean performance retained across the noise levels and lies approximately within [0, 1].
It is also less directly correlated with clean-label R2 than other robustness metrics we
tested (Chapter 3). A secondary summary fits ret(x) ≈ exp −(x/τW )β to the curve.
                                                                          

The shape parameter β separates curves that hold and then collapse (β > 1) from
curves that fall early and then plateau (β < 1). The parameter τW sets the scale of the
decay, and the fit requires at least four noise levels. The package also reports the clean
baseline and the retention at the highest noise level, flags any curve that falls below
zero retention, and can withhold the curve summaries for models whose clean baseline
is too low.


Uncertainty metrics

For a model that returns a predicted standard deviation ui for each sample, the package
computes, at each noise level, the Spearman correlation between ui and the absolute
error |yi − ŷi |, the empirical coverage n1 i ⊮ [|yi − ŷi | ≤ kui ] at k = 1 and k = 2,
                                            P

against Gaussian targets of 68.3% and 95.4%, the mean interval width n1 i 2ui , and
                                                                               P

the expected calibration error (ECE),

                                    10
                                    X  |Bb |
                            ECE =                  ūb − |y − ŷ|b ,                 (2.6)
                                    b=1
                                          n

computed over ten equal-count bins Bb of u; all of these use the clean test labels. Under
Gaussian errors a calibrated model has a mean absolute error of 2/π u ≈ 0.80 u, so
                                                                    p

its ECE is about 0.2 ū, not zero. The Spearman correlation between ui and the injected
noise |ϵi | can only be computed on the corrupted molecules, where the noise is known,
and is reported within each noise level. A correlation pooled across levels is also
returned, but it mostly reflects that uncertainty and noise both rise with the level, so
it does not measure whether a model can identify which molecules are corrupted.
   Three optional wrappers produce ui from a point predictor: split-conformal predic-
tion around any scikit-learn-compatible model, optionally scaled by the label spread of
each molecule’s nearest training neighbors; an exact Gaussian process with a Tanimoto
or RBF kernel through Gauche [68]; and Monte Carlo dropout on any PyTorch network
[104].


                                              26
2.7     Evaluation methodology
Chapter 3 reports predictive performance as R2 on held-out molecules and robustness
as AUCnorm (Equation 3.2). Chapters 4 to 7 state the metric used for each dataset
where it is introduced, and report results as per-fold scores and their spread across
folds, with no significance tests.
   No split or fold in this thesis is random, and no group of molecules is split between
training and test. Random splitting inflates performance estimates for molecular data,
because it places structurally similar molecules on both sides of the train/test boundary
[105–107]. For small molecules the group is the Bemis–Murcko scaffold family (Sec-
tion 2.5.1). Section 3.2 gives the splits of Chapter 3, and Section 5.2.6 gives the folds
of the later chapters and the groups used for datasets that are not small molecules.


2.8     Conclusions
A predictor has two parts, the representation and the model. The representation also
shapes what counts as similar, since similarity depends on both the representation and
the coefficient. Activity cliffs and uninformative features, in turn, break the similar
property principle. Real assay noise is often neither independent nor identically dis-
tributed, and it need not be zero-mean. For this reason we defined seven artificial
noise conditions. Methods for noisy labels fall into noise detection, noise mitigation
and noise-robust training. Whichever group a method belongs to, none is expected to
win on every dataset. We implemented the seven conditions in NoiseInject, an open-
source package that injects them and reports the robustness and uncertainty metrics
for any model. Chapter 3 asks whether the representation or the model architecture
governs how strongly a predictor degrades under label noise, through the questions set
out in Chapter 1.




                                           27
Chapter 3

Noise robustness across molecular
representations and model
architectures

The work in this chapter is adapted from Punt, A., Hanser, T., Werner, S., and Morris,
G.M., NoiseInject: A Benchmarking Framework to Study Noise Robustness in QSAR
Models, to be submitted to the Journal of Cheminformatics. Most of the figures, tables,
and text in this chapter are therefore either identical or similar to the content of that
manuscript. The authors’ contributions are given in the Statement of Contribution.


3.1      Introduction
In this chapter, we inject the noise conditions of Chapter 2 into training labels and
measure how much predictive performance each pairing of representation and model
keeps.
   While there have been many investigations into the effect of noise on molecular
property prediction, direct comparisons between how different molecular representa-
tions respond to label noise remain limited. The choice of molecular representation
certainly affects predictive performance. Deng et al. [108] evaluated 62,820 models and
found that fixed representations, in most datasets RDKit 2D descriptors, outperformed
representations learned from SMILES and molecular graphs. Whether these conclu-
sions translate to noise robustness is our question. Between different types of label
noise, Heid et al. [80] found no difference in model performance between error distribu-
tions with the same mean and standard deviation. We expand on this by considering
alternative patterns of noise, namely noise concentrated in structural groups or on a


                                           28
sparse subset of samples.
   This chapter addresses the questions set out in Chapter 1, specifically how the choice
of representation and model architecture explain the variance in predictive performance
and in noise robustness, what is the impact of the type of label noise, and whether
uncertainty estimates track label noise.


3.2     Methods
We looked at six representations paired with thirteen models, and several variant mod-
els, across four prediction tasks. The hyperparameters of every model are given in
Additional file 2.


3.2.1     Datasets
Although we are investigating the effects of label noise stemming from experimental
settings, for the purposes of testing we inject controlled amounts of artificial noise into
clean labels. Data were extracted from the QM9 molecular property dataset distributed
by PyTorch Geometric [109], which contains 130,831 small organic molecules with
pre-computed quantum-mechanical properties obtained from density functional theory
(DFT) calculations at the B3LYP/6-31G(2df,p) level of theory. We selected the gap
between the highest occupied and the lowest unoccupied molecular orbital (HOMO–
LUMO gap) as the prediction target in QM9. We assumed that QM9 labels were
free of noise, notwithstanding approximations in the level of theory. Each experiment
was replicated 10 times with a distinct random seed and a randomly selected subset
of N = 10,000 molecules, split 80/10/10 into 8,000 training, 1,000 validation and
1,000 test molecules. All models were fitted on those 8,000 training molecules, except
the Gaussian processes, which were fitted on a random subset of 5,000 of them; the
validation and test molecules were unchanged.
   We repeated these experiments on three molecular property datasets with experi-
mentally determined endpoints. From the OpenADMET initiative [110], we used LogD
and Caco-2 efflux permeability, with N = 5,039 and N = 2,161 respectively. We also
selected hERG Ki data from ChEMBL [111], following a protocol inspired by Landrum
and Riniker [16]: filter for binding assays, deduplicate by median pChEMBL value, and
remove compounds with an inter-assay standard deviation greater than 1.0 log unit,
resulting in N = 1,415 compounds. Each of the three was partitioned into five folds by
grouped k-fold cross-validation (CV), and each fold’s training block gave up a further


                                            29
grouped fifth as a validation set for early stopping. A model therefore fits between
3,194 and 3,364 of the 5,039 LogD molecules, depending on the fold, so the Gaussian-
process subsample never applies to these datasets. Unlike QM9’s 10 replicates, which
are 10 independent draws from one pool, each assay dataset is a single fixed set in
which every molecule is tested exactly once and nothing is redrawn.
   Log D is the logarithm of the distribution coefficient of a molecule between octanol
and water, usually measured at pH 7.4. It counts every charge state, whereas log
P counts only the neutral form, and a basic amine that is protonated at that pH
therefore has a log D below its log P [112]. Caco-2 cells come from a human colorectal
carcinoma and grow as a layer that models absorption in the gut. Permeability across
the layer is measured in both directions, and the efflux ratio is the permeability from
the basolateral to the apical side divided by that in the other direction [113]. In this
chapter the Caco-2 label is the efflux ratio on a logarithmic scale. A ratio above about
2 suggests the compound is pumped out by a transporter such as P-glycoprotein. The
hERG gene encodes a cardiac potassium channel. Drugs that block it can prolong the
QT interval and cause arrhythmia, so hERG binding is screened early in drug discovery
[114]. The hERG Ki dataset holds binding affinities for this channel, as pKi values.
   All splits and CV folds were grouped by the chirality-blind Bemis–Murcko scaffold,
implemented such that each distinct acyclic molecule forms its own group, and groups
are filled in random order. Prior to modeling, all molecular structures were sanitized
and canonicalized using RDKit to remove invalid valence states and standardize atom
and bond typing [32]. Prediction targets were standardized to zero mean and unit
variance. Experiments that track uncertainty were run on 10 replicates on QM9 and
five folds on each assay dataset.


3.2.2    Representations
In this study, we evaluated six molecular representations: extended-connectivity fin-
gerprints of diameter four (ECFP4), Avalon, Sort & Slice (SNS), physicochemical de-
scriptors (PDV), ChemBERTa and Molecular Hypergraph Grammar GNNs (MHG-
GNNs). The same six were used on QM9 and on the three assay datasets. We used
the standard ECFP with radius r = 2 with d = 2048 bits [5, 32]. Sort & Slice
takes the same substructures as ECFP4, but it ranks them by prevalence and selects
the top L (L = 1024) [7]. We fitted the Sort & Slice vocabulary on the training
molecules alone, refitting it for each QM9 replicate and for each CV fold. Avalon
is a 2,048-bit fingerprint, generated with the Avalon toolkit through RDKit [8, 32].


                                          30
The PDV representation is a vector of 200 physicochemical descriptors computed with
RDKit’s MolecularDescriptorCalculator [31, 32]; the 200 are listed in Additional
file 2, and the list is fixed in our code rather than taken from RDKit’s default descrip-
tor set, which grows between releases. ChemBERTa embeddings are the mean over
the non-padding token embeddings of the DeepChem/ChemBERTa-77M-MTR checkpoint,
producing 384 features [28]. MHG-GNNs are a GIN-based autoencoder pre-trained on
about 1.38 million PubChem molecules using β-VAE loss, producing 1024-dimensional
embeddings through iterative message passing and found in the published mhggnn_
pretrained_model_0724_2023 checkpoint [9]. We z-score normalized PDV, Chem-
BERTa and MHG-GNN per feature, using the training molecules’ mean and standard
deviation; ECFP4, Avalon and Sort & Slice reach the model as built.


3.2.3     Models
To understand model behavior in the presence of label noise, we explored a range of
model families. We fitted tree ensembles, a support vector machine, Gaussian processes
and neural networks. Several are variants of one another, differing in the probabilistic
machinery they add, and Additional file 2 gives every configuration with its settings.
   Random Forests (RFs) are one of the most common choices for QSAR model-
ing thanks to their robustness and interpretability [115]. Quantile Regression Forests
(QRFs) hold the distribution of training labels in each leaf and compute quantiles over
all trees [116]. While we used 100 trees for RF, we observed a noisy quantile estimate
with 100 trees for QRF, prompting us to use 300 instead. To observe the effect of
the quantile mechanism, we then fit a second RF at 300 trees so the two models were
identical in all other settings.
   We also used eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) [59], Light Gradient-Boosting
Machine (LightGBM) [62], and Natural Gradient Boosting (NGBoost) [63]. NGBoost
extends deterministic gradient boosting by treating the parameters of a chosen para-
metric distribution as regression targets and learns them through boosting with a
natural gradient update rule [63]. NGBoost relies on scoring off validation data, which
differs by dataset. On QM9 the validation split is already held out of every fit. How-
ever, on the three assay datasets NGBoost holds out its own scaffold-grouped fifth of
the rows it is handed, which already exclude the validation molecules.
   Support vector machines (SVMs) are a well-established baseline in QSAR modeling
[17, 64]. We used a radial basis function (RBF) with C = 1.0 and γ = 1/(d Var(X)),
where d is the number of features and Var(X) the variance of all training feature values,


                                           31
as the kernel, as it accommodates continuous vectors.
    Gaussian processes (GPs) are a non-parametric class of models that use a kernel to
produce a Gaussian predictive distribution over every data point [117, 118]. We fitted
three variants of GPs, each with a constant mean and a scaled kernel. The first uses an
RBF kernel and runs on all six representations. The second uses the Tanimoto kernel
[69, 70] and was run on ECFP4 alone; it is not reported in this chapter. The third
is a heteroscedastic variant in which the RBF process is given a second network that
predicts the observation noise of each molecule. One of the major limitations of using
GPs in practice is computational scaling; inference has O(N 3 ) time complexity and
O(N 2 ) memory, where N is the number of training points [118]. This can be mitigated
with sparse GP approximations, but these introduce approximation error and can be
more difficult to tune [119]. As such, we used a standard GP and only fit on at most
5,000 training molecules, drawn by a seeded sample. Due to the size of the datasets,
this cap only affected QM9. We implemented these GP models in GPyTorch [120], with
the Tanimoto kernel taken from the Gauche framework [68].
    We tested two deterministic feed-forward architectures, both fully connected and
both implemented in PyTorch [121]: (i) NN-α with two hidden layers of sizes 128 and
64, and dropout after each hidden layer and (ii) NN-β with two hidden layers, each of
size 128, with dropout applied before the output layer. Each model was trained using
the Adam optimizer with early stopping on validation loss. We used Bayesian trans-
formations to convert NNs into Bayesian neural networks (BNNs), and implemented
them using PyTorch [121] with ReLU activations and dropout regularization (p = 0.2).
Additional file 2 gives their full widths, dropout fractions, learning rates and batch
size.
    To get these networks producing uncertainty estimates, we applied four proba-
bilistic transformations to each architecture, producing eight probabilistic networks.
The first replaces every linear layer with a Bayesian layer carrying a Gaussian prior
N (0, 0.12 ) on its weights, producing BNN-α and BNN-β. They are trained on an ev-
idence lower bound whose Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence term is divided by the
number of training molecules. While this is a straightforward approach, it is computa-
tionally expensive. We explored alternative techniques like our second variant, where
we add an output head that predicts a variance to the BNN and fit the negative log
likelihood of a Gaussian with that variance [122]. The third, Variational Bayesian Last
Layer (VBLL), replaces the output layer with a Bayesian last layer that produces mean-
field variational posteriors q(W) = N (µW , diag(σ 2W )) over the last-layer weights and
trains by maximizing the evidence lower bound. This can be thought of as minimizing

                                          32
the reconstruction loss plus KL divergence DKL (q(W)∥p(W)) from a standard normal
prior, scaled by 1/N [123]. Because only its output layer keeps a learned observation-
noise parameter, VBLL networks report only epistemic uncertainty per molecule, with
one aleatoric variance for the whole fit. The fourth makes observation noise a function
of the input. All eight probabilistic networks estimate their predictive distributions
from 100 stochastic forward passes. Each network and its Bayesian counterpart were
tuned to the same set of parameters so that NN-α against BNN-α and NN-β against
BNN-β differ by the Bayesian layers alone.
   The thirteen base models are RF, QRF, XGBoost, LightGBM, NGBoost, SVM,
the RBF-kernel GP, and NN, BNN and VBLL in each of the two architectures. The
six variant models are RF (300 trees); GP (het.), the heteroscedastic GP; BNN-α (var.
head) and BNN-β (var. head), the two Bayesian networks with a variance head; and
VBLL-α (het.) and VBLL-β (het.), the two heteroscedastic VBLL networks.


3.2.4     Noise conditions and levels
The seven noise conditions are defined in Section 2.5.1.
   We controlled noise quantity using noise levels, representing a fraction of the stan-
dard deviation of clean training labels. We ran experiments across seven levels, 0, 0.2,
0.3, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0 and 1.5. We added the noise to the raw label and standardized the
target afterwards, using the clean training mean and spread. To control the quantity of
noise for censoring, we recorded every label above the kth largest clean training label
as that limit and used this level as the fraction clipped, at 0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.25, 0.3, 0.4 and
0.5. Censoring is common in pharmaceutical assays, where a third or more of labels
can be censored [83].
   We ran Gaussian, grouped-wider and grouped-shifted noise on every model and
representation pairing. The remaining four conditions ran on selected pairs.
   On QM9, Laplace, Student-t and outlier noise were run on eight models, namely RF,
NGBoost, SVM, the GP, GP (het.), BNN-α (var. head), VBLL-α (het.) and VBLL-β
(het.), each on ECFP4, PDV and ChemBERTa. On the three assay datasets they
were run on the same models and representations except VBLL-α (het.). Censoring
was run on five pairings, namely RF and GP (het.) on ECFP4, and NGBoost, the
GP and BNN-α (var. head) on PDV. On the three assay datasets, AUCnorm under
censoring was computed for the same five pairings, except BNN-α (var. head) on PDV
for hERG Ki , whose clean R2 was below 0.3 in every fold. The uncertainty statistics
under censoring on the assay datasets cover seven models, namely QRF, NGBoost, the


                                             33
GP, GP (het.), BNN-α (var. head), BNN-β (var. head) and VBLL-α, each on ECFP4,
PDV and ChemBERTa.


3.2.5    QM9 data pipeline
QM9 [109], loaded through PyTorch Geometric, was processed by a separate pipeline
where the Python code loads and splits the data, and computes several representations.
The data is then passed to the Rust portion of the pipeline which injects the label
noise, standardizes the labels, computes the remaining representations, and sends the
processed data back to Python which trains and scores the models.
   Each replicate, using its own random seed, randomly selects a subset from QM9,
which is split by a new scaffold split (Section 3.2.1), and the same grouping defines the
scaffold families targeted by the grouped noise conditions.
   Python and the Rust program exchange the data through memory-mapped binary
files on disk, one record per molecule (Additional file 11).
   The Rust program draws the noise for each split separately, in the order its records
are stored. The amount of noise, the censoring limit and the affected scaffold families
are all computed from the clean training labels. Validation labels get all three, with the
amount in label units, from an independent draw seeded from the training seed. The
program checks that validation received as much noise as training. Test labels are never
corrupted, but every split records the noise scale each molecule would have received,
so that held-out molecules can be scored against the pattern applied in training.
   For every molecule, the program records its canonical SMILES, clean and noisy
labels, standardized value, injected noise, noise scale and noise pattern. Each run also
writes a summary of the noise parameters and the amount of noise delivered. The run
halts if a record holds a different molecule when it is written than when its noise was
drawn, or if any molecule has an all-zero ECFP4 or Avalon fingerprint, which marks a
failed featurization. A self-test mode runs the same noise checks on a label file alone.


3.2.6    Uncertainty estimation
Up to this point we have been concerned with how noise affects the predictive perfor-
mance of QSAR models. However, label noise should also affect the uncertainty these
models report. Some models return only a point prediction, while others also return
an estimate of uncertainty, either as a predicted distribution or as the spread across
an ensemble [124]. Furthermore, some report a single value of uncertainty while oth-
ers report one that can be decomposed into an aleatoric and an epistemic component

                                           34
[122]. The aleatoric component describes underlying noise the model associates with
the data, while the epistemic component represents the uncertainty within the model’s
fit. We sought to determine if, as expected, the aleatoric component rises with injected
label noise while the epistemic component does not.
   For the BNN and VBLL transformations, we took the two components over T = 100
stochastic forward passes [122]:

                                 T                              T
                             1X                              1X                   2
              û2ale (x) =         v̂t (x),   û2epi (x) =         µ̂t (x) − µ̄(x) ,   (3.1)
                             T t=1                           T t=1

where µ̂t (x) and v̂t (x) are the mean and the variance returned for molecule x on pass
t, and µ̄(x) is the mean over passes. Here v̂t (x) is the predicted observation noise
variance. It varies per molecule in the variance-head and heteroscedastic variants, and
is one number for the whole fit in the VBLL transformation. A full-BNN without a
variance head returns no variance, so its uncertainty is epistemic alone.
   The other models follow the same split. For GPs, the epistemic component is the
posterior variance of the latent function and the aleatoric component is the learned
observation noise [118]. All variants of the GP produce one number for the whole fit
except heteroscedastic. For QRF models, each tree holds multiple training molecules.
So for a given molecule, the aleatoric component is the variance of the labels in the
molecule’s given leaf. The total aleatoric uncertainty is those variances averaged over
the trees. The epistemic component is the variance of the leaf means across the trees.
NGBoost predicts one variance for each molecule from a single fit, which is aleatoric
alone [63]. In every model, the total uncertainty is the sum of the aleatoric and epis-
temic components.
   No held-out label carries injected noise, so every comparison against the injected
noise was made on training molecules. Each training set was divided into five folds
grouped on Bemis–Murcko scaffolds, and each molecule was scored by a refit that
excluded its own fold. For NGBoost on QM9, three of the five folds were scored to
limit its cost. No uncertainty was calibrated after fitting.


3.2.7    Evaluation
We compute statistics on the held-out molecules, for QM9 the test split and for three
assay datasets the held-out fold of the scaffold CV. Different combinations of dataset,
model, representation, noise condition, and noise level are examined in each scenario.
We used the coefficient of determination (R2 ) throughout. For molecule i, let yi be the


                                                35
clean label, ŷi the prediction, ϵi the noise injected into its label and ui the predicted
uncertainty.
   To evaluate the effect of label noise, we tracked predictive performance retention
under increasing artificial noise. For each QSAR pairing we recorded R2 (τ ) at each
noise level τ , and normalized it to obtain the retention ratio R2 (τ )/R2 (0). To quantify
robustness, we used the normalized area under this retention curve,
                                                  Z τmax
                                             1             R2 (τ )
                            AUCnorm =                              dτ,                (3.2)
                                         τmax         0    R2 (0)

evaluated using the trapezoidal rule over the seven noise levels, τ ∈ {0, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5,
0.75, 1.0, 1.5}, each a fraction of the standard deviation of the clean training labels.
While we tested other metrics of robustness, we found AUCnorm to be less directly
correlated to clean-label R2 predictions. For censoring, we sweep over a set of fractions
to clip the labels prior to normalization.
   An AUCnorm value near 1 indicates that the QSAR model in question has retained
predictive performance with artificial noise, meaning it is more robust to noise. Since we
normalize the curve by its own clean-label predictive performance, a model with a low
clean R2 has less to lose, so AUCnorm is reported beside clean R2 . We excluded replicates
with a clean R2 < 0.3 from the robustness analysis (Additional file 3), as performance
retention ratios become unstable when the clean-label denominator approaches zero.
On QM9 one AUCnorm is computed per replicate, and we report the median over the
10 replicates. On the three assay datasets one value is computed per scaffold fold, and
the median over the five folds is reported.
   To compare the relative effect of the 13 base models across datasets and noise
conditions, we compared their rankings using Spearman’s rank correlation coefficient
(ρ) between clean R2 and AUCnorm . We compared probabilistic models with their
deterministic counterparts using a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test paired on the
replicate, at α = 0.05, separately for each representation and noise condition. This test
was run only on QM9, as with five scaffold folds the smallest attainable p is 0.0625.
   We conducted a separate analysis of variance (ANOVA) for each noise condition to
identify the relative contributions of molecular representation and model architecture
to both predictive performance and noise robustness. For a given metric y, either R2
at a noise level of 1.0 or AUCnorm :

            yf mjr = µ + sr + αf + γm(f ) + βj + (αβ)f j + (γβ)m(f )j + ef mjr ,      (3.3)




                                                 36
where sr represents the effect of replicate r, αf represents the effect of model family f ,
γm(f ) represents the effect of model architecture m within its family, βj represents the
effect of molecular representation j, (αβ)f j and (γβ)m(f )j are the interaction terms, and
ef mjr is the residual. The proportion of variance explained by each term was calculated
as the effect size
                                               SSterm
                                      2
                                     ηterm =           ,                              (3.4)
                                               SStotal
using Type I (sequential) sums of squares, entered in the order replicate, model family,
model architecture within family, molecular representation, interaction. The 10 QM9
replicates provide the residual. The uncertainty on each share is the 95% interval of η 2
over 1,000 bootstrap resamples of whole replicates. These shares describe the models
and representations tested here, not the choice of model or representation in general.
   ANOVA relies on the assumption that observations both within and across groups
are independent. Two features of this study break that assumption. First, each QM9
replicate uses the same data split and the same noise draw for every model and rep-
resentation, so observations from one replicate are not independent of each other; we
therefore entered the replicate as a block. Second, across the whole set of models in
this study, some pairs produced similar predictions that are not independent, so a
method present in several near-identical versions would count several times towards
the share of model architecture. To handle this issue, we grouped the models into
families. We considered grouping pairs of models that share a method or an archi-
tecture, and compared each pair on clean R2 and on AUCnorm under the three noise
conditions run on every representation, separately on each of the six representations,
which gives 24 comparisons per pair. A pair was grouped when the mean difference
between the two models was smaller than their standard deviation across replicates
in at least two thirds of these comparisons (Additional file 4). Five families met this
criterion: RF with quantile regression forests (QRF; 16 of 24), XGBoost with Light-
GBM (23 of 24), NN-α with BNN-α (20 of 24), NN-β with BNN-β (18 of 24), and
VBLL-α with VBLL-β (20 of 24). VBLL did not group with the plain or Bayesian
network of its own architecture (4 to 10 of 24), as its clean R2 was lower by 0.02 to
0.09, and NGBoost did not group with XGBoost or LightGBM (2 and 3 of 24). The
two architectures of the plain networks, and of the Bayesian networks, also met the
criterion (18 and 17 of 24); we grouped by architecture instead, which the evidence
supports equally well. The 13 base models therefore form eight families. Within these
families, the differences between models accounted for at most 1.0% of the variance in
any outcome and noise condition on QM9, and up to 15.1% of predictive performance


                                            37
on clean labels on the three assay datasets. We also ran Tukey’s honestly significant
difference test between the members of each family, separately for each representation
(Additional file 5). With 10 replicates it detects differences smaller than the variation
between replicates, so we report it beside the size of each difference rather than use it
to form the families.
   Across representations, we considered grouping SNS with the ECFP4 fingerprint, as
both encode overlapping circular substructure information. The two differed by more
than their variation across replicates on clean R2 for every model (0 of 13), so all six
representations were kept as separate levels (Additional file 6).
   To assess the interaction between uncertainty estimation and label noise, we posed
three questions. First, whether uncertainty rises with label noise, as measured by
the mean ui over held-out molecules at the highest noise level divided by its value
with no added noise, in each replicate or fold. The second pertains to models that
report separate aleatoric and epistemic components. We divided the increase of mean
epistemic uncertainty between the highest level of noise and clean labels by the mean
of the aleatoric component to determine how each responds to label noise. A ratio near
0 means the added noise was attributed to the labels while a ratio of 1 or above means
the epistemic component grew as much or more. We also compared the mean aleatoric
component with the standard deviation of the injected noise. The third question we
had was whether per-sample uncertainty values track injected label noise, as measured
by the Spearman correlation between ui and |ϵi | over out-of-fold training molecules,
per fold and per noise level. We also report the Spearman correlation between ui and
the absolute error of the prediction. Finally, we asked whether dividing a molecule’s
out-of-fold absolute error by ui ranks the corrupted labels better than the error alone,
and report the difference between the two Spearman correlations with |ϵi |.


3.3     Results

3.3.1    Variance decomposition
Considering the two main components of a QSAR model, the molecular representa-
tion and the model architecture, we sought to understand how each contributes to
both predictive performance and noise robustness. ANOVA decomposition divides
the variance between model family, model architecture within its family, molecular
representation, the pairing of the two, the replicate, and a residual. We ran this de-
composition for Gaussian noise, the standard model, as well as methods whose injected


                                           38
noise is related to the feature space, grouped-wider and grouped-shifted (Figure 3.1,
Table 3.1). For predictive performance measured in R2 at a noise level of 1.0, model
family and molecular representation account for comparable shares of the variance,
26.6% and 29.0% under Gaussian noise. However, for noise robustness, quantified by
the normalized area under the R2 retention curve (AUCnorm ; higher values indicate
greater robustness), model family accounts for 47.2% of the variance while molecular
representation for 8.1%. This pattern is observed across noise conditions and makes
sense, as label noise does not impact the features. The interaction between model and
molecular representation explains 19.9% of the variance in AUCnorm , more than twice
the 8.1% explained by representation alone, meaning representation acts on noise ro-
bustness mainly through the model it is paired with. As such, we primarily compare
representations within each model.




Figure 3.1: Share of the variance explained by each term, on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO
gap: a) robustness (AUCnorm ); b) predictive performance (R2 at a noise level of 1.0).
Whiskers are 95% bootstrap intervals over replicates. Model within family, at most
1.0% in every decomposition on QM9, is not drawn.


                                         39
   We compare the variance decomposition on both clean and noisy labels (Fig-
ure 3.2a). For predictive performance, the artificial label noise lowers the impact of the
choice of model family, while the replicate and residual take more. This means that the
variance is left more up to chance and to the underlying variation within the data itself
with label noise, which is the expected behavior. Robustness divides the variance a bit
differently. Representation explains 8.1% of the variance in AUCnorm , against 31.1%
of clean R2 , while model family rises to 47.2%. The replicate nearly disappears from
AUCnorm , likely because each replicate’s curve is divided by that replicate’s own clean
R2 , so a difficult split lowers both and cancels out.
   We include two terms which stem from the data rather than the model, the residual
and replicate terms. The replicate measures how much of the variance can be attributed
to the train and test split, which in this case is a scaffold split. On QM9 we replicated
each experiment 10 times with different random draws of N = 10,000, each time giving
a new scaffold split. On the assay datasets, a replicate refers to one of the five scaffold
CV folds. A large replicate term in ANOVA indicates that the folds differ from one
another. The residual is variance that no other term accounts for.
   While we observe similar patterns between noise conditions, the predictive per-
formance of grouped-shifted noise in particular varies significantly between replicates,
resulting in a larger share for the replicate. We find that 28.4% of the variance in
AUCnorm and 51.0% of the variance in R2 are due to the replicate, with model family
falling to 32.7% and 8.4%. This indicates that the offset applied to each scaffold may
account for more of the variance in both R2 and AUCnorm .

Figure 3.2: Share of the variance explained by each term on a) the QM9 HOMO–LUMO
gap, b) LogD, c) Caco-2 and d) hERG Ki , for clean R2 , and for R2 at a noise level of
1.0 (0.75 on Caco-2, whose clean R2 is only about 0.5, so little is left to lose at 1.0)

3.3.2    Predictive performance and noise robustness
The variance decomposition tells us what aspect of QSAR modeling affects predictive
performance and robustness, however it does not tell us about the characteristics of
individual models. All models’ performance degrades as the labels get increasingly
corrupted with noise (Figure 3.3). The loss remains small until the artificial noise
reaches about half the spread of the clean training labels, and then it begins to drop.
The rate it drops and the quantity of noise retained is not necessarily correlated to
the model’s predictive performance on clean labels. Across the 13 base models under
Gaussian noise at ECFP4, clean R2 and AUCnorm are barely correlated, at a Spearman
correlation of −0.10 (p = 0.73) (Figure 3.4). If anything, the lower a model’s predictive
performance on clean labels, the less it has to lose as the labels get noisier.


                                           41
   The link between predictive performance and noise robustness can be examined by
model family (Figure 3.4). The forests, boosted trees, SVM, and GP all retain rela-
tively high predictive performance on clean labels while maintaining relatively strong
robustness to noise. The plain NNs and BNNs are among the highest in predictive per-
formance on clean labels, and are the four least robust. The two variational networks
sit between these groups, with lower predictive performance on clean labels and mid-
dling noise robustness. Standard feed-forward networks can make strong predictions
under clean labels, however they are not a safe bet under noisy ones.
   NGBoost is a standout case in the sense it is highly dependent on its paired molec-
ular representation. Under Gaussian noise it has the highest AUCnorm on every one
of the six representations, yet also the lowest in predictive performance on four of the
six representations. It is one of the worst performing models with respect to R2 on
clean data. Its high robustness is partially due to the fact that when you start with
a low predictive performance, you don’t have as much to lose. However, it maintains
shallow robustness curves across representations. On ECFP4 NGBoost’s predictive
performance ranks lowest up to a noise level of 0.75 and stays in the lower half at every
noise level. However, on PDV it starts second lowest and ends highest, surpassing the
other models between noise levels 1.0 and 1.5. So when paired with the right features,
NGBoost has the potential to make better predictions under higher quantities of la-
bel noise. On grouped-shifted noise, RF has higher AUCnorm values than NGBoost
on ECFP4, Avalon and ChemBERTa. Even then, NGBoost reports the highest noise
robustness for PDV, MHG-GNN and Sort & Slice. While NGBoost performs well un-
der higher label noise, LightGBM performs well under smaller quantities of noise. Its
performance drops sharply after noise level 1.0.
   As we’ve seen, the choice of representation can have an impact on noise robustness,
but that impact is determined by model family, represented by the interaction term
between model and representation in Figure 3.5. Some models are more impacted
by representation than others, though. In general, networks span a wider range of
AUCnorm across the six representations than any of the other models. Between ECFP4
and PDV under Gaussian noise, the four plain and Bayesian networks differ by between
0.037 and 0.065 of AUCnorm , while no tree or kernel model shifts by more than 0.011.
VBLLs are an exception, as no two variational networks differ by more than 0.019.




                                           42
Figure 3.3: R2 against noise level on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap under Gaussian
noise, for the eight base models with the highest AUCnorm on ECFP4: a) ECFP4; b)
PDV. Medians over 10 replicates. The dashed line marks the noise level of 1.0 at which
R2 is reported.




Figure 3.4: Clean R2 against AUCnorm for each base model on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO
gap with ECFP4, under a) Gaussian and b) grouped-shifted noise.



                                         43
Figure 3.5: AUCnorm of the 13 base models on each representation, on the QM9 HOMO–
LUMO gap under a) Gaussian and b) grouped-shifted noise. Medians over 10 replicates,
on one color scale. Excluded configurations are in Additional file 3; grouped-wider noise
is in Additional file 8.


3.3.3    Noise conditions on the HOMO–LUMO gap
Many studies involving experimental label noise rely on the assumption that noise can
be modeled by a Gaussian distribution. We decided to vary the distribution which
noise is drawn from, as well as the methodology by which it’s injected into specific
labels. Five of the seven conditions draw each label’s error on its own, so the error is
random. Grouped-shifted gives a whole scaffold family a shared offset, and censoring
pulls every label above a limit down to that limit, so in both the error is systematic.
We controlled the quantity of noise by adding it in proportion to the spread of the
clean labels on the training set, so a difference between two conditions is a difference
in pattern rather than amount. One condition, censoring, could not be held to a set
amount as its levels count the fraction of labels clipped.
   We compare Gaussian noise on the HOMO–LUMO gap to Laplace, Student-t and
outlier noise in Figure 3.6. The shape of the distribution from which the noise was
drawn made little difference. SVM reported higher AUCnorm values on non-Gaussian
label noise, 0.937 under Gaussian noise against 0.947, 0.952 and 0.955 under Student-


                                           44
t, outlier and Laplace noise on PDV, while NGBoost lost AUCnorm under every one.
NGBoost’s relatively stronger performance on Gaussian makes sense as it fits a per-
molecule scale under a Gaussian likelihood, which the other distributions violate. On
the three assay datasets with ECFP4, SVM gains AUCnorm under all three distributions,
and NGBoost loses it in all but one case (Table 3.4). The size of the shift stays small,
which is why these results coincide with Heid et al. [80], who found no difference
between error distributions drawn at a matched mean and standard deviation. The
shape of the noise does not change how much performance a model keeps, but it does
change which model keeps the most.
   While alternative shapes of noise may be accurately modeled by the Gaussian dis-
tribution, we do not find that is the case for systematic label noise. Relative to Gaus-
sian, grouped-shifted noise lowered the AUCnorm across all models and representations
(Table 3.3). The models struggle to make predictions when the noise is isolated on
molecules of specific scaffold families instead of being applied randomly across every
sample. The variational networks retained the most predictive performance, while the
plain, deterministic NNs lost the most. The predictive abilities of BNN-α degraded at
a slower rate compared to its deterministic counterpart on every representation except
PDV, while BNN-β did so only on MHG-GNN and PDV. Grouped-wider noise changed
no model beyond its replicate spread except QRF on Avalon and MHG-GNN, which
makes predictions particularly well under this noise condition. Censoring created the
largest drop in predictive performance, AUCnorm was 0.12 to 0.18 lower for the same
QSAR models than under Gaussian noise (Table 3.2). Censoring levels count clipped
labels, though, so the two are on different scales.
   Most of these observations are being made at the upper end of the noise range.
With ECFP4, six of the seven most robust models start within 0.015 of one another
in R2 for clean labels, stay together up to a noise level of 0.5 and separate only above
1.0, finishing between 0.692 for LightGBM and 0.741 for RF (Figure 3.3). We ranked
the models by predictive performance at each noise level. Of the 13 base models, RF
started ranked seventh, but by level 1.0 it was ranked first. SVM on the other hand
falls from first to fifth (Figure 3.8). Because AUCnorm weights the whole range from 0
to 1.5 equally, and the first half of it separates little, it has the potential to understate
just how much the pattern of the noise costs. However, choosing to look at performance
of a set level has its drawbacks, as the ranks shift at a different pace between noise
conditions.




                                             45
Figure 3.6: AUCnorm under the noise conditions run on a subset of models, on the QM9
HOMO–LUMO gap: a) ECFP4; b) PDV; c) ChemBERTa. Medians over 10 replicates,
on one color scale. Censoring is in Table 3.2.


3.3.4    Robustness on the three assay datasets
So far, we have only discussed results coming on HOMO–LUMO gap from QM9, a
computed property whose labels carry no measurement error. We repeated these ex-
periments on three experimentally-obtained datasets, LogD, Caco-2 [110], and hERG
Ki [111], replicating our process of adding artificial noise. Unlike QM9, the experi-
mental endpoints carry their own measurement noise in both training and test labels;

the injected artificial noise therefore adds on top of an unknown noise floor. Repeat
measurements of the same compound have a standard deviation of about 0.54 log units
for pKi [12, 13]. The hERG Ki labels have a spread of 0.915 log units, so a noise level
of 0.6 of that spread is about one unit of that estimated laboratory error. Each model’s
AUCnorm under each noise condition on the three endpoints is given with its clean R2
beside it (Figure 3.10).

a model (Table 3.4). Laplace, Student-t and outlier noise were run on seven models,

these three conditions or under grouped-wider noise, on any dataset or representation.
SVM and NGBoost again move in opposite directions, SVM never loses AUCnorm under
any of the four, while NGBoost loses under all four on Caco-2 and hERG Ki . The two
networks move furthest in both directions. It’s worth remembering that while we are
injecting artificial noise, the assay measurements carry their own source of noise whose
properties are unknown, so no “clean” label is truly clean.
   Of the three noise conditions run on every model, grouped-shifted noise gives the
lowest AUCnorm values on every assay dataset (Figure 3.10). Under it, RF ranks first or
second for AUCnorm with every representation on LogD and with four of six on hERG
Ki . R2 at a noise level of 1.0 depends more on clean performance. By that measure,
SVM ranks first to third on LogD, while RF ranks fifth to ninth across datasets.
   LightGBM has particularly varied results across the datasets. On LogD, it has rel-
atively high predictive performance and middle-of-the-pack noise robustness. However,
on hERG and Caco-2, it loses the most with increased noise, particularly on Gaussian
and grouped-shifted noise. The effects are not as pronounced on grouped-wider, al-
though it still ranks among the least robust there. XGBoost degrades in the same way
on hERG and Caco-2.
   While the results are not identical between the datasets, there are some patterns
that emerge (Figure 3.11). The ranks in this paragraph are among the 13 base models,

while the figure ranks among all 19. In general, the relative AUCnorm model rankings
match between QM9 and LogD, as well as between Caco-2 and hERG Ki with every
representation except ChemBERTa. RF is the most consistent, among the 13 base
models under Gaussian noise it ranks between first and seventh for AUCnorm on every
dataset and representation, second or third on QM9 and first on LogD at five of six. The
GP has the highest predictive performance on clean labels on all three assay datasets
with nearly every representation, and ranks third to ninth for AUCnorm . NGBoost,
the most robust model on QM9, ranks first to third on LogD, second to tenth on
Caco-2 and sixth to ninth on hERG Ki . LightGBM falls furthest, ranking 10th to
13th on Caco-2 and hERG Ki at every representation while being among the highest
in predictive performance on clean labels. VBLL-α is the opposite, ranking first or
second for AUCnorm on hERG Ki and last on LogD at every representation, from one
of the lowest clean R2 values. This fits the pattern of the NNs being exceptionally
varied.
   The forests, RF and QRF, sit in the middle for predictive performance on clean


                                            50
labels, but they are the most reliably robust to noise across all datasets (Figure 3.10).
Their rank barely changes between noise conditions, so no pattern of noise suits them
better than another. Caco-2 is the exception, where RF ranks third to seventh rather
than first or second.
   Other models perform conditionally well, and in some cases that condition is the
representation they are paired with. The GP has some of the higher predictive capabil-
ities on clean labels and under Gaussian noise across assay datasets. It is the highest on
LogD and Caco-2 under grouped-wider noise as well, but under grouped-shifted noise
it falls. The choice of representation has little impact on GP, moving its AUCnorm less
than repeating the split does on every dataset.
   There is a slight consideration with using both AUCnorm and predictive performance
side-by-side. AUCnorm measures the fraction of its own clean R2 that a model keeps,
so predictive performance on clean labels can work against it. A model that already
predicts poorly has little to lose and often scores a higher AUCnorm , while a model that
starts with strong performance has more to lose. The GP and NGBoost are the clearest
cases. The GP has among the highest predictive performance on clean labels and a
middling AUCnorm , while NGBoost has the highest AUCnorm on QM9 from one of the
lowest clean R2 values. While NGBoost’s predictive performance is highly dependent
on its representation, its robustness is not. On QM9 it is both the most robust model
and the lowest ranked model for predictive performance up to a noise level of 0.75 with
ECFP4. But with PDV it’s able to retain some predictive performance under noise,
finishing highest across all the models at noise level 1.5.
   Upon first glance, SVM does not seem like a standout, however, it is the model
whose standing is better with non-Gaussian noise. Among RF, the GP, NGBoost and
SVM, the four models run under every condition except censoring, SVM has the lowest
AUCnorm of the four under Gaussian noise on LogD and the second lowest on Caco-2
with ECFP4 (Table 3.4). It rises to first or second under Laplace, Student-t and outlier
noise and under grouped-wider noise in all but one case. Gaussian noise is actually
the one condition it is worst at. Its AUCnorm stays in the middle, partially because it
starts high, and it ranks among the five highest for predictive performance at a noise
level on every assay dataset under every noise condition.
   The feed-forward networks are the most variable, although in general they have
some of the highest predictive performance under clean labels and are generally not
robust to noise. They are also dependent on the choice of representation. VBLL
networks perform poorly on clean labels, are the least robust to noise on LogD, yet are
the most robust on Caco-2 and hERG Ki . The gradient-boosted trees, LightGBM and

                                            51
XGBoost, were also quite variable, with strong predictive performance on clean labels
and poor noise robustness on Caco-2 and hERG Ki .
   The quantity of noise is an important factor as well. At a noise level of 0.5 the
models still rank by predictive performance much as they do on clean labels, while
by 1.0 the order has changed, most of all on hERG Ki and then QM9. The order of
models by AUCnorm is broadly similar across noise conditions, with the forests near the
top under each.
   While it is clear that QSAR model performance drops with label noise, different
models drop faster than others. How much noise a dataset tolerates before anything
happens is itself a property of the dataset. Up to a noise level of 0.3, three tenths of
the spread of the clean labels, the models are largely unaffected by the noise on the
HOMO–LUMO gap and on LogD, where every base model with ECFP4 still keeps at
least 93% of its clean R2 ; on Caco-2 one of the 13 and on hERG Ki four of the 13 are
already below 90% at that level. By a noise level of 0.5 the lowest retained fraction is
90% on the HOMO–LUMO gap against 76% on hERG Ki (Figure 3.9). Boosted tree
ensembles are some of the first to fall. On Caco-2 with ECFP4, LightGBM has among
the highest predictive performance on clean labels. It is also the first tree ensemble to
fall below 90% of that performance, at a noise level of 0.5. By level 1.5 it retains 26%
of its original R2 , compared to 63% for RF on ECFP4. XGBoost drops off at the next
recorded noise level. However, NGBoost is the opposite, having some of the lowest
predictive performance on clean labels and the flattest curve with injected label noise
across all models. On the HOMO–LUMO gap it never falls below 90% of its clean R2
at any level tested. The networks drop at an inconsistent rate. VBLL-α is the earliest
to fall on LogD, dropping below 90% of its clean R2 at a level of 0.5 and reaching 38%
by 1.5. Yet on Caco-2 and hERG Ki it is among the flattest models.
   The variance decomposition of predictive performance tells us how much the choice
of model and representation matters under clean labels (Figure 3.2; Additional file 7).
Extending that concept, we are also interested in how these shares change under in-
creasing label noise and compare to the variance decomposition of AUCnorm . The
replicate and residuals account for more variance across all datasets. On Caco-2 and
hERG Ki the replicate, which on these datasets is one of five scaffold folds, is the
largest share of predictive performance before and after noise is injected. Conversely,
it falls well below the choice of model family for robustness. On every dataset the in-
teraction between model and representation explains more of the variance in AUCnorm
than representation alone.



                                           52
Figure 3.9: R2 against noise level for the 13 base models on every dataset with ECFP4.
Rows are datasets and columns noise conditions: a)–d) QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap; e)–
h) LogD; i)–l) Caco-2; m)–p) hERG Ki . Medians over 10 replicates on QM9 and five
