3.3.5    Probabilistic and deterministic counterparts
To further explore the impact of model architecture on noise robustness, we examined
deterministic and probabilistic variants of the same core model architecture. The
hypothesis was that a model that reports a distribution rather than a point value may
be more robust to small amounts of injected label noise. We compared variants across
three sets of base models: two NNs labeled NN-α and NN-β and RF (Figure 3.12). For
each NN, we transform a standard deterministic network into a BNN. The BNNs with a
variance head run at different settings from their Bayesian bases, so they are compared
only for uncertainty. We compared RF to its probabilistic counterpart, QRF, with
the same baseline architecture and hyperparameters. Comparisons of this nature are
only possible when the variants carry the same underlying settings (Additional file 2).
NN-α and BNN-α therefore share one setting, as do NN-β and BNN-β, and QRF is
compared with a random forest of the same 300 trees (Additional file 2).



                                          54
Figure 3.11: Rank by AUCnorm of each base model on every dataset, with ECFP4
under Gaussian noise. Models are ordered by their rank on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO
gap. Ranks are among all 19 models; the six variant models are not drawn.


   We compare RF and QRF directly with the same underlying architecture. QRF
had lower AUCnorm on QM9 under Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise on every rep-
resentation, and changed it little under grouped-wider noise, with almost no change
in clean R2 (Figure 3.12). On the assay datasets QRF was less robust than RF under
grouped-shifted noise on every dataset and representation, and under Gaussian noise
on LogD and hERG Ki (Additional file 10). Under grouped-wider noise it was more
robust on Caco-2 and mixed elsewhere.
   The Bayesian transformations that we used to transform feed-forward deterministic
NNs into probabilistic ones produced a slightly larger AUCnorm , but only because they
lowered predictive performance on clean labels. BNN-β kept more AUCnorm than NN-β
in most combinations on every assay dataset, and BNN-α on all assay datasets. On
QM9 the Bayesian transformations made no significant change on either predictive
performance or AUCnorm for either network.




                                         55
Figure 3.12: Change in AUCnorm (top) and clean R2 (bottom) from each model to its
probabilistic counterpart at the same settings, on every dataset: counterpart minus
base, the median of paired differences over 10 replicates on QM9 and five scaffold folds
on the assay datasets. Color is the representation and shape the noise condition. A
filled mark changed in the same direction in every replicate or fold.


3.3.6    Uncertainty under increasing label noise
Until now we’ve discussed how much predictive capabilities a model is able to retain
while its training labels are corrupted with noise. Now, we consider how confident
the model reports its own predictions are in the presence of noise, and whether or not
those estimates match the increased label noise. Kolmar and Grulke [10] found that
the mean predicted uncertainty derived from the GPs increases with the amount of
label noise in the training data. We go beyond the population level and instead ask
if a model’s per-sample uncertainty tracks label noise. One key sign that a model is
able to handle noise is its ability to track it, represented by the per-sample Spearman
correlation between predicted uncertainty and noise magnitude.
   As expected, mean predicted uncertainty is positively correlated with Gaussian
label noise for most models and representations (Table 3.5). There are a few exceptions,


                                          56
namely BNN-β on PDV and VBLL-β (het.) on Avalon for QM9. BNN-α fails to rise in
at least one fold on every assay dataset, though with PDV only on LogD and Caco-2,
and both full-BNNs with a variance head fail on MHG-GNN for hERG Ki , and BNN-β
with a variance head also fails on ChemBERTa and on Caco-2. Kolmar and Grulke
[10]’s finding therefore holds for almost every probabilistic model on the computed
property, and for most of them on measured labels.

  Model                QM9     LogD   Caco-2    hERG Ki     Aleatoric     Epistemic
  QRF                   4.9    2.7      2.4         2.3   per molecule   per molecule
  NGBoost               4.8    3.2      2.6         2.6   per molecule       none
  GP                    5.3    4.1      2.5         2.6    one per fit   per molecule
  GP (het.)              —     4.3      2.7         2.8   per molecule   per molecule
  BNN-α                 3.4    1.4†     1.0†        1.4       none       per molecule
  BNN-α (var. head)     4.1    2.7      1.9         2.1   per molecule   per molecule
  VBLL-α                3.5    2.3      1.4         1.2    one per fit   per molecule
  VBLL-α (het.)         3.0    1.9      2.8         3.5   per molecule   per molecule
  BNN-β                 2.7†   1.2†     1.1         1.2       none       per molecule
  BNN-β (var. head)     4.3    2.5      2.4         1.7   per molecule   per molecule
  VBLL-β                3.3    2.4      1.3         1.2    one per fit   per molecule
  VBLL-β (het.)         2.6    2.0      2.7         3.1   per molecule   per molecule

Table 3.5: Mean predicted uncertainty on held-out molecules at a noise level of 1.5,
divided by its value with no added noise, with PDV under Gaussian noise. †: the
uncertainty fails to rise in at least one of the 10 replicates on QM9 or one of the five
folds on an assay dataset. A dash: not run. Aleatoric and Epistemic: whether the
model reports that component per molecule, as one value per fit, or not at all.



Aleatoric and epistemic attribution

While this tells us that the models are reportedly less certain under noisy data, we
don’t know what the model is attributing this uncertainty to. To answer this question,
we decompose uncertainty estimates into their aleatoric and epistemic components
wherever possible. We expect to see aleatoric uncertainty rise with injected label
noise, while the epistemic component should remain relatively constant, as it is the
aleatoric component that describes uncertainty in the underlying data. The models
whose frameworks allowed for this decomposition include full-BNNs with a variance
head, the heteroscedastic GP, and QRF (Table 3.5). The two heteroscedastic VBLL
networks also report both components, and they separate them more often than any
other model, though not on every dataset and representation. They are not drawn in
the figures, as they were not scored out of fold.
   The full-BNNs with a variance head behave as expected (Figure 3.13), the epistemic
component grows by at most 0.43 of the aleatoric growth in every configuration where

                                           57
the ratio can be computed. Despite QRF’s noise robustness, both of its aleatoric
and epistemic components rise with noise and on some representations, the epistemic
component actually rises at a faster rate.
   Unlike the predictive performance and robustness, the representation actually dic-
tates whether decomposition works. For QRF, the epistemic growth is 0.61 to 1.14
times the aleatoric growth on the three fingerprints, 1.02 to 1.44 times on PDV, and
1.29 to 2.04 times on ChemBERTa and MHG-GNN. Under Gaussian noise the het-
eroscedastic GP keeps its epistemic growth below a quarter of its aleatoric growth on
PDV, ChemBERTa and MHG-GNN except MHG-GNN on QM9, but not on ECFP4
under grouped-shifted noise, where the two grow by similar amounts.
   The noise conditions follow the pattern found in robustness, the three conditions
which alternate the noise distribution show similar decompositions to those under Gaus-
sian noise, while grouped-shifted raises the epistemic share of the heteroscedastic GP
and of QRF in every pairing of dataset and representation where they were run. This
scaffold family-based offset may be appearing as structure in the data rather than label
noise to the models.




Figure 3.13: Aleatoric and epistemic components of the predicted uncertainty against
noise level, one panel per model, on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap with ECFP4 under
Gaussian noise. Each value is the mean over the molecules of one out-of-fold pass; lines
are medians over folds and bands their range. A component that is one value per fit is
not drawn (Table 3.5).


   The aleatoric component indicates that label noise increased, but it doesn’t tell us
by how much (Figure 3.14). For each unit of added noise it rises by roughly 0.4 to 1

                                             58
unit, depending on model, dataset and representation. The aleatoric component can
therefore compare noise between datasets or noise levels, but it does not estimate a
dataset’s internal error.
   Uncertainty estimation breaks under censoring as the process of clipping the labels
narrows their spread, which in turn shrinks the total uncertainty. None of the models
tested treat a clipped label as a noisy one, so they actually grow more confident while
losing information.




Figure 3.14: Predicted aleatoric standard deviation against the standard deviation of
the added noise, under Gaussian noise. Rows are PDV (top) and ECFP4 (bottom);
columns are the QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap, LogD, hERG Ki and Caco-2. Each line is
one model: the median over folds of the mean over the molecules of one out-of-fold
pass, with a band spanning the folds. The dashed line is equality. The heteroscedastic
Gaussian process was not run on QM9 with PDV, and the two heteroscedastic varia-
tional networks were not scored out of fold.



Per-sample uncertainty and label noise

Finally, we ask whether a model’s uncertainty on one molecule tracks the noise on that
molecule’s label. We assess this using the Spearman correlation between predicted

                                          59
uncertainty and the size of the injected noise over out-of-fold training molecules.
   Since Gaussian, Laplace, Student-t and grouped-shifted conditions add noise from
the same scale to every label, there is not much for uncertainty to find. It’s the same
situation with outlier noise, under which a tenth of the data is chosen at random for
extra noise, and with grouped-wider in which each scaffold family falls in a single
fold, so a model makes predictions based on unseen scaffolds and has no basis for
estimating uncertainty. The only case where per-sample uncertainty tracking is possible
is censoring, as the noise is dependent on the value of the label itself. The correlations
are then large and mostly negative, meaning the model is least uncertain on the labels
that were clipped most. On QM9 with half the labels clipped, on PDV, they run from
−0.84 to −0.80 across folds for NGBoost and from −0.47 to −0.41 for the GP, and on
LogD with half the labels clipped every fold of every model except the two GPs is at
−0.29 or lower. We divided each molecule’s error by its uncertainty and found that the
division ranked the clipped labels better than the error alone on QM9 and on most of
LogD. Models are most confident on the clipped labels, probably because the clipping
piles many labels into one value, which the model learns to tightly predict. Ironically,
per-molecule uncertainty can be used in reverse to identify clipped labels: the labels a
model is most certain about, especially when they fall in a narrow range, are the most
likely to have been clipped.


3.4     Conclusions
We investigated how a variety of molecular representations and models perform under
increasing label noise. We used ANOVA variance decomposition across three noise
conditions on four datasets to show that molecular representation accounts for a larger
share of the variance in predictive performance than in robustness. Robustness to label
noise, as measured by AUCnorm , the normalized area under the R2 retention curve, is
more impacted by the choice of model. The representation each model is paired with
explains more variance than the representation alone. As such, a representation should
not be chosen independently of the model it will be used with, and some models
benefit from alternative molecular representations across both predictive performance
and noise robustness. Model family was the primary factor in every decomposition of
AUCnorm except one, Caco-2 under grouped-shifted noise, where the scaffold fold took
the larger share.
   Grouped-shifted and censored noise, the two conditions in which the error is system-
atic, corrupt QSAR model predictions the most. Changing the shape of the distribution

                                           60
from which noise is drawn barely has an impact on model performance, a conclusion
which is in agreement with Heid et al. [80]. Low quantities of noise do not make much
of an impact, on the HOMO–LUMO gap every base model keeps at least 93% of its
clean R2 up to a noise level of 0.3, and grouped-shifted noise separates from the other
conditions only from a level of 0.75 (Figure 3.7). Models’ performance begins to de-
grade more rapidly on the assay datasets than on the HOMO–LUMO gap, which makes
sense as assay data contain inherent experimental noise. Tree and kernel models main-
tained consistent AUCnorm across all representations, while the plain NNs and BNNs
depended on the representation they were given, as did the predictive performance of
NGBoost. Across datasets and noise conditions, RF and QRF were reliably robust,
although their predictive performance on clean labels was middling and on Caco-2
they struggled under Gaussian noise. QRF was the slightly less robust of the two,
falling below RF under Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise. Other models performed
well only under some conditions. The GP had the highest predictive performance on
clean labels and under independent noise but fell behind when the noise was shared
within a scaffold family, NGBoost predicted well only with the right representation,
and SVM led in predictive performance under scaffold-shared noise on some datasets.
Transforming a feed-forward network into a Bayesian one increased AUCnorm on the
assay datasets only by lowering clean R2 , and made no significant change on QM9, so
switching to a tree-based model is the stronger move under label noise.
   With regard to uncertainty estimation, mean predicted uncertainty rose with label
noise for almost every probabilistic model on QM9 and for the majority of models on
the assay datasets. Of the models whose aleatoric and epistemic components can be
split per molecule, the BNNs with a variance head, the heteroscedastic GP and the
heteroscedastic VBLL networks correctly attributed error from the injected noise as
aleatoric, while QRF read that error as epistemic. The success of a split is also depen-
dent on the representation, which is not necessarily the case for predictive performance
or noise robustness. The aleatoric component rises by roughly 0.4 to 1 unit for each
unit of noise added, so it can compare noise between datasets or between noise levels,
but it does not measure a dataset’s internal error. Outside censoring, no model’s per-
sample uncertainty identified which labels had been corrupted. Under censoring the
models’ most confident predictions are the clipped labels themselves, and dividing a
molecule’s error by its uncertainty picked out the clipped labels better than the error
alone.
   QM9 served as a relatively clean baseline as its properties are computed. The
assay datasets containing experimental measurements on the endpoints LogD, Caco-2

                                          61
[110], and hERG Ki [111] are smaller and cover a more narrow range of the chemical
space. As the labels in these data are experimentally-derived and pooled from different
laboratories, they contain some unknown amount of noise. We are then adding artificial
noise on top of the existing experimental noise. These validation datasets supported
the QM9 findings for the forests, with the boosted trees XGBoost and LightGBM the
notable exceptions, degrading on the external datasets. Future work should extend this
type of noise robustness benchmarking to classification tasks and larger experimental
datasets with better-known uncertainty estimates.
   When training on noisy experimental data in the context of QSAR, it is important
to keep in mind that a model’s robustness to noise is primarily driven by its own
mechanism of training, and to an extent the molecular representation the input data
comes in, oftentimes with respect to the model. Of the models tested, RF was the most
consistent choice across the four datasets, ranking between first and eighth for AUCnorm
on every dataset, representation and noise condition. Where a predictive distribution is
wanted, its probabilistic counterpart QRF maintains RF’s predictive performance and
nearly all of its noise robustness. What QRF cannot do is say where that uncertainty
came from. Beyond the forests, the better choice depends on how much noise there is.
LightGBM and XGBoost predict well on clean labels and lose the most as noise rises,
so they suit data you trust, while NGBoost and the VBLL networks start lower and
hold what they have, except the VBLL networks on LogD.
   In this research, we define a noise level as a fraction of the spread of the clean train-
ing labels. It is therefore relative to the dataset rather than an absolute measurement
error, and cannot be mapped onto a given dataset exactly. There are other mechanisms
used to estimate the noise in a dataset directly. When an assay’s controls are available,
the Z′ -factor can be used to estimate noise [86]. You can also use screening data to test
for row, column and plate effects, which produce systematic rather than independent
error [125]. When you lack controls, replicates, and metadata, a label can be checked
against the data itself. Confident learning, developed for classification, flags labels by
comparing each label to the class probabilities a model predicts for that molecule when
it was left out of training [52]. You can use the point at which the model error matches
the data’s error to generate a predictive limit [10, 126]. Beyond formal methods, you
should consider how a dataset was assembled. How many independent replicates were
done, how many laboratories contributed, whether assay protocols were consistently
applied, and how varied are the compounds in chemical space?
   The first limitation of this study is data availability. While HOMO–LUMO gap from
QM9 is a good baseline as its computed labels carry no measurement error, it is only a

                                            62
single task. As the noise is meant to replicate experimental data, this analysis would be
improved by more quality-controlled, relatively clean and well-replicated experimental
data across different targets. However, data of that nature are hard to find publicly.
   Second, AUCnorm is a ratio to clean R2 , so a model that starts from a low clean
baseline has less to lose, and a few replicates scored above 1.05. We tried other measures
of noise robustness, but they were either too strongly correlated with clean R2 or did
not capture the right quantity. The change in R2 at a single noise level, for example,
misses the shape of the curve. AUCnorm is not a perfect metric, but it describes the
whole curve, and across the 13 base models it is barely correlated with clean R2 .
   Third, it is hard to draw conclusions that hold across so many conditions. No model
or representation wins everywhere, although RF comes closest. When working with
cheminformatics data, you don’t know in advance how a model and representation will
perform on the given prediction task. The underlying data is probably noisy, but you
don’t know how noisy it is or what shape that noise takes.
   Given that, our recommendation is to test a handful of models against a handful of
representations rather than tuning a single pairing, since on every dataset the pairing
explains more of the variance in robustness than the representation does alone. The
pattern of the noise should also guide that choice. Under Laplace, Student-t and
outlier noise, RF was usually the most robust model and SVM usually ranked second
on the assay datasets. NGBoost lost AUCnorm under all three, except Laplace noise on
LogD. Under grouped-wider noise, QRF was the most robust model and SVM was close
behind. NGBoost lost AUCnorm on every assay dataset. Grouped-shifted noise was the
hardest condition. Every model lost AUCnorm on every dataset and representation.
RF lost the least of the models that also predicted well, and on the HOMO–LUMO
gap the plain networks lost the most. Gaussian noise was the only condition with a
clear specialist. NGBoost was the most robust model on the HOMO–LUMO gap under
Gaussian noise with every representation, but not on Caco-2 or hERG Ki .
   These results follow from the loss function each model fits. NGBoost fits a per-
molecule scale under a Gaussian likelihood, so it loses ground when the noise is not
Gaussian. SVM’s loss grows only linearly outside its margin, so large errors cost it
less, and it gains under the same conditions. Each boosting stage fits the residuals of
the stage before it, and those residuals are largest in the noisy families. The GP is the
most affected by error that is correlated with structure, because its kernel measures
structure. None of this matters if the data are clean, as on the HOMO–LUMO gap
and LogD little separated the models below a noise level of 0.3.
   In this chapter each model was given a standard molecular representation, and the

                                           63
choice of representation explained relatively little of the variance in noise robustness.
We observe that this choice does impact the performance of different models to varying
degrees, and the representations seem to capture different signals on each dataset that
we tested. Chapter 4 asks how well these representations compare molecules on a single
dataset.




                                           64
Chapter 4

Limitations of the similarity
assumption

4.1     Introduction
The previous chapters have been focused on the impact of label noise. Before we delve
into techniques that are meant to train on, identify, and mitigate such label noise, we
first focus on a tangential point, molecular similarity. All of the aforementioned noise-
adjacent methods, as well as the QSAR modeling itself, rest on the similar property
principle (SPP; Section 2.4), which posits that structurally similar compounds display
similar chemical properties [73]. This serves as the conceptual foundation for QSAR
modeling. The concept of molecular similarity is also used in various contexts to
compare neighboring molecules. This comes into play when identifying noisy molecules
that in theory stick out from their neighbors. There are some important exceptions
to the SPP, namely activity cliffs (ACs) [58, 77]. Furthermore, there is no single way
to define similarity between compounds. This is where the principle begins to break
down in practice. When defining similarity between two molecules, we typically rely
on some pairwise mathematical function, or coefficient, that takes in two molecular
representations as input. Two factors vary here, the representation and the coefficient.
Bajusz et al. [75] compared eight coefficients and found Tanimoto, Dice, Cosine and
Soergel the best and largely equivalent, while similarities derived from Euclidean and
Manhattan distances were not recommended on their own. In this chapter we ask if
the standard methods of comparing molecules satisfy the SPP, that is, whether they
quantify how similar two molecules actually are.
   General-purpose molecular representations are designed to work across chemical
domains. Consequently, when considering data from a specific domain, many of their


                                           65
features show little to no variance. Some representations assign separate molecules
identical feature vectors. Since common fingerprints do not encode stereochemistry
[127], they are often unable to differentiate stereoisomers, which can have substantially
different measured activity [128]. As these coefficients compare every feature, irrelevant
features dilute the result. Different representations also disagree about which molecules
are close based on the information they carry.
   The problem of whether neighboring molecules can predict chemical activity has
been measured before. The modelability index of Golbraikh et al. [129] scores a binary
dataset by a class-weighted share of nearest-neighbor pairs that share an activity class,
producing a single value for how strongly the similarity assumption holds on that par-
ticular combination of representation and data. It is not clear whether a low value is
due to the data being intrinsically hard to model or to the representation not capturing
adequate features for the task. Other work in this area relates to activity cliffs. Sheri-
dan et al. [130] compared experimental error, activity distribution and activity-cliff
metrics as predictors of how well a dataset can be modeled, and found the cliff metrics
the strongest of the three. However, they found that these metrics cannot separate real
cliffs from those produced by uncertain activity measurements. Medina-Franco [131]
points out that some of these activity cliffs are artifacts of the chosen representation,
similarity threshold, or noise in the data. Activity cliffs are distinct from noise, as
they carry some of the most valuable information about the interaction between struc-
ture and activity. None of these measures ask specifically about whether the specific
features within these representations matter for the property at hand.
   We first consider how much the representation impacts similarity measures. Riniker
and Landrum [132] benchmarked 12 fingerprints and two simple baseline fingerprints
for similarity-based virtual screening on 88 targets from three public collections, and
found that the screening performance of the 12 fingerprints was highly correlated. Only
the two baselines differed significantly from the rest. Nearly all of 25 pretrained neural
embeddings were found to have a negligible improvement over ECFP across 25 datasets;
only CLAMP was significantly better [133]. However, when looking into the differences
between the structure of fingerprints, Huber and Pollmann [134] found that fingerprints
which store raw counts are more effective at defining similarity than folded fingerprints
when compared against a graph-based structural reference. Folding introduces bit
collisions, which disproportionately distort similarity for molecules with many set bits
[134]. ACs follow the same pattern: across 15 configurations of representation and
similarity measures on 3 datasets, the representations disagreed about which pairs
form cliffs [135], supporting the point Medina-Franco [131] makes about ACs being

                                           66
artifacts of their representation.
   Any method that incorporates similarity measures, such as AC detection, or clean-
ing a training set by comparing each sample’s label with its nearest neighbors’ labels,
whether by removing the samples that disagree [102] or by smoothing their labels,
inherits this choice, as do the noise estimation and mitigation methods of this thesis.
Coupry and Pogány [136] learn a molecular similarity from graph embeddings, but
independently of any endpoint. Zheng and Tropsha [137] did choose the similarity for
the task. They predict each molecule’s activity as the average over its k most sim-
ilar molecules by Euclidean distance over descriptors, and choose the descriptors by
simulated annealing to maximize the leave-one-out q 2 . Itskowitz and Tropsha [138]
also optimize the number of neighbors and their weighting. Both select features for
a nearest-neighbor model of one endpoint, whereas this thesis selects features across
several representations.
   The purpose of this chapter is to establish the problem that Chapter 5 sets out
to solve. Section 4.3.1 asks, for a given dataset, how much the features of standard
representations vary and how many molecules map to identical feature vectors. Sec-
tion 4.3.2 asks what happens when these features are used to quantify similarity, and
Section 4.3.3 follows the consequences for methods built on these similarity measures,
including a small sweep of published noise-handling methods.


4.2     Methods

4.2.1     Distance metrics for continuous molecular descriptors
While binary fingerprints are compared with the Tanimoto coefficient (Chapter 2), we
need distance metrics for continuous feature vectors. The small sweep of published
noise-handling methods (Section 4.3.3) used the following four:

   • Euclidean (L2 ): standard geometric distance, d(x, y) =
                                                               pP
                                                                                2
                                                                    i (xi − yi )


   • Manhattan (L1 ): sum of absolute differences, d(x, y) =      i |xi − yi |, more robust
                                                               P

      to outlier features

   • Cosine: angle-based, d(x, y) = 1 − ∥x∥∥y∥
                                          x·y
                                               , invariant to magnitude

   • Mahalanobis: covariance-adjusted distance, d(x, y) =
                                                                  p
                                                                   (x − y)⊤ S −1 (x − y),
      where S is the feature covariance estimated on the training molecules [139]



                                          67
   All features are standardized to zero mean and unit variance prior to distance
computation.


4.3     Results

4.3.1     Flaws in standard representations
General-purpose representations are built to span a broad range of chemical domains.
When a dataset covers only a small region of the chemical space, a lot of the features
within these generalized representations become irrelevant, describing chemistry which
is absent from the data at hand. Pairwise comparisons of these representations rely on
these irrelevant features.


Constant and near-constant features

To investigate similarity estimation, we first look at a simple measure: how many
features vary within a dataset. A single dataset often contains a set of relatively similar
molecules. Many features within a generalized representation contain information that
describes all, or almost all, of the molecules in the set, failing to capture the variation
between them. We define a constant feature to be one that takes a single value in every
molecule of a dataset and a near-constant feature to be one that carries the same value
in at least 95% of the molecules. We counted constant and near-constant features from
a set of representations (described in Table 5.12) across hERG Ki , Caco-2, LogD, QM9,
MDR1–MDCK efflux, human liver microsome (HLM) clearance, rat liver microsome
(RLM) clearance and the Lhasa hERG blocker training set. MDR1–MDCK efflux and
the two clearance datasets come from Biogen [140]. HLM clearance and the Lhasa
hERG set are not in Table 5.12; their base representations are those with counts in
Additional file 12.
   The substructure-based fingerprints have the most near-constant features. The
learned embeddings, which are taken from the last layers of a NN, have none except
MHG-GNN (Additional file 12, Figure 4.1). ECFP4 is the worst offender. On hERG
Ki (1,412 molecules) 1,815 of the 2,048 features are near-constant. That jumps to
1,997 out of 2,048 near-constant features for the HOMO–LUMO gap on QM9. 292 of
MHG-GNN’s 1,024 features are zero in every molecule across all seven datasets that
used it. They come directly from the saved encoder output rather than the data, and
the cause was not investigated.

distance is 1 − T ) ignores bits off in both molecules, but a bit on in every molecule
raises every molecule’s measured similarity. It does not use the 105 constant ECFP4
bits on hERG Ki . Constant features add zero to a Euclidean or Manhattan distance;
however, near-constant features are amplified. That is useful if those features define
an AC. If not, they are contributing disproportionately to the measure of similarity.




                                                                           69
Molecules with identical feature vectors

A standard fingerprint- or descriptor-based representation can produce the same fea-
ture vector for two separate molecules. In turn, models using this representation will
produce a single prediction for the molecules with identical features. Identical feature
vectors do not always mean that the representation has merged two different molecules,
since a dataset pooled from several sources can hold two measurements of the same
molecule.
   Many fingerprints are computed without stereochemistry [127]. In this research,
ECFP4 is built with stereochemistry off, and the 2D descriptor sets (PDV, Mordred)
also ignore it, so almost every molecule they produce identical representations for is
a stereoisomer of another (Table 4.1). Any representation that reads from these sub-
sequently merges stereoisomers. Additionally, methods using the Weisfeiler–Lehman
relabeling scheme are bounded by the 1-Weisfeiler–Lehman test (Chapter 2), so some
non-isomorphic graphs also receive the same vector. This is seen in the graph-kernel
features which tie 105 of 1,412 hERG Ki molecules, 56 of which differ only in stereo-
chemistry and 49 in more than that.
   Whether or not a learned embedding is able to differentiate stereoisomers is de-
pendent on its input as well as its individual implementation. SMI-TED, a pretrained
SMILES encoder, ties 56 hERG Ki , 633 LogD and 177 Caco-2 molecules, all stereoiso-
mers. A tokenizer can drop stereochemistry prior to training. The tokenizer of the
ChemBERTa-77M-MTR checkpoint (ChemBERTa-2 in Additional file 12, called Chem-
BERTa in Chapter 3) gives 72 of the 1,415 hERG Ki molecules of Chapter 3 the same
token sequence as another molecule. Since stereoisomers can differ in measured activity
[128], these representations lack potentially useful chemical information.
   Two molecules are tied when a representation gives them exactly the same feature
vector. Table 4.1 counts ties on hERG Ki , LogD and Caco-2 for each dataset’s base
representations and for its augmentation features, the small feature sets added to the
hybrid after it is built (Section 5.2.3). No tie came from one compound listed twice
(checked by InChIKey, which differs between stereoisomers) or from a row of zeros.
   Apart from the MACCS keys, most tied molecules are stereoisomers. Atom pairs,
PDV, Mordred and SMI-TED tie only stereoisomers, and ECFP4, Avalon and topo-
logical torsions add a few molecules that differ in more than their stereochemistry.
On hERG Ki , ECFP4, atom pairs, PDV, Mordred and SMI-TED tie the same 56
molecules, so joining any of these five representations leaves all 56 tied. The WL graph
kernel also ties 49 hERG Ki and 26 Caco-2 molecules that are not stereoisomers, and


                                          70
the MACCS keys tie the most molecules on every dataset, mostly not stereoisomers.
The 3D augmentation features and the learned embeddings ChemBERT, GROVER,
MHG-GNN, MoLFormer and GraphMVP tie none, and joining all of a dataset’s rep-
resentations leaves no molecule tied.
   A model has to give every molecule in a tied group the same prediction. The best
single prediction for a group is its mean label, so ties set an error that no model can
go below,                               s
                                            1 XX
                           RMSEmin =                (yi − ȳG )2 ,                    (4.1)
                                            n G i∈G

where n is the number of molecules, G runs over the tied groups and ȳG is the mean
label of group G. The matching best R2 is 1 − RMSE2min /Var(y). On hERG Ki this
lowest error is at most 0.134 pKi (MACCS keys), small next to the label standard
deviation of 0.915 pKi .
   Single groups can still disagree by a lot. Independent pKi measurements of the
same compound against the same target differ by 0.44 log units on average, with a
standard deviation of 0.54 [12], so a gap larger than 0.54 inside a tied group is larger
than typical measurement noise. On hERG Ki , 6 of the 26 ECFP4 groups and 38 of
the 153 MACCS groups differ by more than 0.54. The widest ECFP4 group is a pair
of stereoisomers 1.14 pKi apart (Figure 4.2). An ECFP4 model can at best predict
their mean, 5.59 pKi , which leaves each molecule 0.57 pKi from its measured value.
   A tied pair with different labels is also the most extreme activity cliff possible. The
structure–activity landscape index of molecules i and j,

                                               |yi − yj |
                                  SALIij =                                            (4.2)
                                             1 − sim(i, j)

[141], is infinite when their similarity is 1 and their labels differ, and here the cliff is
made by the representation rather than the chemistry. A method that judges a label
by its nearest neighbors sees a tied neighbor at distance 0 with a different label, and
may flag a correct label as noise (Section 4.3.3).


4.3.2       Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations
Section 4.3.1 described some of the limitations of standard representations. Here we
ask what happens when two molecules are compared through one of them, as these
pairwise comparisons (Chapter 2) can violate the SPP.
   The Tanimoto coefficient is the most popular similarity metric in cheminformatics
[142], and is commonly computed on ECFPs, although the coefficient applies to any

                                                      72
bit vector. It has been described as the “coefficient of choice” for molecular similarity
[143], while the Morgan fingerprint, ECFP4 in this research, ranks among the strongest
fingerprints for virtual screening and target prediction [144]. ECFP4 and ECFP6
specifically, alongside topological torsion, rank among the best of 28 fingerprints tested
for ordering diverse structures by similarity [145]. However, it is not a one-size-fits-all
solution. Across 96,456 human metabolites, 72.9% had an ECFP4 nearest neighbor at
Tanimoto distance 0 [144]. Stereochemistry had been removed before that comparison,
so these are constitutionally different molecules that ECFP4 cannot tell apart.
   A similarity coefficient cannot tell you which features matter to the prediction task
at hand. And the SPP holds only relative to such a measure. To understand the impact
of irrelevant features on similarity measures, we make the following computation. First,
we categorize a representation’s features into those relevant to the task, R, and those
irrelevant to the task, I, and take the squared Euclidean distance as a sum over both:

                                      X                     X
                        d2 (x, y) =         (xj − yj )2 +         (xj − yj )2 .       (4.3)
                                      j∈R                   j∈I

   Every non-constant irrelevant feature adds one further term, so the irrelevant por-
tion grows linearly with |I|. After standardization each feature contributes the same
amount on average over all pairs, so the share of the distance carrying signal is about
|R|/(|R|+|I|). When we use a general-purpose representation like ECFP4 on a dataset
spanning a small portion of the chemical space, |I| can be a lot larger than |R|. Con-
stant features contribute nothing, while near-constant features have a strong impact.
The worst-case scenario involves a single rare bit set in only a handful of molecules.
After standardization, uncommon bits take on larger values, outweighing common fea-
tures that may be more relevant. If this bit is irrelevant to the task at hand, the
similarity measure suffers.
   Larger representations compound the problem. As the number of features grows,
the nearest and farthest molecules end up at nearly the same distance, meaning that
these pairwise distances are less able to discriminate, as explained in Chapter 2. Two
measures of this effect are the relative contrast, defined as the difference between the
largest and smallest measured distance divided by the smallest [146], and hubness, a
measure of how unevenly the molecules appear in other molecules’ nearest neighbors
[147]. These errors propagate along larger data pipelines, starting from the represen-
tation into similarity, from similarity into the set of neighbors a molecule is compared
against, and from there into a prediction of some kind.
   The four measurements below use the six small-molecule regression datasets of


                                                 73
Table 5.12 (Chapter 5) and HLM clearance. Binary representations were compared
by Tanimoto distance, and all others by Manhattan distance on standardized features
(Section 4.2), with constant features removed.


Activity cliffs

Chapter 2 gives the MoleculeACE definition of an activity cliff, which combines three
similarity measures. Here we use one. Two molecules are similar if their ECFP4
Tanimoto similarity is at least 0.55 [148], and a molecule is a cliff if a similar molecule
has a label that differs from its own by more than a set gap. We chose the gap on each
dataset so that 15% of the molecules are cliffs, so the gap follows the spread of the
labels (Table 4.2). On hERG Ki the gap is 1.76 pKi , and 211 of the 1,412 molecules
are cliffs. On QM9 only 1,093 pairs of the 9,978 molecules are similar, and the gap is
0.05 eV, 0.06 times the median absolute deviation of the labels. Because the gap is set
by a share of molecules, on QM9 a cliff marks a small label difference, not a cliff in
the usual sense. On the three Biogen datasets (RLM clearance, MDR1–MDCK efflux
and HLM clearance) it is 0.20 to 0.34 log units, 0.50 to 0.60 times the median absolute
deviation.
   The count of cliffs depends on the representation. We kept each dataset’s gap and
replaced ECFP4 with each other base representation, calling similar the same number
of closest pairs as ECFP4 does. On hERG Ki the number of cliff molecules then ranges
from 138 (atom pairs) to 267 (WL graph kernel). Of the pairs that ECFP4 calls similar,
only 57% to 74% are also among another representation’s closest pairs, and on QM9
only 1% to 24% (Section S.3 of the Additional files).


Distance and label difference

We asked whether distance tracks label difference in two ways. Over all pairs of
molecules, we took the Spearman correlation between the distance of a pair and the
absolute difference of its labels, a one-number summary of the neighborhood-behavior
plot of Patterson et al. [149]; datasets above 2,000 molecules were sampled down to
2,000. Over each molecule’s closest pairs only, we predicted each label as the mean
label of the molecule’s five nearest neighbors, taken from other scaffold groups, and
took the Spearman correlation between predicted and measured labels.
   Across the 70 combinations of dataset and representation, the all-pairs correlation
ranged from −0.04 to 0.23 and was below 0.10 in 44 of them. The five-neighbor
prediction, a correlation between predicted and measured labels, ranged from 0.29 to


                                            74
0.89 (Table 4.2; every representation is in Section S.3 of the Additional files). On hERG
Ki with ECFP4, the all-pairs correlation is 0.06, while the five-neighbor prediction
reaches 0.72, with an RMSE of 0.63 pKi against a label standard deviation of 0.92
pKi .
   Figure 4.3 shows why the two differ. Of the 996,166 hERG Ki pairs, 93% have an
ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity below 0.20, and only 1.1% reach 0.55. Over all pairs the
median label difference is 0.81 pKi , but above a similarity of about 0.4 it is 0.20 to 0.30
pKi , although 8.3% of the 774 pairs at 0.8 or above still differ by more than 1 pKi .
Mordred shows the same pattern: the closest 5% of pairs have a median difference of
0.44 pKi , and every other 5% has 0.78 to 1.11 pKi . The useful information sits in
the few closest pairs. The all-pairs correlation is set by the other 95%, whereas the
five-neighbor prediction uses only the closest pairs.


Agreement on neighbors

We compared the five nearest neighbors of each molecule, from other scaffold groups,
under ECFP4, SMI-TED and Mordred (PDV on Caco-2, where Mordred is not a base
representation; on HLM clearance ECFP4 is not a base representation and its stored
features were used). On hERG Ki any two of the three share on average 0.47 to 0.52
of each molecule’s five neighbors, and on the three Biogen datasets and QM9 only 0.13
to 0.21 (Table 4.3).


Agreement with shared structure

For 500 random pairs per dataset we found the maximum common substructure (MCS)
with RDKit [32], allowing 2 seconds per pair and leaving out pairs that ran out of time.
The MCS distance is 1 − n/(a + b − n), where n is the number of atoms in the common
substructure and a and b are the numbers of heavy atoms in the two molecules. Its
Spearman correlation with ECFP4 Tanimoto distance ranged from 0.20 (hERG Ki and
HLM clearance) to 0.51 (Caco-2 and LogD).
   Every method in Section 4.3.3 that takes a similarity as input inherits the dilution
described here.


4.3.3     Consequences for similarity-based methods
We have identified some issues within the representations and similarities computed
over them. While this calls into question the predictive limits of QSAR and other

structure-based modeling, it also calls into question downstream applications of molec-
ular similarity. Nearest-neighbor methods, where a molecule’s neighbors, as measured
by similarity, are the inputs to several other processes, are by definition sensitive to the
quality of similarity measurements. They are used within other processes like predict-
ing a molecule’s activity using its neighbors’ labels, detecting outliers, either ACs or
noise, defining an applicability domain by distance to the training set [150], grouping
molecules into clusters for data splits [151], identifying activity cliffs, and, in federated

learning, weighting each site’s vote on a molecule by that molecule’s distance to the
site’s own data [152]. If this measure of similarity, or equivalently distance, is flawed,
then all of these downstream applications will feel the effects of it.
   “A small sweep of published noise-handling methods” reports a sweep in which
the 20 configurations with the lowest retention under noise all relied on a pairwise
distance measurement somewhere in their process. “Noise detection by neighbors”
focuses directly on neighbor-based noise detection (Table 2.3), the most clear-cut use
of similarity.


A small sweep of published noise-handling methods

In Chapter 3, model family explained the largest share of variance in noise robust-
ness, and representation at most 8.1% on QM9 (Table 3.1). Earlier in this project
we therefore ran a small sweep of published noise-handling methods, to ask whether
training methods built for noisy labels improve robustness and whether representation
still matters under them.
   The three groups of methods for noisy labels and the five categories of Song et al.
[51] are described in Section 2.5.2, and such methods have been studied across ma-
chine learning, including QSAR. Most methods in the LNL literature were built for
image classification with flipped labels [51], so they do not transfer automatically to
continuous labels.
   The study tested 12 published noise-handling methods on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO
gap (10,000 molecules, scaffold splits), adding Gaussian noise with σ from 0 to 1.0 in


                                            77
steps of 0.1 to the training labels only. The noise was added to the raw gap in eV
before the labels were standardized. Its σ is therefore in eV, not the noise level of
Section 2.5.1. The representations were ECFP4, Sort & Slice [7], PDV, canonical
SMILES and randomized SMILES. Depending on the method, a configuration could
also add the model’s own uncertainty or a distance between molecules (Tanimoto,
Euclidean, Manhattan, cosine or Mahalanobis). Others added an alternative GP kernel
or loss function, for 221 configurations in all. Four methods detect suspected molecules,
remove them and retrain (noise mitigation): small-loss selection, confident learning,
distance-based selection and multistage cleaning. The other eight change the training
(noise-robust training). Table 4.4 lists the 12 methods and how each uses a distance
between molecules.
   We measured noise robustness by retention, R2 under noise divided by R2 with
clean labels, as a percentage: the retention ratio of Chapter 3 (Equation 3.2). The
study predates AUCnorm , the area under the retention curve. Retention correlates
more closely with clean R2 , so it separates robustness from accuracy less well, but it
is a good enough proxy for comparing methods with and without a distance. In an
analysis of variance over the adapted components, using a distance explained the most
variance in R2 . All 20 configurations with the lowest retention used a distance, and
none of these 20 reached both a clean R2 above 0.6 and a retention above 80%.
   The methods that held up estimate noise from the model’s own loss or uncertainty.
DivideMix [153] retained the most, 86.7% (standard deviation 0.4% across its config-
urations), followed by small-loss selection [154] at 84.2% (2.1%) and the uncertainty
curriculum, which adds molecules in order of the model’s own predicted uncertainty
[155], at 82.7% (3.5%). The heteroscedastic GP, a GP whose noise is predicted per
molecule by a second model [156], retained 79.0% at σ = 0.6, averaged over 15 con-
figurations, with R2 of 0.71 on clean labels and 0.56 at that level. The exploratory
study does not record the noise level for the other methods, so this value cannot be
ranked against theirs. Regularization alone retained less, 73.0% for Mixup and 74.0%
for sharpness-aware minimization. Under these methods, the choice of representation
explained even less variance than in Chapter 3.
   The study added Gaussian noise to every training label of a single dataset and
property, the QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap. It also added a distance to methods designed
without one, so poor adaptation is an alternative explanation for their low retention.
Chapter 7 runs a new implementation of the same 12 methods on more datasets, on
hybrids and under the other noise conditions of Section 2.5.1 (Section 7.3.8).
   The distances may have failed for two reasons, which the evidence here does not

                                            79
separate. First, a single representation may lack the information a distance needs, with
and possibly even without noise. PDVs encode global molecular properties and ECFP4
contains substructure information, and any one of these sources alone may not be suf-
ficient for robust similarity estimation. Second, some representations are sparse, with
many features irrelevant to the molecules of a given dataset (Section 4.3.2). We sought
to address the second with the adaptive hybrid representation (Chapter 5), which con-
catenates the top-ranked features by importance from several base representations.


Noise detection by neighbors

Neighbor-based noise detection treats a label far from its neighbors’ labels as probably
wrong, an idea that goes back to editing nearest-neighbor training sets [102]. The wider
literature on filtering noisy labels concerns classification [157, 158], but Martín et al.
[159] adapt fourteen classification noise filters to regression, seven of them nearest-
neighbor filters that use the neighbors’ mean label. They find that the filters improve
regression methods on 20 general regression benchmarks with injected label noise,
none of them QSAR datasets. In QSAR, outlier removal with nearest neighbors has
targeted molecules unlike the rest of the dataset [160], not labels that disagree with their
neighbors’ labels. The neighbor-based noise scores, sample removal, sample weighting,
label smoothing and the recovery rate are defined in Chapter 7 (Sections 7.2.2 and 7.2.3,
Equation 7.6).
   The method needs a distance that picks the right neighbors, and neighbors with
clean labels. Earlier sections of this chapter argue that the first condition fails, and
the second fails whenever noise is spread over the whole training set. A large gap from
the neighbors’ labels can mean a noisy label, a noisy neighbor or the wrong neighbors,
and the score cannot tell which. Activity cliffs also look like noise to such a detector
(Chapter 2, “Molecular similarity and activity cliffs”).
   The exploratory study also used neighbors to clean labels, on 5,000 randomly chosen
QM9 molecules (HOMO–LUMO gap, scaffold split) with Gaussian noise on the training
labels at σ = 0.3 and σ = 0.6. Each cleaning method was judged by retraining a random
forest on ECFP4 with clean, noisy and cleaned labels, giving the recovery rate, the share
of the R2 lost to noise that cleaning recovers (Equation 7.6). Neighbor-based scores
compared each label with those of the molecule’s k nearest training molecules under
Euclidean, Manhattan or cosine distance. Table 4.5 holds only the configurations built
on a single representation. This study is separate from the sample-removal study of
Chapter 7.
   With the neighbor-based scores used as sample weights at strength α = 0.1, mean

                                            80
recovery was negative for every representation at both noise levels (Table 4.5). Of all
860 cleaning configurations, 8 had positive recovery at both noise levels; 6 of these
used a single representation, and 4 of those 6 were neighbor-based. Label smoothing
toward neighbor or model estimates gave positive recovery in 5.0% of configurations
at σ = 0.3 and 0.8% at σ = 0.6. Mean recovery was negative at every weighting and
smoothing strength tried, from 0.1 to 0.7.

                           Mean recovery (%)      Positive (% of configurations)
         Representation σ = 0.3       σ = 0.6     σ = 0.3         σ = 0.6
         ECFP4              −3.0       −7.3        33.3             16.7
         PDV                −2.4       −3.4        50.0             27.8
         MHG-GNN            −0.1       −7.2        38.9             5.6

Table 4.5: Recovery rate in R2 on the QM9 HOMO–LUMO gap when neighbor-based
noise scores are used as sample weights, for each representation used to find the neigh-
bors, at two levels σ of Gaussian label noise. Recovery is the share of the R2 lost to
noise that cleaning regains, so negative values mean cleaning made the model worse.
Positive is the share of configurations with recovery above zero.

   Judging a label by its neighbors usually made the model worse and rarely helped
at both noise levels, but it did not always fail. The best configuration at σ = 0.6 was
neighbor-based (PDV, Euclidean distance, local variance ratio score) and recovered
+17.2%. Because noise was added to every training label, the neighbors’ labels are
noisy too; the exploratory study gave this as the reason label smoothing failed.
   Uncertainty-based methods, which use no neighbors, showed potential at lower noise
but degraded at higher noise. Sample weighting did better than label smoothing but
usually still hurt. Over all sample-weighting configurations, not only those in Table 4.5,
recovery was positive in 14.2% of configurations at σ = 0.3 and 4.7% at σ = 0.6. The
mean recoveries were −22.9% and −20.9%.


4.4     Conclusions
On a single dataset, often very little of a standard representation varies. On hERG Ki ,
1,815 of the 2,048 ECFP4 features take one value in at least 95% of the 1,412 molecules
(Section 4.3.1). In addition, several representations give different molecules, mostly
stereoisomers, the same feature vector, and no model can separate those molecules,
whatever their labels. ECFP4 gives 56 of the 1,412 hERG Ki molecules a vector
shared with another molecule, all of them stereoisomers (“Molecules with identical
feature vectors”).

                                             81
   A distance sums over every feature, so when only a few features matter for the prop-
erty, the rest dominate the sum (Section 4.3.2). Across 70 combinations of dataset and
representation, the Spearman correlation between pair distance and label difference
was below 0.10 in 44. Predicting each label from its five nearest neighbors gave Spear-
man correlations of 0.29 to 0.89 with the measured labels, so the useful information
sits in a few close pairs rather than in the distance as a whole.
   In the small sweep of published noise-handling methods on QM9, the configurations
with the lowest retention all used a distance, and judging a label by its neighbors’ labels
usually made the model worse (Section 4.3.3). The sweep added a distance to some
methods designed without one, so poor adaptation may explain part of this.
   A representation that avoids these flaws would need five properties (Table 4.6). Its
features would be chosen for the task, using only each fold’s training labels. It would
be compact, so that irrelevant features do not dominate a distance, with the number
kept searched rather than fixed. It would draw on several representations, since each
misses information the others capture; plain concatenation does this but is not compact
(Chapter 5, “Combining representations”). It would work both as a model input and
as a space for similarity. Finally, it would be traceable: each chosen feature leads back
to its representation and can be read chemically wherever the base feature can.
   Feature selection cannot fix every flaw. Molecules identical in every base repre-
sentation stay identical in the hybrid, and a selected hashed bit still merges every
substructure folded into it. Selection also needs enough labeled molecules to estimate
which features matter.
   Chapter 5 builds the adaptive hybrid representation and asks how to select its
features for a given prediction task (Section 5.2.2).




                                            82
 Flaw (where shown)               Requirement                 Where handled

Chapter 5

Building adaptive hybrid
representations

5.1     Introduction
So far, Chapter 4 explained how the standard methods of estimating molecular sim-
ilarity, or conversely distance are flawed and potentially break the SPP. Specifically,
the representations used in these similarity calculations contain irrelevant features that
either do not vary or more generally do not pertain to the prediction task at hand. This
chapter explores a solution. With the understanding that each representation contains
a set of features which contain a certain type of information about that data, we aim
to select the most informative features across a larger set of representations, while fil-
tering out the irrelevant ones. We create an adaptive hybrid representation, a set of
features bounded by a budget that is selected from several base representations by a
search using one dataset and downstream model. The goal is to select a set of features
that is customized for a given model and prediction task. As discussed in Chapter 3,
performance can vary significantly across datasets and models. Rather than hope the
features you give a model will be useful, this process lets you prescreen them.
   In this chapter we build the adaptive hybrid representation, referred to as the hybrid
representation, first reporting on when and why it helps. The questions this chapter
and Chapter 6 address are set out in Chapter 1. This chapter measures predictive
performance on the labels as published, apart from one separate-site experiment with
flipped labels (Additional file 33). Distance measures between hybrids are covered in
Chapter 6, and noise detection and learning with noisy labels in Chapter 7.




                                           84
5.1.1    Combining representations
Not every molecular representation is equal. In fact, they hold very different types
of information. RDKit2D descriptors matched or outperformed features derived from
GNN embeddings and pretrained language models on many datasets across 62,820 mod-
els on a variety of molecular property prediction benchmarks [108]. Descriptor-based
models held 73% of top-three positions on 11 benchmarks [161]. In 1,310 ChEMBL
assays, feedforward networks on ECFP fingerprints outperformed graph convolutions
and recurrent networks trained on SMILES [162]. We know that the choice of repre-
sentation matters, as is discussed in Chapter 3, yet the combinations of representation
and model are hard to explore by hand [163]. Beyond just molecules, general protein
language models predict antibody expression better than antibody-specific ones, while
the antibody-specific models are better on binding [164], and no pathology foundation
model is best on every clinical task [165]. We turn our attention to combining features
from these different representations.
   Most research in this field relies on a single representation, and few combine features
from different representations beyond concatenation. One of the reasons just pooling
features from different representations together doesn’t work is dimensionality. If you
add a standard ECFP4 (d = 2, 048) with Mordred descriptors (d = 1, 613) and an
MHG-GNN embedding (d = 1, 024), you end up with 4,685 features, which is far too
many considering many datasets from experimental assays contain only a few hundred
labeled samples. With such ratios, more features mean weaker predictions [166, 167].
Therefore, we consider ways of combining representations.
   The most straightforward method of combining representations is concatenation.
This however degrades with non-homogeneous feature sets and obscures which rep-
resentation is responsible for the score [168]. Deng et al. [108] found that appending
RDKit2D descriptors to GROVER lowered RMSE on only 9 of 24 datasets tested. Late
fusion trains one model per source and aggregates the predictions [169]. MOGONET
is of this kind, combining per-source predictions in label space [170]. While this is
effective, it loses interactions between the source representations, an issue which is
addressed by intermediate fusion, for example cross-attention between sources [171].
Multiple kernel learning does this with kernels rather than whole models [172, 173],
and produces no interpretable feature sets. Laufkötter et al. [174] found that combin-
ing bioactivity-based fingerprints with structure-based ones like ECFP4 improved both
predictive performance and scaffold-hopping. Similarly, Turkina et al. [175] kept, from
each of six non-hashed fingerprints, the bits that carried most of a random forest’s


                                           85
importance on it, and joined them. The joined bits consistently improved predictive
accuracy over the original fingerprints across six datasets. Most of this work involves
limited combinations, adding together standard fingerprints and linear representations
[23, 174]. Although the features derived from embeddings are not as conceptually
straightforward, they contain another axis of information.
   When you want to filter a feature set for the features most important to the pre-
diction task, you can use a dimensionality reduction technique or feature selection
methodology. Principal component analysis (PCA) uses linear combinations of the
original features, ordered by the variance they explain [34]. Their combined features
are dependent on the original feature units unless each feature is z-scored first which
matters when data from different modalities like fingerprint bits, descriptors and em-
beddings are joined [34]. PCA never sees the labels, and components with small vari-
ance can still be important in prediction [176]. Partial least squares (PLS), the super-
vised counterpart in chemometrics, builds components that covary with the property
[177]. Neither gives components that can be traced to one substructure, descriptor or
representation, making them difficult to interpret.
   Feature selection keeps a subset of the original features, unlike PCA or PLS which
builds new ones. Of its three families [33], filter methods score features against the
labels before any model is fitted, as minimum-redundancy maximum-relevance selec-
tion (mRMR) ranks features by their mutual information with the label minus their
redundancy with already-chosen features [178]. Wrapper methods score subsets of fea-
tures by fitting the model [179]. Embedded methods select features while the model
is fitted, as the lasso does [180]. Recursive feature elimination repeatedly fits a model
and drops the lowest-ranked features [181].
   When selecting features, the question is not only what features are most important,
but also, what features does the model prefer? One of the ways to answer this is to
consider how many features relative to the size of a dataset a given model works best
with. Distance-based models such as k-nearest neighbors (kNN) and SVM use every
feature in every distance computation (Chapter 4), whereas tree ensembles choose one
feature per split and can essentially ignore irrelevant ones. Dablander et al. [7], who
created a substructure-based fingerprint called Sort & Slice, suggest that using labels
to select features may result in overfitting. Table 5.1 compares these alternatives with
the adaptive hybrid methodology developed in this thesis.




                                           86
   Method                   Uses the      Keeps        Several repre- Retrain to add

   Late fusion and           only if     yes           no            no, one more
   ensembling [169, 182]     pooling                                 model
