whether each method uses the labels, keeps the original features, lets one model see
several representations, and must refit to add a representation.


5.1.2      The adaptive hybrid representation
The task of selecting the best features from a set of representations and combining
them is not simple. How do you measure what features are “best”? Are these features
“best” relative to the representation they came from or to the whole pool of features?
Should you select more features from representations that seem to have more signal
to begin with, or take a set number of features from every representation? How many
base representations should you select features from? How many features should you
select? When scoring potential combinations of features, what type of evaluator should
you use?
   In a dream world, there would be a single answer to each one of these questions
that applies to every dataset. Unfortunately we live in reality, and as we discovered,
the answer to every single one of these questions is “depends on the data.” The task
of selecting the best features then became a search space optimization challenge. The
question actually became for a given prediction task, defined by the dataset, prediction
target, and model, what is the best methodology for selecting features? The hybrid
search does not output just a list of features, rather it outputs an ideal search con-
figuration to find features for your data. It tells you how to score features (feature


                                          87
importance score), what rules you should use to select them (allocation method), how
to score potential hybrid feature combinations (internal evaluator). In this research,
we run this hybrid search multiple times across different maximum number of features
(budgets), number of input representations, and models (downstream evaluators) on
each dataset. While it is not formal “training” in the ML sense, the hybrid search is es-
sentially a feature selection method that is fit on the training data. The representations
used in this thesis are listed in Tables 5.9, 5.10 and 5.11.
   Formally, an adaptive hybrid representation is a set of features, capped at a fixed
budget, selected from several base representations of the same molecules. The output
of a hybrid search is itself a feature selection method that when run on the given
dataset, produces the hybrid representation. This search is run on a single dataset and
downstream model, and the resulting hybrid differs by model, dataset, and even split.
Its intended use case is when there are multiple base representations that describe
different aspects of a molecule, and you want to combine these different features for a
single training task. The hybrid ranks features by a fitted model’s importance scores,
as embedded methods do, and keeps a fixed number of the top-ranked ones, as a
filter does. Classical methods treat all features as one flat list. The nearest classical
relative, the group lasso, keeps or drops predefined groups of variables whole [183],
whereas the hybrid splits the budget among representations (Section 5.2.2). Because
only original features are kept, each traces back to one representation, which makes
blueprints possible.


5.1.3     Blueprints and their chemical interpretation
As explained above, the adaptive hybrid representation relies on a search. A completed
search is saved as a blueprint, which records the settings needed to select the features
that make the hybrid representation. Because each selected feature traces back to its
original base representation and relative importance rank, the search can tell you about
your own prediction task. The best configuration is saved for each downstream model
and budget, giving one blueprint per pair, allowing us to compare blueprints and see
if different models prefer different features.
   Depending on the origin of the feature, some are chemically interpretable. By defi-
nition, the PDV contains values that relate back to chemical properties like molecular
weight or logP. An ECFP4 bit can be shown as the atom environment that sets it, and
a MACCS key stands for a known substructure. Beyond small molecules, the readable
unit of an antibody is a sequence position and its residue. This feature-level explain-


                                            88
ability fails when features are derived from learned embeddings. That will only tell
you if the learned embedding itself is useful as a representation, but nothing about the
selected feature.
   While a set of thousands of features may seem overwhelming and irrelevant, iden-
tifying substructures or descriptors that recur among the selected features can point
to parts of the molecule that may be involved in binding or activity. If the selected
features make sense chemically for that endpoint, it’s a good sign that the selection
mechanism is finding real signal instead of fitting noise. Conversely, if the hybrid
search is done over a range of budgets or models, and the blueprints are showing a
vastly different set of features, that may be an indication that the features at hand are
struggling to provide information about the prediction task. A predictor that scores
proposed molecules in generative design [184] could also take a hybrid as input, which
we leave to future work.


5.1.4    Applications beyond small molecules
The hybrid search is a generalized search that takes in multiple feature matrices from
the same samples and a supervised prediction task, and outputs a feature selection
method that produces a filtered set of features from those original vectors. The default
search objective scores prediction only, meaning the methodology applies to tabular
tasks or cases where you have different measurements of the same inputs. Although
this was developed with small molecules and QSAR modeling in mind, it is by no
means limited to that use case. While there are some functionalities in this research
that are tailored to chemical tasks, such as blueprints mapping features back to their
chemical interpretations and the use of nearest-neighbor objectives (Chapter 6) to aid
in the computation of molecular similarity, the core functionality of the hybrid process
is domain-independent.
   One caveat concerns sparse representations such as one-hot encoded sequences,
where each feature is set in only a few samples and so scores low importance on its
own. Here, the one-hot encoding is a base representation on three antibody datasets:
nanobody thermostability, change in binding free energy (∆∆G) and CHO polyreac-
tivity. The blocks substitution matrix (BLOSUM) encoding is a base representation
on humanness.
   While this setup was initially intended for small molecules, we extend this research
to antibodies. Twelve antibody datasets are used in this chapter, from deep mutational
scans of one antibody to panels of distinct antibodies (Table 5.7). One Buchwald–


                                           89
Hartwig yield dataset tests the method on reactions, a separate domain.
   Another caveat of this setup is that the hybrid can help only on datasets with
enough samples (see Section 5.4) and with base representations whose features carry
importance. When all the representations score poorly, there are not enough useful
features to pull from. Additionally, if there is only a single base representation that
carries signal, there is nothing for the hybrid to improve upon, which is shown in
Section 5.3.1.


5.2        Methods
Figure 5.1 outlines the hybrid search.




Figure 5.1: Schematic overview of the hybrid search. The inputs (left) are split into five
cross-validation folds, and the single representations are scored as baselines. Each fold
is searched over importance methods, allocation methods, budgets, internal evaluators
and downstream models (center). The best hybrid for each downstream model is stored
as a blueprint (right).



5.2.1      From base representations to a hybrid
The input to the search is K frozen representation matrices X(k) ∈ Rn×dk , k = 1, . . . , K,
for the same n samples, whose widths dk range from tens to thousands of features, as
well as a label vector y, one group label per sample for cross-validation (for example, its
scaffold group), the evaluation metric, the task type, one or more downstream models
and a list of budgets. It returns a hybrid of at most B features before any augmentation
features added outside the budget (Section 5.2.3), where the budget B is a maximum
rather than a target, and a blueprint that records the settings necessary to rebuild the
hybrid representation on another set of samples, such as another subset of the same
dataset.
   When combining representations, we are careful to avoid data leakage. Because
the feature selection process trains on the features and their labels, pooling all the

                                            90
features prior to selection would leak validation-level information into the training,
which is a well-documented bias in feature selection [185, 186]. As such, we run each
hybrid search within a cross-validation fold. When a single representation is scored, we
impute missing values with training-fold medians. A representation wider than 5,000
features is scored on its own using only its top 500 features (200 on datasets under
1,000 samples). These are the features that a LightGBM model of 50 trees of depth
5, fitted on the training part of each fold, splits on most often. This affects only that
representation’s score, not the features the hybrid can select from it.
   The data are split at three levels within CV runs. Every split keeps each group
whole, a scaffold for small molecules, a sequence cluster for antibodies, and some pre-
defined grouping for datasets from other domains (Section 5.2.6). With fewer than five
groups, the inner and internal splits use as many folds as there are groups (Additional
file 19). Figure 5.2 shows the three levels as nested boxes.

     Whole dataset

        Outer test fold: used once, to score the finished hybrid


        Outer training part (the other outer folds)

          Inner test fold: scores each search setting


          Inner training part

                                                            20%
                        80%
                                                  representation scores,
                  importance scores
                                                 which set the budget split



Figure 5.2: The three levels of splitting, each inside the one before. Every split keeps
each group whole.

                                                                                    (k)
   For each representation k, the importance method gives every feature a score sj ≥
0, computed on the 80% part of the internal split (Figure 5.2). These scores rank
features only within their own base representation. The standalone score rk is the score
of the internal evaluator, trained on representation k in the 80% part and scored on the
20% part, in the dataset’s metric. These scores cannot be compared across metrics as
they stand: a lower RMSE is better and it has no fixed upper limit, whereas a Spearman
correlation is higher when better and lies between −1 and 1. Before the budget is
split, each rk is therefore converted to a score r̃k on which 0 means the representation

                                           91
predicts no better than a trivial predictor and 1 means it predicts perfectly. The trivial
predictor depends on the metric. For Spearman, Pearson, the Matthews correlation
coefficient (MCC) and R2 , r̃k = max(0, rk ). For AUROC and the concordance index,
r̃k = max(0, 2(rk − 0.5)). For RMSE, r̃k = max(0, 1 − RMSEk /RMSE0 ), where RMSE0
is the error of predicting the training mean, and mean absolute error is rescaled likewise
against the training median. For accuracy and weighted F1, r̃k = max(0, (rk − r0 )/(1 −
r0 )), where r0 is the score of always predicting the majority training class.
   An optional representation count N keeps the N representations with the highest
standalone scores on the fold and discards the rest for that iteration of the search. Each
remaining representation receives a weight wk = g(r̃k ), with g set by the allocation
method, and a share b∗k = B wk / l wl features, rounded by largest remainder and
                                  P

capped at its width. A representation left out by the representation count, or whose
standalone fit fails, receives no features. Thus k bk = min(B, k dk ) and bk ≤ dk ,
                                                P               P

and a representation with wk > 0 keeps at least one feature. The global pool does not
use these shares (see below).
                                                           (k)                         (k)
   Let Sk be the top bk features of representation k by sj , among those with sj > 0;
if none scores above 0, its full bk is taken. The cut exists because tree importance gives
exactly 0 to every feature no tree split on, and filling the budget with those would
                                                                     (1)         (K)
pick them by their position in the matrix. The hybrid is H = [X:,S1 , . . . , X:,SK ], with
   k |Sk | ≤ B, so it can be narrower than its budget.
P

   The hybrid search returns a list of settings for feature selection, referred to here as
a configuration. It is scored by fitting the downstream model on the hybrid in each fold
of the search and averaging the held-out scores. Each pair of downstream model and
budget has its own search, and thus its own winner. Because this score is chosen over
a range of selected configurations, it is optimistic [187, 188]. However, so is the score
of the highest-performing base representation it is compared with, which is also the
best of several. The whole search runs inside each outer training fold, and the winning
settings are rebuilt there and scored once on the untouched outer test fold, both for the
winner at each budget and for each downstream model’s best across budgets. The inner
search score minus the outer test score is reported against the budget in Section 5.3.1.
   Two variants of this search are used later: under label noise, training quantities use
noisy labels while held-out scoring uses clean labels (Chapter 7). Under the nearest-
neighbor objective, each sample’s label is predicted from its five nearest neighbors in
the feature space (Chapter 6).
   As explained above, a hybrid search returns a list of settings that function as a
feature selection method to generate the hybrid representation itself. These settings

                                            92
are recorded in a blueprint that can be used on new folds of the same dataset or even
new data. The blueprint contains additional information, including some fields for
analysis, meta-data, and scores. These scores are used only for comparative purposes
as in this research, the hybrids are rebuilt per-fold and are scored as such.
   Additional file 20 lists every field of a blueprint and its type.
   The blueprint does not record the specific features that are returned by the hybrid,
as it is meant to be re-run using the same settings on new, labeled data. Alternatively,
you can reuse the features of a labeled fit and apply them to data with no labels under
the assumption that the same features matter between sets.
   The blueprint is extended by an additional explainability layer. Since it does not
store feature names, this additional step runs on a completed hybrid, records where
each selected feature came from and, wherever possible, what that feature means. An
ECFP4 bit is named by the atom environment that sets it, written as a SMILES frag-
ment from a molecule with the bit set. MACCS keys are named by their SMILES
arbitrary target specification (SMARTS) pattern and a short name, and descriptor
features by descriptor name. Protein composition, descriptor and k-mer features, an-
tibody composition, one-hot, BLOSUM, structural and inverse-folding features and
PCA components are named too. MAP4 and DRFP features get only a bit index,
and Avalon, Mordred, atom-pair, graph-kernel and learned-embedding features only
the representation name and dimension index. The Mordred descriptor names in Fig-
ure 5.6 were added in a separate step.


5.2.2     The search space
To run a hybrid search, we first measure feature importance, select how many features
to take from each contributing base representation, and choose how to score a candidate
feature set. And for each of those steps, there’s no one right way to do it. Different
prediction tasks prefer different methods of scoring feature importance or candidate
features, different sets of input representations, and so forth. The search tries to find
the ideal settings for the hybrid feature selection process, tailored to a given dataset and
downstream model. All combinations of settings values are explored, as the settings
interact. The best budget depends on the downstream model, as shown below for
nanobody thermostability. This search grid also tests the chapter’s claim that no
single setting suits every dataset.
   Six settings in total are searched, namely the downstream model, the budget, the
representation count, the importance method, the allocation method and the internal


                                            93
evaluator. Table 5.5 gives the values each setting can take, and Table 5.6 the smaller
set searched on each dataset in this chapter (Section 5.2.4). The importance methods
tested are LightGBM gain, TreeSHAP or DeepLIFT (described below). Feature-level
importance depends on the model as well as on the data [189], and as such we ob-
serve that within the search, a different ranking of features produces a different hybrid
output.
   The downstream model is trained on the hybrid representation. This model can be
a supervised model of any form, classification or regression. We used random forest,
XGBoost, LightGBM, SVM, a deep neural network (DNN) and a Gaussian process.
Each dataset in this chapter uses two of them (Section 5.2.8).
   The budget is the maximum number of features in the hybrid, taken from eight
values (10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1,000 and 2,000 features). When the budget is too
small, informative features can be left out, yet when it is too large it will pick up on
too many uninformative ones, especially ones that give small datasets room to overfit.
   We select a set of up to 10 base representations per dataset, chosen prior to the
search (Table 5.12). The selections were made based on each representation’s relative
performance on the prediction task. We took into account what type of information
each representation contained, and tried to span a wide set across the selected 10.
For example, while there are many high-performing graph-based embeddings in QSAR
modeling, for the purposes of testing a feature selection process which takes features
from different sources of information, it is better to have a mix of graph-based embed-
dings, as well as other types of molecular representations like substructure fingerprints,
property vectors, sequential embeddings, etc. The representation count (2, 3, 5 or
all) sets how many may contribute features, keeping those with the highest standalone
scores on each fold. The number of representation counts to test is searched in the
sweep as datasets differ in how many representations have features worth selecting,
and pooled feature selection over all representations has no such setting. While we
tested various methods of pooling the features, we found computing feature impor-
tance scores and concatenation techniques on a global pooled feature set was less often
best in preliminary runs than when the feature scoring is kept on a per-representation
basis (Additional file 16). Since the features contain different types of information
at different scales, pooling the features too early makes comparisons within the pool
challenging. We found that it’s better to determine which features are important to
each representation first, and go from there.
   Every allocation method is paired with every count. In preliminary runs, some
datasets performed best with 2 or 3 representations and others with all 10. The full

                                           94
runs show the same split. On LogD, where counts of 2, 3, 5 and all were searched, the
hybrid used all 10 representations in nine of its ten searches (five folds for each of the
two downstream models). On QM9 with LightGBM, which searched 3 and all, it used
3 on every fold.


Feature importance scoring

We run a feature importance scoring method on each base representation, which gives
each feature a single positive score. These scores remain relative within a given repre-
sentation, so they are not compared or normalized between representations. Split-based
tree importance is biased toward features with many possible split points [190], which
matters here because we use binary fingerprint bits alongside continuous embeddings.
No single importance method works best across datasets and downstream models. Af-
ter testing a wider variety of methods found across literature, we settled on three
methods whose rankings did not correlate strongly with each other and whose selected
features performed well as model input (Additional file 16).
   We score features using LightGBM gain importance, SHapley Additive exPlana-
tions (SHAP) values for tree ensembles (TreeSHAP) and DeepLIFT (Deep Learning
Important FeaTures) [191]. LightGBM gain measures how much a feature reduced the
training loss where trees split on it [62]. TreeSHAP gives each feature’s exact Shap-
ley value for each molecule’s prediction from a tree model [192]. DeepLIFT measures
how much a feature moves the output of a small network, trained with Adam [193],
away from its output on an all-zero reference input, which after standardization places
every feature at its training mean [191, 194, 195]. On Caco-2, the median share of
features scoring above 0 was 30% for LightGBM gain, 70% for TreeSHAP and 90%
for DeepLIFT (preliminary, over 10 representations and 3 folds). Table 5.2 gives the
settings of each method, including the pre-screen of representations wider than 2,000
features. Each dataset searches one, two or all three importance methods depending
on its individually-decided search settings (Table 5.6).


Feature allocation methods

The allocation method decides how many of the B features each representation con-
tributes while the importance method decides which. Of the five methods searched,
four give each representation a fixed share computed from its rescaled standalone score
(linear, squared, softmax and rank-decay allocation). The fifth, the global pool, lets the
candidate features offered by every representation compete in one importance ranking.

Table 5.2: Settings of the importance methods, fitted per representation and fold.
Unlisted settings are library defaults.


   The four fixed-share methods compute budget shares from the standalone score on
internal validation. Only linear allocation makes the share proportional to that score.
All four assume that representations with stronger predictive performance contribute
more valuable features. This assumption fails when a strong representation relies on a
few select features. In that case, a large share of features will be given to that repre-
sentation, picking up on weaker features and potentially missing out on stronger ones
from other representations. Fixed shares ignore complementarity, so two representa-
tions carrying the same information both receive a share. Only the global pool ranks
features from different representations against each other, however ranking feature im-
portance on a global pool is dubious as the features are not necessarily appropriate to
compare.
   The first method of feature allocation we tried was a greedy forward selection, in
which the hybrid is built one block of features at a time [33, 179]. In each round,
the next block is added from representations in order of which improves the internal
validation score. This in theory lets it choose features for how well they work together.
In practice, this is very expensive. With blocks of 10 features and a budget of 500 and
10 representations, it needs up to 500 model fits per configuration and fold against
10 for a score-based method. It was also less accurate: in preliminary runs on Jain
antibody neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) retention it placed 16th of 17 allocation methods,
with a Spearman correlation of 0.360 against 0.453 for linear allocation. We therefore


                                              96
removed it from the search (Additional file 16).
   The fixed-share methods differ only in how wk is computed from r̃k (Table 5.3;
worked example in Table 5.4), with settings fixed for every dataset. Because every
rescaled score lies between 0 and 1, softmax cannot give one representation most of
the budget: with 10 representations the best takes at most 23.2%. Rank decay uses
only the order: with 10 representations the best receives 27.3% and the tenth 1.8%,
whatever the gaps between their scores.

Table 5.4: Worked example of the four fixed-share allocation methods for a budget
of 100 features and three representations. Each entry is the number of features a
representation receives, with shares rounded by largest remainder.

   The global pool sizes each representation’s offer with squared weights r̃k2 , as shares
of a pool of 2B candidate features. Each offer is capped at the representation’s width
and holds only features scoring above 0. The same importance method is fitted once
on the joined offers, and the hybrid keeps the top B features that score above 0. Each
representation’s count is therefore an outcome of the joint ranking, with no minimum
of one feature, and a feature’s rank depends on what the other representations offered.

                                                       97
Search configuration and internal evaluation

The most useful features for an RF may not be the most useful for an SVM, and vice
versa. The relative importance of a feature is dependent on the model [189]. Within
the search grid, we use an internal evaluator to fit on one candidate feature set at
a time. This process is separate from the final downstream model evaluation. In a
standard search, four internal evaluators are used: RF, DNN, LightGBM, and the
matched evaluator. When the internal evaluator is set to match, the search scores each
candidate hybrid on the same model type as the downstream model, for each of the
twelve model types the code supports. So internal evaluators outside the standard
sweep may be tested. For a downstream model outside of the pre-defined set, if no
matched evaluator exists in the code, the search uses the other internal evaluators that
were requested. Each internal evaluator is fitted on the internal training fold and scores
on the internal validation fold within that CV iteration using the dataset’s specified
evaluation metric.
   In the full grid, a random forest, LightGBM or DNN downstream model is searched
with three internal evaluators, because its matched evaluator is already one of the
other three. An XGBoost, SVM or Gaussian process downstream model is searched
with four, because the matched evaluator adds one of its own family. Five datasets
searched fewer (Table 5.6). EGFR binding has no random forest evaluator, expression
titer and Buchwald–Hartwig yield have two evaluators, and AC-SINS self-association
has no Gaussian-process evaluator. SMAC colloidal stability scores the SVM’s hybrids
without the DNN evaluator. Only the DNN evaluator shares its downstream model’s
architecture and training settings. Even it draws its 20% early-stopping samples at ran-
dom, whereas the downstream DNN splits them by the outer split’s groups. Additional
file 13 gives every evaluator setting.
   The internal evaluator’s scores determine how the budget is split and which repre-
sentations are kept. The internal evaluator ranks candidate feature sets, it explicitly
does not rank features within a representation. This process of selecting a feature set
by fitting a model on it, and keeping the set that scores the best is known as the wrap-
per method [179]. This is distinct from a filter method which scores features without
fitting a model.
   The search tests every combination of the six settings on a dataset under 5-fold
cross-validation. Over every value of Table 5.5, this is 10,080 configurations with all
six downstream models, or 8,160 without the Gaussian process (Additional file 15).
With each dataset’s own values, the searches reported in this chapter scored 108 to


                                           98
3,360 configurations per outer fold (Table 5.6). Each dataset’s grid is a small cut of the
full search, made to save compute (Section 5.2.4); at worst, it finds a less informative
hybrid than the full search would have.
   Before the full runs, we ran preliminary design experiments on 1 to 9 datasets to
choose the values each setting may take (Additional file 16). A value was kept if it gave
the best score for some dataset, downstream model and budget. A setting was fixed at
one value when the spread between its values was smaller than the spread of one value
across folds. On hERG Ki , for example, five ways of rescaling each representation’s
score differed by less than one of them varied across folds. Hence the rescaling is one
fixed rule per metric.


5.2.3    Augmentation
Some feature sets are small, such as log-likelihood ratios (3 features) and the 3D shape
and pharmacophore features (12 to 60 features). Others, such as mutation features
that record differences from a parent antibody, may carry more meaning beside a full
representation. On some datasets we therefore add them as augmentation, calling the
finished hybrid H the base and the feature sets that supply extra features the donors.
Donors are set per dataset (Augmentation column of Table 5.12):

   • small molecules: MACCS keys, 3D pharmacophore features, shape features (USR,
     USRCAT), the Coulomb matrix, topological torsions and, on hERG blocking,
     MolCLR;

   • antibodies: mutation features, log-likelihood ratios, amino-acid composition, and
     the paratope and structural features;

   • Buchwald–Hartwig: MACCS keys and 3D pharmacophore features of the reac-
     tants and of the product.

Uncapped augmentation adds the extra features outside the budget, and capped aug-
mentation puts them in place of the weakest base features. An earlier search of each
whole dataset covered 16 datasets of an earlier list. On none did either augmented
condition beat no augmentation by more than 1% (3% on datasets under about 1,000
samples). We therefore ran the searches in this chapter without augmentation on all 20
datasets (Section 5.2.4). Only the nearest-neighbor search on SMAC colloidal stability
and HIC retention keeps uncapped augmentation (Chapter 6). Additional file 21 gives
how donor features are ranked and added, and the three conditions.


                                           99
5.2.4       Search grid per dataset
Searching every value of Table 5.5 in every fold of every dataset was too slow, so
each dataset searches a smaller grid (Table 5.6). From an earlier search of each whole
dataset, we kept the two downstream models with the best scores. Likewise, we kept
the budgets whose ten best configurations scored within 1% of the best budget (3% on
datasets under about 1,000 samples), widened to at least three consecutive budgets.
Importance methods, allocation methods, internal evaluators and representation counts
that scored well below the best were dropped.
   Two datasets, QM9 and hERG blocking, were too slow for the earlier search over ev-
ery value. They were searched over a shorter fixed list, called the fast tier (LightGBM
gain importance; linear, squared and rank-decay allocation; three or all representa-
tions), and their grid stays within it. The search with the nearest-neighbor objective
has its own grid, given in Chapter 6.

   Additional file 22 follows hERG Ki through these cuts, from the 8,220 configurations
of its earlier search, which included kNN among six downstream models, to 224, and
its final grid still contains the best configuration.


5.2.5       Evaluation protocol
Every experiment splits each dataset into outer folds as set out in Section 5.2.6. The
search sees only the training samples of each outer fold, and the selected features are
rebuilt on them and applied unchanged to the held-out samples. Only three experi-
ments limit each held-out feature to the range it takes on the training samples: the
comparison with the best single representation, the frequent and rare scaffolds, and

the comparison of many and few representations.
   Because of time and computational constraints, not every experiment in Chapters 5
to 7 ran on every fold or every dataset. Some experiments here ran on the first three
outer folds, most noise experiments in Chapter 7 ran on the first outer fold only, and
some experiments have no result on some datasets. We report the results that exist,
give the number of folds beside each one, and name the datasets where a result is
missing. We do not repeat this caveat at every result.
   In this chapter, the following results are missing. The comparison with ensembles
has no result on the anti-VEGF binding scan. The overfitting experiment has none on
rat microsomal clearance, MDR1–MDCK efflux, either anti-VEGF scan, HIC retention
or angiopoietin-2 binding. The search with one fold fewer, and the stability of the
selected features, have none on rat microsomal clearance or the anti-VEGF binding
scan. Feature reduction at the same width has none on rat microsomal clearance,
the anti-VEGF binding scan or HIC retention. The internal-evaluator experiment has
none on rat microsomal clearance or either anti-VEGF scan. The variance shares
have none on either anti-VEGF scan. Tuning the model has none on rat microsomal
clearance or the anti-VEGF binding scan. Adding a new embedding has none on
QM9, rat microsomal clearance, either anti-VEGF scan, nanobody thermostability,
AC-SINS self-association, HIC retention or angiopoietin-2 binding. The shuffling of
representations has none on the anti-VEGF expression scan.
   The hybrid is compared with the best single base representation at its full number
of features, which is what a user would otherwise run. This comparator is chosen per
downstream model and per fold from the search’s scores on the outer training samples,
and it can differ between folds. It is then refit on those samples and scored on the same
held-out samples as the hybrid. The hybrid is held to its budget, and the comparator
is consequently often many times wider. Both widths are recorded for every fold, since
a difference between the two scores is partly a difference in width. The main quantity
per fold is the hybrid’s score minus that of the best single representation, signed so
that a positive value means the hybrid scored better (for RMSE the sign is flipped).


5.2.6    Datasets and splitting
The hybrid experiments use 20 datasets (Table 5.7): seven of small molecules, 12 of
antibodies and one of reactions. Their curation is given in Section S.7 of the Additional
files and their representations in Table 5.12. Antibody datasets outnumber small-
molecule ones, but small molecules are the focus of this chapter, and results are read


                                          102
per dataset, not pooled across a domain.


Pre-processing

Section S.7 of the Additional files gives the shared and per-dataset pre-processing steps,
with the counts after each step. The LogD and Caco-2 labels are clipped at their 1st
and 99th percentiles, so on Caco-2, for example, 22 molecules share the highest label
and 23 the lowest.


Splitting rules

As in Chapter 3 (Section 3.2), no group is split between folds, and classification folds
are also stratified with a fixed seed. Every dataset is split into five outer folds, though
some experiments ran on fewer (Section 5.2.5), and no split is random (Section 2.7).
On a given dataset, every representation, model and hybrid is scored on the same folds.


Groups by domain

The small-molecule datasets, QM9 included, are grouped by Bemis–Murcko scaffold
(Section 3.2), here keeping stereochemistry. On QM9 the largest group is the empty
scaffold of acyclic molecules, with 1,041 of the 9,978 molecules. The two datasets from
Biogen [140] (rat microsomal clearance and MDR1–MDCK efflux) have 1.2 and 1.1
molecules per scaffold, respectively, so holding out a scaffold there is close to holding
out single molecules. Chapter 3 used a different scaffold key and different curation for
its versions of LogD, Caco-2, hERG Ki and QM9, and Section S.7.2 of the Additional
files lists the differences.
   The reaction dataset is Buchwald–Hartwig C–N couplings from high-throughput
plates. Following Ahneman et al. [207], it is grouped by additive: each held-out fold
holds four or five additives unseen in training (Section S.7 of the Additional files).
   In the Koenig deep mutational scan of the anti-vascular endothelial growth factor
(VEGF) antibody G6, each variant carries one mutation. Variants are grouped by their
mutated position, giving 225 groups. With all mutations at one position on the same
side of a split, a model cannot look up a per-position effect at test time [208]. The
nanobody and humanness sets are grouped by single-linkage clusters at 95% sequence
identity, the identity threshold Kenlay et al. [164] used to cluster their pretraining
sequences.
   The two AbAgym deep mutational scans [201] (EGFR and angiopoietin-2 binding)
are grouped by mutated position, as for the Koenig scans, giving 36 and 20 groups. The

∆∆G set joins antibody–antigen entries from SKEMPI 2.0 [203] and AB-Bind [204].
It is grouped by protein complex, giving 30 groups, and hence no complex is on both
sides of a split. The five Ginkgo developability assays are measured on the same panel
of antibodies. Two are retention times, in hydrophobic interaction chromatography
(HIC) and standup monolayer adsorption chromatography (SMAC). The others are
affinity-capture self-interaction nanoparticle spectroscopy (AC-SINS), expression titer,
and polyreactivity to Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cell proteins. We use the five
cross-validation folds released with the competition [206] as the groups, which makes
the outer folds the competition’s own.


Consequences of the grouping

Some groupings give very unequal folds: the Ginkgo competition folds hold 35 to 53
antibodies, and per-fold scores on these datasets spread more widely.


5.2.7     Representations by domain
Any representation that gives one fixed-length vector per sample can serve as a base
representation (Section 5.2.1). The first version of the method used three molecular rep-
resentations: PDV, ECFP4 and MHG-GNN. These three are now one small-molecule
example among many. Every base representation is frozen: it is computed once from
the raw input (a SMILES string, a sequence or a reaction) and is never trained on the
labels.
   To choose them, every candidate representation was first scored on its own with a


                                           105
gradient-boosted tree model [62] under the dataset’s grouped folds. Candidates were
then compared by the Spearman correlation between their out-of-fold predictions, since
two representations whose predictions correlate highly carry much the same signal. At
most ten were then chosen by hand, looking at each candidate’s own score next to
these correlations and favoring representations that encode the input in different ways.
Binding free energy has eight.
   Which representation is best depends on the downstream model as well as the
dataset. The best one is the same under both of a dataset’s downstream models on
three of the seven small-molecule datasets and five of the twelve antibody datasets (Ta-
ble 5.7). On hERG Ki , for example, MHG-GNN is best with the SVM and GROVER
with the DNN. Augmentation features do not count toward the ten, and no search in
this chapter uses them (Section 5.2.3).
   If an input cannot be parsed or embedded, it gets an all-zero row, so that every
representation of a dataset keeps the same rows. In computed descriptors, a missing or
infinite value is set to 0, and values are stored unscaled. A pretrained model gives one
vector per input, the mean of its last-layer token vectors with padding excluded, unless
the tables say otherwise. Scaling and filling of missing values happen inside each fold,
on the training rows only, as described in the construction of the hybrid.


Small molecules

The small-molecule representations (Table 5.9) are the fingerprint, descriptor, graph
and language-model families described in Chapter 2. The only additions are four
sets of 3D shape and pharmacophore features, used as augmentation only. All four
are computed from a single conformer per molecule, embedded with a fixed seed and
optimized with the Merck molecular force field. A single conformer may misdescribe
flexible molecules.


Chemical reactions

Reaction datasets (Table 5.10) use three kinds of representation. The condensed graph
of reaction (CGR) merges reactants and products into one graph in which the bonds
that change are marked [209, 210]. Here it is hashed into a circular fingerprint after
atom mapping [211]. Reaction language models read the reaction SMILES directly
[212, 213]. Any molecular representation can also be applied to the reactants, to the
product, or to their difference. A one-hot encoding of which aryl halide, ligand, base
and additive was used carries no chemistry. It is kept because on plate data such


                                          106
identity features can predict yield about as well as chemical descriptors [214].


Antibodies

Antibody representations (Table 5.11) start from the heavy- and light-chain amino-
acid sequences. Besides one-hot and BLOSUM62 encodings [215], most are frozen
language models, either trained on all proteins or on antibody repertoires only. Most
models embed each chain separately and join the two vectors, so their width doubles on
datasets with both chains. Several representations read one model in different ways, for
instance averaging over earlier layers or over the complementarity-determining region
(CDR) residues only, with CDRs found by IMGT numbering [216] using ANARCI
[217]. Datasets of point variants of one parent antibody also use features that describe
each mutation.

Table 5.9: Representations used for small molecules. In the Role column, B marks
a base representation, A an augmentation and B/A a representation that is a base
representation on some datasets and an augmentation on others.

5.2.8     Models and metrics
The downstream models (Chapter 2) are random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, an
SVM, a DNN and a Gaussian process [118]. Each dataset uses the two that scored
best in the earlier search of the whole dataset (Table 5.6). All six are used: the Gaus-
sian process is one of the two models on four small antibody datasets (angiopoietin-2
binding, HIC retention, AC-SINS self-association and CHO polyreactivity). Tree en-
sembles are the baseline to beat, because a hybrid is a tabular feature matrix and
tree-based models still lead on medium-sized tabular data of about 10,000 samples
[242].


                                             112
   By default, every model uses the same settings on every dataset, so that score
differences come from the input. An optional per-fold random search tries 20 draws of
a model’s settings on 3 group-aware inner folds of the outer training fold. In the search,
the DNN is not tuned this way and relies on early stopping instead. On one outer fold
of five datasets, tuning helped the SVM most and made the random forest worse on
Buchwald–Hartwig yield (Additional file 23). The Gaussian process and the SVM are
skipped above the dataset sizes in Table 5.5, because their fits no longer finish in useful
time. Other models left out on named datasets are listed in Additional file 14, and all
model settings are in Additional file 13.
   The kNN model is not a default downstream model, but a third downstream model
in the search with the nearest-neighbor objective (Chapter 6), on every dataset. It
takes k = 5 neighbors by default, lowered to the square root of the number of training
samples when a fold has fewer than 25.
   Each dataset is scored in the metric its field normally uses, so that it can be com-
pared with published work (Table 5.7). Regression on small molecules, QM9 included,
and on reaction yields uses RMSE in the label’s units. Antibody regression uses Spear-
man ρ, because these assays rank candidates and their scales differ between laborato-
ries. Binary classification uses AUROC, which does not depend on a decision threshold.
We do not average metrics across datasets.
   Tables give the mean over the 5 folds, and the text gives the number of folds on
which one side is ahead. We use no significance tests and no confidence intervals,
because 5 fold scores are correlated through shared training molecules. No unbiased
estimator of the variance of k-fold cross-validation exists that is valid under all distri-
butions, and estimates that ignore the overlap underestimate it [243].
   Two settings differ between runs. In later runs, the DNN clips each prediction to the
range of its training labels. Without the clip, one molecule with an extreme descriptor
value could make a fold’s error several orders of magnitude larger on rat microsomal
clearance. Most DNN results in this chapter come from runs without the clip, and the
experiments where such an error occurred have no rat microsomal clearance result. In
later runs, the Gaussian process also starts its kernel length scale at the square root of
the number of features rather than at 1. On inputs of over 1,000 features, a start of 1
left some fits predicting one value for every sample. The hybrids of the four Gaussian-
process datasets were chosen by searches that started at 1. We report all results as
computed and name the setting beside each result it affects.
   Summed over its 5 outer folds, the search of one dataset took 2.3 hours (hERG Ki )
to 28.2 hours (MDR1–MDCK efflux) on small molecules, and 11.1 hours on Buchwald–

                                            113
Hartwig yield. On antibodies it took 0.4 hours (CHO polyreactivity) to 38.6 hours (the
anti-VEGF expression scan). Each fold ran as its own job on a CPU node of the Oxford
ARC or HTC cluster, with 8 cores and 64 GB of memory by default. The times count
only each fold’s last attempt, so a fold resumed from a stopped run is undercounted.
One hERG Ki fold recorded only 90 seconds.


5.2.9     Comparators, controls and ablations
The hybrid is the best of many configurations; hence its gain may only reward a
large search. Each experiment below asks one question about this or a related issue.
Table 5.13 lists them, with the settings each varies and holds fixed.
    Unless stated otherwise, every experiment below uses the dataset’s grid (Table 5.6),
its two downstream models, five outer folds and the dataset’s own metric. From each
fold’s search it takes, for each downstream model, the two single representations and
the two hybrids with the best scores on the training samples.


Best single representation and PCA

As a second reference beside the best single representation, every base representation is
joined, standardized on the training samples and reduced by PCA [34] to each budget
of the dataset’s grid. The downstream model is fitted on the principal components as
they are. On each fold, the hybrid is compared with PCA at the hybrid’s own budget;
in this way, neither side’s width is chosen on the held-out samples. PCA cannot have
more components than training samples, and where the budget is larger the two widths
differ.


Ensembles

The ensemble fits one model per representation and pools their predictions (late fusion),
whereas the hybrid fits one model on one fused feature vector (early fusion) [168]. Every
choice for the ensemble is made on an inner 3-fold group split of the outer training
samples. The first ensemble uses the same downstream model on every representation,
and the second uses each representation’s own better model. Each picks its members,
the top 3 representations or all of them, and one of seven ways to pool predictions,
from the mean and median to trimmed, outlier-removed and score-weighted means.
The mean is the high-level fusion of Turkina et al. [175], which averages the predictions
of one random forest per fingerprint.

                                                                                    in

       beat the best single               representation,      search’s grid
       representation, and                PCA of all

Table 5.13: The experiments of this chapter. One row is one experiment. Datasets is
the number of the 20 datasets shown in the experiment’s table, and every experiment
uses five outer folds unless its section says otherwise.




                                                 115
Many or few representations

On each outer fold, the base representations are ranked by their summed feature im-
portance on the training samples. The top 1, 2 or 3 of them supply the B features
with the highest importance among their own features. These are compared with the
search’s own hybrid at B and with an even split of B over every base representation,
each using the importance method that the fold’s best hybrid used. The search’s hy-
brid differs from the hand-built hybrids in two ways at once. It ranks representations
by their standalone scores rather than by summed importance, and it stops at the last
feature with importance above zero.


Frequent and rare scaffolds

Datasets above 2,000 samples are first reduced to about 2,000 by drawing whole scaffold
families with a fixed seed. Scaffolds at or above the 80th percentile of group size form
the frequent half, and the rest form the rare half. Each half gets its own search on
each outer fold. The hybrid of a search on all the subset’s training samples is also
rebuilt and scored on each half. Three other splits were run as well (by heavy-atom
count, label spread within a scaffold and feature spread within a scaffold), which are
not reported here.


Overfitting with the budget

The budgets are the standard list (Table 5.5) plus 4,000 and 8,000 features. They also
include 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 1.5 and 2 times the smallest outer training set, which places
one budget where the number of features equals the number of samples. At every
budget, the winning configuration is rebuilt on the outer training samples and scored
on the held-out fold. The selection bias at a budget is the search’s own score minus
the held-out score; a positive value means the search promised more than the held-out
fold gave. We report how the bias changes with the budget, and the budget the search
picks on each fold from its training samples alone. We do not compare hybrids wider
than the training set against narrower ones by their best held-out budgets, because
that choice reads the held-out labels on both sides.


One fold fewer

A second check repeats each outer fold’s search with one more fold removed from its
training samples, for each of the other four folds. The held-out fold is the same and



                                          116
the two searches differ by one fold of training samples. Each search, however, picks its
own budget, so the two hybrids can differ in width as well.


Feature reduction at the same width

On regression datasets, every method receives B features from all base representations
joined, fitted on the fold’s training samples. The methods are the two best hybrids
at budget B from the fold’s search, mRMR [178], recursive feature elimination [181]
and B random features. mRMR ranks features by mutual information and stops at
200 features for run time. It consequently has no result on any small-molecule dataset,
where every budget is 250 features or more. Recursive elimination fits a 50-tree random
forest of depth 5 and removes 10% of the features at each step. Each of the dataset’s
two downstream models scores every method, and only the features differ.


Internal evaluator

One search per outer fold is run for each evaluator setting. The matched evaluator
belongs to the downstream model’s family, while a near-family evaluator pairs tree
models with each other, the SVM with a Gaussian process, and the DNN with a
narrower DNN. A far-family evaluator pairs tree models with an SVM, the SVM with
LightGBM, and the DNN with k-nearest neighbors. A fourth setting scores the DNN
with a wider DNN, and a fifth searches the dataset’s own internal evaluators (Table 5.6).
Each setting’s search picks its own budget, and the number of features can thus differ
between settings. On every dataset, the SVM’s near-family evaluator is a Gaussian
process whose length scale started at 1 (Section 5.2.8).


Importance during training

Importance is computed on the training samples by the method the search picked. Sam-
ple weighting is not run for the Gaussian process, which takes no per-sample weights,
nor on multiclass tasks. The five conditions are:

1. No weighting: the features as selected.

2. Feature weighting: standardized features multiplied by the square root of their
   importance, with the weights normalized to average 1.

3. Sample weighting: samples weighted by how well their label agrees with their 10
   nearest neighbors in the rescaled space.



                                          117
4. Combined weighting: feature and sample weighting together.

5. Above-median selection: only the features strictly above the median importance.

The weight of sample i is 1 − |yi − ȳN (i) |/(max y − min y), clipped to lie between 0.1
and 1, where ȳN (i) is the mean label of its 10 nearest neighbors by Manhattan distance.
Tree splits depend only on the order of values [244], so for tree models feature weighting
differs from no weighting only in that zero-importance features drop out. The rescaling
does change which samples are nearest neighbors, and so changes sample weighting.
Sample weighting down-weights rather than removes a suspect label, unlike neighbor-
based filtering (Section 4.3.3). We found no QSAR study that does this, and learned
per-sample weights for noisy labels come from general machine learning [245].


Fewer features or a tuned model

For the SVM and the random forest, the best hybrid at a small budget is fitted at
the model’s default setting. It is compared with a hybrid rebuilt at the largest width
we allow: the smallest of the pool width, twice the number of samples in the whole
dataset and 2,048 features. This wider hybrid is fitted at the setting that scores best
on the other folds (the SVM’s C or the forest’s maximum depth). Datasets whose two
downstream models include neither family are not run.


Adding a new embedding

Each embedding of the pool is taken in turn as the new one, and the search is run
with it and without it. As a control, the embedding’s selected features in the hybrid
are replaced by the same number of Gaussian random features, with no new search.
On LogD and hERG blocking the experiment ran on 2,000 molecules, drawn as whole
scaffold families.


Calibration

Split-conformal 90% prediction intervals are built on the training samples and scored
on the held-out samples by the fraction of held-out labels they cover and by their mean
width.


Stability and blueprints

Selection on fold m, for m = 1, . . . , F , gives a binary vector zm ∈ {0, 1}d . Here d is the
number of features in all base representations joined, not the size of the union of the

                                             118
selected features. We measure the stability of the selection with the index of Nogueira
et al. [246],
                              1
                               Pd       2
                              d  f =1 sf                 F
                    Φ̂ = 1 −           ,     s2f =        p̂f (1 − p̂f ).
                            k̄
                                1 −  k̄                F −1
                            d        d

Here p̂f is the fraction of folds that selected feature f , and k̄ is the mean number of
features selected. Φ̂ = 1 means the same features on every fold, and Φ̂ ≈ 0 is what
random selection gives. Each fold picks its own budget, and the index allows for the
resulting difference in set size through k̄. To read a blueprint, each selected feature is
traced to its base representation, and its importance in the fitted model is measured
with SHAP: TreeSHAP for tree models [192], the DeepLIFT-based SHAP explainer for
the DNN, and Kernel SHAP on 50 held-out samples for the SVM and the Gaussian
process [247].


5.2.10      Transfer, collaboration studies and separate sites
The first study asks whether search settings found on a large dataset can be reused on a
small one instead of a new search. The settings are the importance method, allocation
method, budget B and internal evaluator. The source is LogD, with 5,028 molecules,
and the target is hERG pKi , with 1,412 molecules. Both use the seven representations
the two datasets share: Avalon, ChemBERT, ECFP4, GROVER, MHG-GNN, Mordred
and SMI-TED. The source settings come from one search on every LogD molecule, none
of which is in the target. The closest prior work, Meta-QSAR [248], chose the learning
method and representation for a new QSAR dataset from results on over 2,700 others
and, like this study, reused decisions rather than selected features. We have not found a
paper that applies feature indices selected on one QSAR dataset unchanged to another,
although our literature search was not complete.
   Four comparisons run inside every held-out fold of the target: the best single repre-
sentation, a hybrid searched on the target’s training molecules as the upper reference,
the source settings with features re-selected on the target, and the source’s selected
features applied unchanged. The last two ask separately whether the search decisions
and the chosen features transfer. We use five folds grouped by scaffold family, RMSE
in pKi units and the two downstream models decided for both datasets, the DNN and
SVM.
   Two further studies come from a collaboration with the Oxford Protein Informatics
Group. The first repeats the activity-cliff QSAR setting of Dablander et al. [57] on
three targets, with five scaffold folds. Dopamine D2 (6,333 compounds) and factor Xa


                                             119
(3,605) come from ChEMBL, and the SARS-CoV-2 main protease (1,924) comes from
the COVID Moonshot project. Each target’s search draws on eight representations,
which do not include GROVER or SMI-TED. The downstream models are LightGBM
and the DNN, and the metric is the mean absolute error (MAE) in log units of activity.
   The second study counts the molecules that a representation cannot tell apart,
meaning that two or more molecules get an identical feature row, on every represen-
tation of the datasets in Table 4.1 (Section 4.3.1), and asks whether a hybrid removes
the problem. The representation with the most such molecules on hERG Ki , the WL
graph kernel, is the target. Two hybrids add features of two partner representations
to it. One adds ECFP4 and PDV, which are also blind to stereochemistry. The other
adds ECFP4 and MHG-GNN, which together give no two hERG Ki molecules the
same row. We report the score of each hybrid against the graph kernel alone, and the
number of molecules that still share a row in the hybrid.
   Federated learning trains one model across data held by several parties without
moving the data [249]. It has been run across pharmaceutical companies [250], whose
structures and assay results are commercial assets that cannot be pooled [18, 19].
Our collaborator, Lhasa Limited, uses the federated knowledge-distillation process of
Chapter 2 (Table 2.1) [152]. We use their code for the fingerprints, site models and site
clustering, and re-implement from it the step that combines the site models’ predictions.
   Our addition is that feature selection, as well as model fitting, takes place at the
sites. One search on the training molecules of all sites together chooses the settings
(allocation method, importance method and internal evaluator). Each site then selects
features with these settings on its own molecules and reports only feature indices,
importance values and one score. A coordinator splits the total budget B across sites
in proportion to their scores, with at least one feature for each site whose selection
succeeded. The main experiment below instead gives each site an even share, and
no experiment compares the two splits, so we cannot say what the site scores buy.
A feature already taken by another site is replaced by the site’s next-ranked feature,
which keeps all features in the hybrid distinct. Indices and importances are smaller,
and harder to invert, than model weights or gradients. In related work, each client
runs mutual-information feature selection on its own data and a server aggregates the
results [251].
   All four separate-site experiments run on Caco-2 and LogD, with five folds grouped
by scaffold family and three seeds, the DNN and SVM, and budgets of 500 to 2,000
features. In the main one, each site holds the same molecules but one family of rep-
resentations: substructure fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors or pretrained em-

                                          120
beddings. It compares the best single family, picked on the training molecules, with
one feature selection over all families, and both with one selection per family at an even
share of the budget. When a family is narrower than its share, the rest goes to the
other families. The even split can, as a result, build fewer features than one selection
over all families. The built widths are recorded on every fold.
   Three further experiments, with flipped labels, with one representation per site and
with features selected per cluster, are described in Additional file 33.
   The separate-site work has two further limitations beside the simulated sites (Sec-
tion 5.4). It depends on the collaborator’s implementation of the published process.
That process trains a student on a large separate public set, a role the training molecules
take here as a compromise, with held-out molecules excluded from it.


5.3     Results
We first compare the hybrid with single representations, with ensembles and with
simpler ways of choosing features. We then ask where its gain comes from and what
its blueprints show, and end with the collaboration and separate-site studies.


5.3.1    Performance of the hybrid
Every number in this section is a held-out score, in the dataset’s own metric, over five
outer folds unless stated otherwise, and most DNN results come from runs without the
prediction clip (Section 5.2.8).


Against the best single representation

On small molecules the hybrid scores better than the best single representation on every
dataset, with both downstream models (Table 5.14), and on the six regression datasets
it does so on every fold. On hERG blocking the hybrid is ahead on most folds with each
model. On every small-molecule dataset the hybrid also beats, with both models, the
single representation that was best on the held-out molecules, a choice no user could
make in advance (Table 5.14); on the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions it does not. On
Caco-2 with the DNN, for example, the best single representation in hindsight is PDV.
To see which representations the hybrid relies on, we shuffled each representation’s
features in turn on the held-out molecules (Additional file 24). On hERG Ki , Caco-2,
LogD, rat microsomal clearance and MDR1–MDCK efflux, the drop in score spreads
about equally over five to seven representations, none carrying a third of it. On QM9


                                           121
with LightGBM and on hERG blocking the hybrid leans on one representation, which
carries most of the score on QM9 and about half on hERG blocking. The hybrid still
beats every single representation there.

Table 5.14: Held-out score of the best single base representation and the hybrid, per
dataset and model, mean over outer folds, in each dataset’s own metric. Bold: the
four rows where the hybrid gains most over the best single base representation, as a
fraction of the single score. Best in hindsight is the single representation best on the
held-out samples, with its score.


                                             122
   Table 5.14 gives the antibody results, which differ from one dataset to the next.
On the anti-VEGF expression scan, one of the two largest antibody sets, the hybrid
is ahead with the SVM on all five folds. On ∆∆G the mean gain is the largest in the
chapter with both models, though the gain moves by more than its own size from fold
to fold. On EGFR binding, expression titer and AC-SINS self-association the hybrid’s
mean is higher with both models, while it wins on only two to four of the five folds.
   In an antibody representation, as in a molecular fingerprint, the features that matter
are few and buried among many that carry nothing: a language model’s embedding
of the whole sequence mixes the CDRs that vary with a framework that mostly does
not. Many of the antibody representations in the pools were built for this thesis to
separate the two (Table 5.11). On four antibody datasets the hybrid beats even the
single representation that was best in hindsight, and on two of them that representation
is one of ours (Table 5.14). On the anti-VEGF expression scan it is the CDR readout
of ESM3 with the SVM, and on ∆∆G it is the mutation features with the SVM and
the change from the parent embedding with the DNN.
   In the ∆∆G hybrid, the representations built for this thesis carry most of the
drop in score under shuffling with both models. On the other antibody datasets they
carry much less, so they matter most where the antibodies differ by defined mutations.
On AC-SINS self-association, another of the four, the hybrid draws mainly on two
antibody language models, IgT5 and AbLang-2. Across the antibody datasets the
hybrids draw on about one and a half to five representations by the same shuffling
(Additional file 24). On angiopoietin-2 binding the best representation in hindsight
with the SVM, AntiBERTy, was never ranked first on any fold’s training antibodies.
   On nanobody thermostability, SMAC colloidal stability, HIC retention and CHO
polyreactivity the hybrid adds nothing (Table 5.14). On HIC retention it is behind
with the Gaussian process, and here the search picked the same ESM-2 representation
on every fold, so the single representation was not the problem. The thermostability
label also pools five assays into one. On angiopoietin-2 binding, whose labels were read
off a published heatmap, the hybrid is higher with both models on three of five folds
(Table 5.14). Antibody humanness is a task one representation already solves, so the
hybrid has nothing to add.
   On the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions the hybrid lowers the RMSE with LightGBM
and raises it with the random forest (Table 5.14), both by less than the spread across
folds. The search drew on only two of the ten representations on every fold (Additional
file 28): the reaction fingerprint and the change in the MHG-GNN embedding from
reactants to product. The folds hold out whole additives, so on this split the reaction

                                          123
representations carry little that the hybrid could combine.


Against ensembles, and many representations against few

With the same downstream model on every representation, an ensemble of one model
per representation beats the hybrid with both models on most antibody datasets with
a result (Table 5.15). On the anti-VEGF expression scan the DNN ensemble is ahead
of the hybrid on every fold. On small molecules the hybrid holds up better: the
same-model ensemble beats it with both models only on hERG blocking, and on the
Buchwald–Hartwig reactions. The hybrid is ahead of both ensembles on QM9 with the
DNN and on hERG Ki with the SVM, on every fold. On antibodies the representations
are language models of one sequence that make similar predictions, and averaging them
smooths out the noise of each model on a small training set. On small molecules the
representations carry different information, and one model on their selected features
can use interactions that an average of separate models never sees.

   At the same budget, drawing on several representations beats drawing on one (Ta-
ble 5.16; every budget in Additional file 25). Filled from the top-ranked representation
alone, the budget scores worse than the search’s hybrid at every budget searched, with
both models, on most datasets with a result, and at all but one budget on the rest.
On small molecules, how the budget is split matters much less. An even split over the
same representations, at the same width, comes close to the search’s split there, except
QM9 with the DNN. On antibodies the split can matter. At the smallest budget, the
search’s split is well ahead on nanobody thermostability with the DNN, angiopoietin-2
binding with the SVM and SMAC colloidal stability with the DNN. The even split is
well ahead on ∆∆G with the SVM and expression titer with the random forest, and on
EGFR binding at every budget. On small molecules, the gain comes more from which
representations are drawn on, and from ranking features within each one, than from
the allocation rule that sizes their shares.

   On the four small-molecule regression datasets shown, the hybrid also gains on rare
scaffolds, with each half searched separately (Table 5.17). On the rare-scaffold half of
LogD the hybrid’s gain is larger than on the frequent half with both models, and so
it is on Caco-2 and QM9 with the DNN. On QM9 with LightGBM the gain is smaller
on the rare half, and QM9 has a rare-half result on only two folds. On hERG Ki the
hybrid is ahead in both halves, by less on the rare half, and on hERG blocking it is
ahead on the rare half with LightGBM only. On rare scaffolds few training molecules
resemble the held-out ones, so a single representation has less to go on.
   Against PCA of all representations joined, at the budget each fold’s search picked,
the results split by model. With the SVM the hybrid is ahead on every fold of hERG Ki ,
Caco-2, LogD, rat microsomal clearance and MDR1–MDCK efflux. With both models
it is ahead on every fold of QM9 and hERG blocking. With the DNN, PCA is ahead
on LogD on all 5 folds, 0.392 against 0.409 RMSE, and the hybrid is ahead on only 2
of 5 folds of Caco-2. On hERG Ki , MDR1–MDCK efflux and rat microsomal clearance
the DNN hybrid and PCA are within 0.0020 RMSE. Additional file 26 compares this
with two other experiments, one of which disagrees on LogD and Caco-2.
   On antibodies neither method wins throughout. With the SVM, PCA is ahead on
4 of 5 folds of the anti-VEGF expression scan and angiopoietin-2 binding, and on 3 of
5 of ∆∆G. With the DNN, PCA is ahead on 3 of 5 folds of the expression scan, and
the hybrid on 4 of 5 of ∆∆G, 0.354 against 0.239 Spearman.


Overfitting of the search

With budgets searched up to 8,000 features, and at half to twice the number of training
samples, the selection bias is small on the larger small-molecule datasets. On the small
antibody datasets the search promises much more than it delivers (Table 5.18). On
these datasets the folds also often pick a hybrid wider than their own training set, for
example on every fold of EGFR binding with the SVM. The search’s internal scores
can choose between configurations with a few thousand molecules, but not with 150 to
540 training antibodies, where its choice is partly noise.

   On permuted class labels, selecting features on the full dataset before cross-
validation gave Ambroise and McLachlan [185] an error close to zero, against close
to chance with selection redone inside each fold. Apart from the search’s own scores
in Table 5.18, every hybrid score in this chapter is on samples the search never saw,
so the gains above are not this bias. The bias still exists inside the search, and the
numbers above show where it is large: on small datasets, where many configurations
are compared on few samples [252].
   Leaving one more fold of training samples out of each fold’s search makes the

(Table 5.19). On QM9, hERG Ki , Caco-2, LogD and MDR1–MDCK efflux it is worse

5.3.2     Sources of the gain
This section asks whether the gain is more than a cut in the number of features,
and how it depends on the downstream model and on reusing the importance scores in
training. It ends with three smaller checks: tuning the model, adding a new embedding
and calibration.


Selection at a fixed number of features

Figure 5.3 and Additional file 27 compare the hybrid with recursive feature elimination
and random features over all base representations joined. Recursive elimination ranks
all joined features in one list, the pooled ranking the hybrid avoids (Section 5.2.2).
On QM9, Caco-2, LogD and MDR1–MDCK efflux the hybrid has the lower error at
every budget with both models. The exception is hERG Ki with the DNN, where
recursive elimination and even random features beat the hybrid at 1,000 and 2,000
features. On the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions recursive elimination is ahead at most
budgets. On antibodies (Figure 5.4) random features are beaten at most budgets, but
recursive elimination is ahead on ∆∆G with the SVM and on CHO polyreactivity at
every budget. On angiopoietin-2 binding, recursive elimination is ahead at 50 and
100 features with both models. mRMR has a result only at 100 features or fewer, on
nanobody thermostability, angiopoietin-2 binding, ∆∆G and AC-SINS self-association.
It is ahead of the hybrid at every budget on angiopoietin-2 binding, close to it on
thermostability and far behind it on ∆∆G.




                                         131
Dependence on the downstream model

Figures 5.3 and 5.4 show the budget each fold’s search picked, and Additional file 28 lists
it. We did not test whether the best settings follow measurable properties of a dataset,
so we only describe these picks. Much of what looks like a dataset pattern was fixed
in advance by each dataset’s grid (Table 5.6). The QM9, LogD and hERG blocking
searches could use only LightGBM gain. SMAC colloidal stability, HIC retention,
expression titer and CHO polyreactivity searched only budgets wider than their 152
to 198 training antibodies. Where the grid left a choice, the downstream model moved
the pick as much as the dataset did. Dataset size did not order the budgets either:
QM9, the largest dataset with 7,982 training molecules per fold, picked 1,000 features
on 4 of 5 folds with the DNN, below LogD’s 2,000.
   On Caco-2 the search picked more features for the DNN than for the SVM, and
on nanobody thermostability it picked 1,000 features for the DNN on four of five folds
against 250 or 500 for the SVM (Figure 5.4). On LogD it picked 2,000 for the DNN on
every fold, the top of the grid. With budgets searched up to 8,000 features, the DNN
picked 2,000 to 8,044 features on LogD (Table 5.18). The SVM’s smaller picks fit what
Chapter 4 showed for distances: a kernel computed over many features is diluted by the
ones that carry nothing, while a network can learn to ignore them. On angiopoietin-2
and ∆∆G the search picks at most a few hundred features. On expression titer the
random forest’s pick varies widely between folds, the selection noise of the previous
section showing up in the budget. No one budget or representation count would serve
every dataset and model, which is why both are searched.
   Across the configurations of a search, the choice of downstream model accounts
for more of the variation in score than the budget on most datasets with a result
(Table 5.20). On LogD, for example, it accounts for about half of the variance and
the budget for almost none. The budget still interacts with the model’s own settings
(Additional file 29). With the SVM strongly regularized (C of 0.01 or 0.1), the search
picks the same budget on every fold of LogD, rat microsomal clearance, MDR1–MDCK
efflux and Caco-2. With C = 10 it picks larger budgets on LogD and rat microsomal
clearance, since a model that is allowed to fit more can use more features. The width
of the DNN does not move its pick in the same way: on LogD it takes the top of the
grid at every width, on all folds but one.
   Scoring candidate hybrids with the downstream model itself, rather than a model of
another family, makes almost no difference on most small-molecule datasets (Additional
file 30). On LogD, Caco-2, hERG Ki and MDR1–MDCK efflux with the SVM the three

evaluator settings end within a few thousandths of an RMSE unit of each other. A
cheap evaluator ranks candidate feature sets about the same way as the model that
will use them, so on these datasets the search does not need the expensive one. The
exception is QM9 with LightGBM, where an SVM evaluator gives 0.363 RMSE against
0.346 for the matched one, over three folds. On some antibody datasets it matters:
the matched evaluator is best with both models on EGFR binding, HIC retention,
expression titer and colloidal stability, and on EGFR binding with the SVM it is well
ahead of a LightGBM evaluator.


Using importance during training

Using the importance scores again during training does little on small molecules (Ta-
ble 5.21). The best of the four conditions gains little over the plain hybrid on any
small-molecule dataset, and feature scaling makes LogD with the SVM worse. The
selection has already used the importance scores, so on small molecules there is little

                                         135
left for them to add. On antibodies some condition helps on most datasets, and by
more. Feature scaling helps colloidal stability with the DNN most, and keeping the
above-median features also helps ∆∆G with the SVM. Both act as further regulariza-
tion on training sets of a few hundred antibodies. Which condition helps changes from
one dataset and model to the next, so none of them is a default.
