   Choosing fewer features does not replace tuning the model on small molecules (Ta-
ble 5.22). A hybrid of up to 2,048 features with the model’s regularization setting
tuned beats the search’s smaller hybrid at the default setting. It does so on all four
small-molecule datasets with a result, for example 0.411 against 0.430 RMSE on LogD
with the SVM. On nanobody thermostability, angiopoietin-2 binding, ∆∆G, expres-
sion titer and the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions the smaller hybrid is ahead. On EGFR
binding, the anti-VEGF expression scan and antibody humanness the tuned wider hy-
brid is ahead. The budget and the model’s own regularization both control how much
the model can fit. With thousands of molecules the model’s setting does that better,
but with a few hundred antibodies neither does so on every dataset.
   On small molecules the hybrid uses a new embedding added as one more base rep-
resentation (Additional file 31): its selected features beat Gaussian random features
swapped into the same places in the same hybrid. No new search is run for the random
features, so this is a weaker test than giving a search random features to choose from.
The embedding’s features win for every embedding on hERG Ki , Caco-2 and MDR1–
MDCK efflux (three folds). On LogD, run on 2,000 molecules, they win for every em-
bedding except Uni-Mol2 with the DNN. On hERG blocking, run on 2,000 molecules
and three folds, GROVER and MoLFormer lose to random features with LightGBM,
and Mol2Vec loses with XGBoost. On EGFR binding and ∆∆G the embedding’s fea-
tures win for most embeddings with both models, and on CHO polyreactivity with the
Gaussian process. On SMAC colloidal stability, expression titer, antibody humanness,
CHO polyreactivity with the DNN and the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions they win for
fewer than half of the embeddings. On these antibody datasets another language model
of the same sequence adds little.

   With 90% conformal intervals, the hybrid’s intervals on small molecules cover close
to 90% of the held-out molecules, as the single representation’s do (Additional file 32).
They are also narrower, for example on LogD with the SVM. On several small antibody
datasets the hybrid’s intervals cover too few held-out antibodies, fewer than the single
representation’s, for example on angiopoietin-2 binding and ∆∆G with the SVM. The
calibration set is part of a training fold of a few hundred antibodies, and the hybrid
chosen on those antibodies is optimistic about them, the same selection bias as above.


5.3.3    Blueprints on specific datasets
Figure 5.5 shows one fold of each dataset: several hybrids draw on all ten representa-
tions in similar amounts, for example on LogD with the DNN and on the anti-VEGF
expression scan with the SVM. Elsewhere one or two representations carry the hybrid:
on EGFR binding almost all its features come from ESM-2 run on the joined chains.
   LogD can be read chemically (Figure 5.6). The SVM’s hybrid picked 372 features
on all five folds, 38 of them Mordred descriptors, 36 ECFP4 bits and the rest from eight
representations whose features carry no names. Among the descriptors are Crippen’s


                                          138
Figure 5.5: Number of features the hybrid took from each base representation, one bar
per dataset, on the first outer fold with the dataset’s first downstream model. Hatched
segments are representations built for this thesis. Dataset short names as in Table 5.8.


calculated logP (SLogP), the number of basic groups (nBase), several surface areas
binned by partial charge (PEOE_VSA), and counts of secondary amine and aromatic
nitrogen environments. Among the bits are tertiary amine environments, in which a
nitrogen is bonded to three carbons (CN(C)C), and aryl chloride environments. Unlike
logP, which counts only the neutral form of a molecule, logD at pH 7.4 counts every
charge state. At that pH a basic amine is largely protonated, which lowers logD below


                                          139
logP [112]. A model of LogD should therefore need both lipophilicity and basicity, and
the hybrid picked both on every fold. Lipophilicity comes from Mordred’s SLogP, and
basicity from Mordred’s nBase and from ECFP4 amine bits.




Figure 5.6: Number of features the SVM’s LogD hybrid picked on all five outer folds.
Mordred descriptors are grouped by descriptor family and ECFP4 bits by substructure.
Every other representation is one bar.

   The features picked are partly the same from fold to fold. On the stability index of
Nogueira et al. [246], the small-molecule hybrids score higher than the antibody and
Buchwald–Hartwig hybrids, and all score well below 1 (Table 5.19). Many features are
correlated within and across representations, so a feature on one fold can stand in for
a different one on another; an index well below 1 therefore need not mean the hybrid
fits noise. A blueprint should still be read for what recurs across folds, as for LogD
above. On antibodies, features drawn from several language models of one sequence
are largely interchangeable, and on a few hundred antibodies which of them is picked


                                         140
changes from fold to fold.


5.3.4     Collaboration studies
The small-molecule result carries over to the three activity-cliff targets of Dablander
et al. [57], data the hybrid was not developed on. For every target and model pair except
factor Xa with LightGBM, the hybrid has a lower error than the single representation
the same search ranked first (Table 5.23). With the DNN on factor Xa it is lower on all
five folds. These targets have many activity cliffs, which is where a single similarity-
based representation struggles most (Chapter 4).

   On Caco-2, MACCS keys and the WL graph kernel both give some molecules a row
that another molecule also has (Table 4.1). Amino-acid composition does the same to
432 of the 659 variants in the EGFR scan, which differ by single mutations. None of
the hybrids built on these datasets leaves two samples with the same row.
   On hERG Ki the graph kernel leaves some molecules sharing a row (Table 4.1).
Adding features from ECFP4 and MHG-GNN lowers its RMSE with the SVM on all
five folds, and no two molecules share a row (Table 5.24). Adding ECFP4 and PDV
instead improves it almost as much while some molecules still share a row. Most of
the gain therefore comes from the information the added representations give every
molecule, and little from breaking the ties.
   Search settings found on LogD were reused on hERG Ki , with the features selected
again on hERG Ki . They beat hERG Ki ’s best single representation with both models
(with the SVM on all five folds), but stay behind a search run on hERG Ki itself
(Table 5.25). Reusing settings saves the search on the smaller dataset at some cost in
error.

5.3.5    Features chosen across separate sites
Feature selection can run where data cannot be pooled. When each site holds one
family of representations, one selection over all families beats the best family and the
best single representation, on Caco-2 and LogD with both models (Table 5.26). On
LogD with the SVM, for example, it is well ahead of the best family, and the best
single representation is further behind. Selecting at each site with an even share of
the budget does as well as one selection over all families on every dataset and model,
although it can build a different number of features. Each site can therefore select on
its own and send only feature indices.
   Additional file 33 reports three further experiments: one hybrid over sites that each
hold one representation, selection at sites with flipped labels, and selection per cluster
of Caco-2.


5.4     Conclusions
This chapter asked how the best set of features can be selected for a given prediction
task (Chapter 1). We found that the answer depends on the dataset and the down-


                                           142
                              Best single   Best One selection,       Selection
        Dataset   Model
                           representation family   all families      per family
        Caco-2    DNN                 0.305     0.288        0.284        0.283
                  SVM                 0.304     0.301       0.2847       0.2845
        LogD      DNN                 0.453     0.446        0.419        0.420
                  SVM                 0.493    0.460      0.43823       0.43822

Table 5.26: Feature selection when each site holds one family of representations of
the same molecules. One row is one dataset and downstream model. Columns: the
best single representation; the best single family; one selection over all families; one
selection per family at an even share of the budget. Held-out RMSE, lower is better,
mean over three seeds of the five-fold mean.


stream model, and that a search can find it. On every small-molecule dataset the
hybrid beats the best single representation with both downstream models, and it beats
even the representation that was best in hindsight. A likely reason is that the rep-
resentations carry different information: on hERG Ki , Caco-2, LogD, rat microsomal
clearance and MDR1–MDCK efflux the hybrids draw on five to seven of them about
equally. On small molecules this supports the hypothesis behind the hybrid, that the
feature set is part of the problem.
   At the same number of features the hybrid beats recursive elimination and random
features on all but one small-molecule dataset with a result. Its gain is therefore more
than a reduction in width. Drawing on several representations beats drawing on the
top one alone on most datasets. On small molecules, which representations are drawn
on matters more than how the budget is split. Since we found no setting that suits
every dataset and model, the settings should be searched for each new task rather than
fixed in advance. The DNN picked more features than the SVM on Caco-2 and LogD,
and cheaper internal evaluators were good enough on most small-molecule datasets
but not on QM9 with LightGBM or on some antibody datasets. Finally, the selected
features can be read: on LogD the hybrid picks lipophilicity and basicity features on
every fold.
   On antibodies the hybrid may help for a similar reason: the features that matter
may be few and buried in representations of the whole sequence. On ∆∆G, AC-SINS
self-association and both anti-VEGF scans it beats every single representation, even the
one best in hindsight. On ∆∆G and the expression scan, that representation is one we
built to read the regions and mutations that vary. On most antibody datasets, however,
the large expression scan included, an ensemble of one model per representation does
better. On reactions the method gains nothing on the additive split. On data held at



                                              143
separate sites, selecting at each site does as well as one selection over all representations.
On most small-molecule datasets the hybrid can also take in new embeddings added
to the pool, but on several antibody datasets it gains little from them.
   The fourth question asked whether weighting features by their importance improves
the hybrid and model training. In construction, the features ranked by importance beat
random features of the same number on most datasets. In training, by contrast, using
the importance scores again adds little on small molecules and helps on some small
antibody datasets, with no condition that works everywhere.
   The hybrid needs data. Its gains are consistent on datasets of a few thousand
samples and inconsistent on a few hundred, where the search’s own scores overstate
what the hybrid delivers (Section 5.3.1). Most of our antibody datasets are that small,
so their results say as much about dataset size as about antibodies. The antibody pools
also offer few kinds of representation, mostly language models of the same sequence.
The search is costly and must be rerun when the data, representations or model change.
Where a simpler selection, such as recursive elimination or a tuned model on a wider
hybrid, does as well, the search costs more than it returns. The separate sites are
simulated from two public datasets, Caco-2 and LogD.
   This chapter used the hybrid only as input to a model. Whether hybrids keep
more of their performance under label noise, and whether their features stabilize noise
mitigation, are the subjects of Chapter 7. Chapter 6 first asks whether a feature set
chosen for the task also gives better neighbors, which every method that relies on
similarity could use, including the detection of noisy labels.




                                             144
Chapter 6

Similarity in an adaptive hybrid
representation

6.1     Introduction
Chapter 5 built the adaptive hybrid representation, a set of features selected from
several base representations for one task, as input to a model. Chapter 4 showed that
in most standard representations the distance between two molecules barely rises with
the difference in their labels. Any method that trusts a molecule’s neighbors inherits
this, such as predicting a label from the nearest neighbors or finding a noisy label
by comparing it with its neighbors’ labels. The first idea behind the hybrid was to
compute distances on the features relevant to the task alone, and this chapter tests
that idea.
   A hybrid can be built for similarity in two ways. In the first, the search of Chapter 5
uses a nearest-neighbor model as its downstream model, and we measure distances in
the hybrid it selects. In the second, the search ranks each candidate hybrid by how well
each molecule’s five nearest neighbors in it predict its label, and fits no downstream
model. We ask two research questions:

  1. Does distance in a hybrid track label differences better than distance in the best
      single representation?

  2. Do the methods built on that distance work better in a hybrid? We test pre-
      diction from nearest neighbors, activity cliffs, Bayesian optimization, distance to
      the training molecules as a guide to prediction error, retrieval of neighbors and
      clustering.

   This chapter uses 14 datasets: six small-molecule, seven antibody and one reaction

                                          145
dataset. Antibody datasets outnumber small-molecule datasets, so we give every result
by domain rather than pooled.


6.2     Methods
Datasets, folds (grouped by scaffold family for small molecules) and metrics are as
in Chapter 5, with five outer folds. Of its 20 datasets, we leave out six: rat micro-
somal clearance, both anti-VEGF scans, angiopoietin-2 binding, antibody humanness
and HIC retention. Table 6.6 has no weighted values on Caco-2, hERG blocking,
hERG Ki , expression titer and SMAC colloidal stability, whose runs measured un-
weighted distances only. In each outer fold, everything an experiment compares is
chosen from searches on that fold’s training molecules alone, and then scored on its
held-out molecules. Features are scaled on the training molecules: features that only
take the values 0 and 1 are left as they are, and the rest are standardized. In the
experiments, but not inside the searches, held-out values are cut to the training range
as well, which stops a single extreme molecule from dominating a distance. Where a
statement covers all 14 datasets, “molecule” also stands for an antibody or a reaction.
   The fold split, the sample of molecule pairs, the label permutations, the random
feature subsets and the k-means starts all use fixed seeds. The spread we report is
therefore the spread across the five outer folds of one split. We report the mean over
the five folds, and the per-fold values where the spread matters, without significance
tests. The simulation of choosing molecules to measure is repeated over three starting
sets instead.


6.2.1     Feature spaces compared
Each outer fold runs three searches on its training molecules. The first, the general
search, is the search of Chapter 5, run for each of the dataset’s two downstream models
(Table 5.6). On regression tasks the SVM is a support vector regressor. The second,
the distance search, uses a k-nearest-neighbor model as its downstream model, with
Euclidean distance and five equally weighted neighbors, or fewer when a fold has under
25 training molecules. These are the defaults of the scikit-learn model, which the search
does not change. The distance hybrid is therefore chosen under Euclidean distance with
equal weights, but scored in Section 6.3.2 under Manhattan distance with neighbors
weighted by rank. It has its own lists of importance methods, allocation methods,
numbers of representations and budgets. Its budgets are capped at 500 features, and


                                          146
on some datasets its other lists differ from the general search’s too. On hERG Ki ,
for example, the general search tries 250, 500, 1,000 and 2,000 features, while the
distance search tries 100, 250 and 500. The third is the similarity search, described in
Section 6.2.2. From these three searches, we take the following feature spaces:

   • the nearest-neighbor model’s best and second-best single representations, ranked
     by the distance search;

   • each downstream model’s best and second-best single representations, ranked by
     its general search;

   • each downstream model’s two best hybrids, four in all: the predictive hybrids;

   • the top hybrid of the distance search: the distance hybrid;

   • the top hybrid of the similarity search: the similarity hybrid, on the datasets
     where that search is defined.

   Unless stated otherwise, “the single representation” in the results means the nearest-
neighbor model’s best single representation, and “the hybrid” means the distance hy-
brid. Retrieval and clustering also score one control: the principal components of all
representations joined, with as many components as the hybrid has features.


6.2.2    Similarity in a hybrid
The search of Chapter 5 can be written as follows. Each configuration θ fixes an
importance method, an allocation method, a number of representations and a budget
B. Given base representations R1 , . . . , Rm , a configuration builds a feature set Sθ ⊂
R1 ∪ · · · ∪ Rm with |Sθ | ≤ B. The search returns Sθ∗ , where θ∗ = arg maxθ s(Sθ ) and
s is the search’s score. In the general search, s is a downstream model’s score on the
search’s own held-out folds. In the similarity search, s(S) = q5 (S), how accurately
each molecule’s label is predicted from its five nearest neighbors in S (Equation 6.1),
and no downstream model is fitted. All other settings and the folds are shared with
the general search. The neighbor score, computed on a validation set taken from the
fold’s training molecules, also replaces the internal evaluator when the budget is split.
   Zheng and Tropsha [137] and Itskowitz and Tropsha [138] chose descriptors to
maximize nearest-neighbor prediction within one descriptor set, and we modify this by
choosing features across several representations. The similarity hybrid tests whether
choosing features for how well the neighbors predict a label does better than choosing
them for a model’s prediction.

                                          147
   The score uses the Manhattan distance of Chapter 4, on features scaled as above.
Each standardized feature adds a term of similar size to this distance. A represen-
tation’s weight in the distance, which the allocation method sets, is thus roughly its
number of features divided by the budget. A fingerprint weighs less than this per fea-
ture, because a feature that only takes the values 0 and 1 adds less. The neighbor score
uses no importance weighting. In this score, Manhattan distance and inverse-distance
weights are design choices that we do not compare with alternatives.
   To rank whole candidates, we compute the score on the held-out molecules of each
fold of the search, inside the outer training fold. These molecules act as each other’s
neighbors. Their groups (scaffold families, for small molecules) are split into up to
5 parts, and each molecule’s 5 nearest neighbors are drawn from the other parts. A
neighbor from the same scaffold family is an easy match and would inflate the score.
This is the only place in the chapter where a molecule’s own group is kept out of its
neighbors. Each neighbor is weighted by the inverse of its distance [253]:
                                       P
                                         j∈N5 (i) yj /dij
                                 ŷi = P                    .                       (6.1)
                                           j∈N5 (i) 1/dij


A neighbor at distance 0 receives all of the weight, shared equally if there are several.
On regression data, the score q5 is the Spearman correlation ρ(ŷ, y). On binary data,
ŷi is the distance-weighted fraction of neighbors in class 1, and q5 = 2(AUROC − 0.5),
where AUROC is the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve. Both scores
put chance at 0, which the budget split needs, because each representation’s share of
the budget is its score divided by the sum of the scores. If any score is negative, all
scores are first shifted so that the lowest is 0. The modelability index of Golbraikh
et al. [129] (Chapter 4) is related, but uses a single nearest neighbor and a hard class
match.
   A fold of the search is undefined when it has fewer than 10 held-out molecules or
fewer than 2 groups, or when its labels or predictions are constant. A candidate hybrid
with an undefined fold is refused. We do not run the similarity search where its score
is undefined:

   • The four Ginkgo developability datasets in this chapter (AC-SINS self-
     association, SMAC colloidal stability, CHO polyreactivity and expression titer)
     use the competition’s five folds as their five groups. Inside an outer fold, each
     held-out block of the search is then a single group, so no antibody has a neighbor
     outside its own group.


                                           148
   • On the change in binding free energy (∆∆G) caused by a mutation, one protein
      complex holds 69 of the 429 rows. On outer folds 1 to 4 it falls alone into one
      held-out block of the search, and every candidate is refused. We skip this dataset
      on all five folds.


6.2.3     Nearest-neighbor settings
Every experiment that predicts from neighbors or measures retrieval scores each feature
space at every combination of the settings in Table 6.1, instead of at one chosen setting.
A held-out molecule’s prediction is the weighted mean of its neighbors’ labels,
                                           P
                                            j∈N (i)   wij yj
                                     ŷi = P k                 ,                                    (6.2)
                                                j∈Nk (i) wij


where Nk (i) is the k training molecules nearest to i under distance d, and wij is the
neighbor weight. On classification data, each class receives the summed weight of
its neighbors. Unlike in the similarity score, a training molecule from the held-out
molecule’s own group (scaffold family, for small molecules) can be one of its neighbors.
The one experiment that does not use these settings is the one on all representations
joined (Section 6.2.4).

                  first; 1/d2ij

weighting                                                          [255]

Table 6.1: Nearest-neighbor settings. One row is one setting, with its levels; every
combination of levels is scored. In the last row, s is the vector of feature importances,
s̄ its mean and a an exponent set by the distance. Minkowski distance is left out on
datasets above 5,000 molecules (hERG blocking, LogD and QM9).

   We found no published source for the rank scheme and count it as new. We sweep
it and 1/d2 rather than choose between them.
   Importance weighting is feature-weighted nearest neighbors [255], modified so that
the weights come from the importance scores of the hybrid search. Each feature is


                                               149
multiplied by its weight before the distance is computed, x′ = x ⊙ w, with negative
importances set to 0. For a single representation, the importance s is computed on the
fold’s training molecules, with LightGBM gain in most experiments. For a hybrid, it
is the importance its search used to select the features, with two exceptions. Distance
against label difference refits LightGBM gain on the hybrid’s features together, and
retrieval recomputes the search’s importance on the hybrid as a whole. We use two
formulas for w:

   • for distance against label difference and the best number of features, w = (s/s̄)a ,
     with a = 1 under Manhattan distance, a = 0.5 under cosine and a = 2 under
     Minkowski with p = 0.5;

   • for every other experiment that weights, w = s1/2 under every distance.

The exponent a of the first formula is set so that each feature’s term in the distance
is multiplied by its relative importance, sf /s̄, under all three distances. The second
formula has no such matching. Under Manhattan distance, a feature with four times
the average importance counts four times as much as an average feature under the
first formula, and only twice as much under the second. The weighted distances of
Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2 are therefore the same distance only under cosine. Some
experiments divide the second formula by a constant, which changes neither the ranking
of distances nor any prediction from neighbors. The square root was first written for
Euclidean distance and kept for the others, and no experiment in this chapter varies
the exponent.


6.2.4    Experiments
Table 6.2 lists every similarity experiment, and the paragraphs below define what each
one estimates.


Distance against label difference

For one feature space, this experiment estimates the Spearman correlation between the
distance of two held-out molecules and the absolute difference in their labels. A positive
value means that molecules further apart differ more in their labels. We estimate it in
each outer fold from a sample of up to 50,000 pairs. We measure it first in each of the
dataset’s representations alone, under every distance of Table 6.1, with no selection and
no importance weighting. We then measure it in the feature spaces of Section 6.2.1,
unweighted and weighted.

Prediction from nearest neighbors

We predict each held-out molecule’s label from its nearest training molecules (Equa-
tion 6.2), and make two comparisons. The first was fixed before the run, and nothing
in it is picked on the folds it is scored on. It compares the distance hybrid against
the single representation, both unweighted, under Manhattan distance with 5 neigh-
bors weighted by the inverse of their rank. The second picks a setting again for each
held-out fold, using the other four folds’ held-out scores. On the hybrid side, it picks
among up to six hybrids (the four predictive hybrids, the distance hybrid and the
similarity hybrid), weighted and unweighted, at every setting of Table 6.1, up to 216
combinations. On the single side, it picks among the nearest-neighbor model’s two sin-
gle representations, unweighted only, which gives 36. On the three datasets with two
distances, the numbers are 144 and 24, and where the similarity hybrid is undefined
they are 180 and 36. So the two sides differ in two ways at once. The hybrid side


                                                         151
picks from five or six times as many combinations, and only the hybrid side can use
importance weighting.


Importance weighting in four models

We score every feature space with and without importance weighting, using four models
that read a distance. The first three are prediction from nearest neighbors at every
setting, a Gaussian process with an RBF kernel and an SVM with an RBF kernel. The
fourth is each downstream model given four extra features from its nearest training
molecules, following q-RASAR [256]. The four extra features are the neighbors’ mean
label and its spread, the distance to the nearest neighbor and the mean distance to
the neighbors. They are computed at the three neighbor settings that rank best in a
screen on the training molecules. The Gaussian process and the SVM run on regression
data only. A separate experiment applies the weighting to all representations joined,
on regression datasets, once for each importance method.


Activity cliffs

An activity cliff is a pair of structurally similar molecules with very different activity
[58, 77]. Only the two hERG datasets measure an activity, but we keep the term for
QM9, Caco-2 and LogD. We decide which molecules are cliff molecules in each outer
fold, from its training molecules. We keep the structural side of the rule of Hu et al.
[148]: two molecules are similar when their ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity is at least
0.55. We modify its activity side, because a gap of two log units flags no molecule at
all on Caco-2. Instead, we choose the gap so that 15% of the training molecules are
cliff molecules. A training molecule is a cliff molecule when it is in at least one similar
pair whose labels differ by more than the gap. On classification data, a similar pair
counts when its two molecules are in different classes. A held-out molecule is a cliff
molecule when it has at least two similar training molecules whose labels span more
than that gap; its own label is never read. Each downstream model is fitted on its two
best single representations and its two best hybrids. Each fit is scored separately on
the cliff molecules and on the other held-out molecules. The main comparison is the
first hybrid against the best single representation.


Distance against prediction error

This follows Sheridan et al. [257], who found that a molecule’s similarity to the training
molecules tracks how well it is predicted. Each downstream model is fitted twice, on


                                           152
all representations joined and on its own first hybrid, and we record its error on every
held-out molecule. On regression data this is the absolute error, and on classification
data it is one minus the predicted probability of the true class. We then compute
the Spearman correlation between that error and the molecule’s mean distance to its
nearest training molecules, at 5, 10 and 20 neighbors and every distance. We do this
in each feature space of Section 6.2.1, unweighted and weighted.


Retrieval and clustering

Retrieval measures how close a molecule’s neighbors are to it in label. For each held-out
molecule, the label gap (lower is better) is the weighted mean absolute difference be-
tween its label and those of its nearest training molecules, at every setting of Table 6.1.
On classification data, it is one minus the weighted share of neighbors in the molecule’s
class. For clustering, we fit scikit-learn’s k-means [258] on the training molecules with
3, 5 and 10 clusters, and assign each held-out molecule to a cluster. Label coherence
(higher is better) is one minus the mean standard deviation of the held-out labels within
a cluster, divided by the standard deviation of all held-out labels. On classification
data, it is the mean share of the largest class in each cluster. Clusters with fewer than
two held-out molecules are left out.


Three smaller checks

To find the best number of features, we take each downstream model’s top hybrid
at every budget of its search. At each budget we record, for that same hybrid, its
prediction score and the correlation between distance and label difference, and find
the budget where each peaks. On hERG Ki , QM9 and Buchwald–Hartwig only, we
simulate choosing which molecules to measure, a form of Bayesian optimization. The
search looks for the lowest label on hERG Ki and QM9, and the highest yield on
Buchwald–Hartwig. Starting from a set of whole groups (scaffold families on hERG
Ki and QM9, additives on Buchwald–Hartwig) holding at least 5% of the molecules, a
surrogate model picks 100 molecules one at a time by expected improvement [259]. We
repeat this from three starting sets, and the score is the area under the best-found-so-
far curve. Last, for each representation a predictive hybrid draws from, we compare the
label information in the features it kept against principal components and a random
subset of the same size. Each block of features is reduced to at most 50 principal
components. A mutual-information estimate is summed over the components, and the
mean over three label permutations is subtracted. We use the estimators of scikit-learn


                                           153
1.6.1, which follow Kraskov et al. [260] for continuous labels and Ross [261] for classes.
This sum counts information shared by two components twice.


6.3        Results
We first ask whether distance in a hybrid tracks label differences more closely than in a
single representation, and then whether the methods built on that distance gain from
it.


6.3.1       Distance against label difference
As in Chapter 4, distance in a standard representation rises only weakly with label
difference (Table 6.3). On the small-molecule datasets, the best of 30 pairs of represen-
tation and distance, picked on the held-out molecules, reaches at most 0.281 Spearman,
on MDR1–MDCK efflux. It varies widely between folds, for example on hERG Ki .
      On Buchwald–Hartwig and the small-molecule datasets other than MDR1–MDCK
efflux, no representation has a correlation at or below zero. On MDR1–MDCK efflux,
EGFR binding, CHO polyreactivity, AC-SINS self-association and SMAC colloidal
stability, at least one representation does (Table 6.3). SMAC colloidal stability is
weakest: its best single representation reaches 0.016 Spearman, and about half of its
combinations of representation and distance are at or below zero.
      In the distance hybrid, distance tracks label differences more closely on all six small-
molecule datasets, all seven antibody datasets and Buchwald–Hartwig (Table 6.4). It
is higher in every fold on LogD and lower in two folds on Caco-2.
      The predictive hybrids, built for each downstream model, do not always do this. On
Caco-2, MDR1–MDCK efflux, hERG blocking, EGFR binding, expression titer, SMAC
colloidal stability and Buchwald–Hartwig, some track label differences less closely than
their model’s best single representation (Table 6.5). On CHO polyreactivity all four
are lower, for example 0.106 to 0.064 Spearman for the DNN’s first hybrid. A hybrid
used for distance therefore has to come from the distance search.
      Nine datasets have weighted values (Table 6.6). On six of them, importance weight-
ing alone closes most of the gap between the single representation and the distance
hybrid. On LogD, AC-SINS self-association and Buchwald–Hartwig, the weighted sin-
gle representation passes the unweighted distance hybrid, on LogD with 0.276 against
0.269 Spearman. On EGFR binding, CHO polyreactivity and ∆∆G, weighting closes
little of the gap.

   Instead of the representation the nearest-neighbor model ranked first for prediction,
we can pick, with hindsight on the held-out molecules, the representation and distance
whose distance best follows the label (Table 6.3). That pick beats the distance hybrid
on Caco-2, EGFR binding, expression titer and CHO polyreactivity (Table 6.6). On
Caco-2 the similarity hybrid beats that pick in turn.
   On QM9, Caco-2, MDR1–MDCK efflux, LogD, nanobody thermostability and CHO
polyreactivity, the DNN’s hybrid predicts best at more features than the number at
which its distances best track label differences (Table 6.7). On hERG Ki and expression
titer both peak at the same number of features, and on EGFR binding, SMAC colloidal
stability and ∆∆G the prediction score peaks at fewer. On LogD the DNN’s hybrid
predicts better at 2,000 features than at 500, while distance in the same hybrid falls
from 0.231 to 0.154 Spearman.


6.3.2     Prediction from nearest neighbors
At the setting fixed before the run, prediction from the five nearest neighbors is more
accurate in the distance hybrid on all six small-molecule datasets (Table 6.8). It is
better in every fold on LogD, and worse in one of five folds on hERG Ki and Caco-2
(Additional file 34).
   Picking the setting again for each fold does not change this: the hybrid still wins on
all six small-molecule datasets, on LogD from 0.638 to 0.565 RMSE (Table 6.9). This
comparison favors the hybrid (Section 6.2.4).

while on LogD and MDR1–MDCK efflux it barely moves (Table 6.10). With neighbor-

ten neighbors under cosine distance and gets worse with twenty.
   Weighting also improves prediction from all representations joined (Table 6.11).
With 10 neighbors, weighting by LightGBM gain improves all five regression small-
molecule datasets, for example QM9 from 0.575 to 0.484 RMSE. At least one impor-
tance method helps on every antibody dataset and Buchwald–Hartwig. Weighting loses

the gain comes from the importance itself.

similar for each distance and number of neighbors, and the hybrid loses on ∆∆G under
all three distances. Weighting both sides shrinks the gain on every dataset where the
hybrid wins, on LogD from 0.089 to 0.025 RMSE. Part of what the hybrid does for a
distance is therefore what weighting does. Where the hybrid loses, the spread across
folds is larger than the loss, for example on ∆∆G and AC-SINS self-association. These
datasets have a few hundred training antibodies, against one to eight thousand training

6.3.3     Activity cliffs
We report activity cliffs on the six small-molecule datasets only. The antibody datasets
have too few held-out cliff antibodies for a score; on ∆∆G, 11 of the 429 held out over
five folds. The rule for held-out molecules is looser than the one for training molecules,
so the share of cliff molecules differs between the two. It is highest on LogD and hERG
blocking, nearly half of the held-out molecules in some folds, and lowest on QM9.
   On LogD, Caco-2, hERG Ki and hERG blocking, each model’s first hybrid beats its
best single representation on cliff molecules and on the other molecules (Table 6.13).
On Caco-2, hERG Ki and hERG blocking the gain is larger on cliff molecules; on
Caco-2 the DNN goes from 0.340 to 0.288 RMSE on them. The gain on cliff molecules
is slightly smaller on LogD, but both models gain on them in every fold. A separate
run of the same comparison flags the same cliff molecules and agrees in direction on
all four datasets.
   On QM9 and MDR1–MDCK efflux the hybrid is better on the other molecules for
both models (Table 6.13). On each, only two folds hold the 10 cliff molecules a score
needs. Averaged over those two folds, the QM9 hybrid is worse on cliff molecules for
both models, for the DNN from 0.242 to 0.280 RMSE. On MDR1–MDCK efflux it is
worse for the DNN, from 0.455 to 0.465 RMSE, and level for the SVM. Fold by fold,

6.3.4    Prediction error, retrieval and clustering
A molecule’s distance to its nearest training molecules rises weakly with the error made
on it (Table 6.14). The distance hybrid tracks error better for both models on QM9,
Caco-2, MDR1–MDCK efflux, hERG Ki and LogD, and worse for both on hERG
blocking. The largest gain is on hERG Ki , from 0.200 to 0.276 Spearman with the
SVM’s errors, while on LogD it is small.
    On nanobody thermostability, ∆∆G, expression titer, CHO polyreactivity and AC-
SINS self-association, the hybrid tracks error better for both models (Table 6.14). It
tracks error worse for both on SMAC colloidal stability, where the DNN goes from 0.136
to 0.030 Spearman, and on Buchwald–Hartwig. Distance in the single representation
does not rise with error for either model on Buchwald–Hartwig, ∆∆G and expression
titer.

   With the setting picked again for each fold, the hybrid retrieves neighbors with
closer labels on all six small-molecule datasets, and QM9’s label gap falls from 0.601
to 0.393 (Table 6.15). On hERG blocking, LogD, MDR1–MDCK efflux and QM9, the
hybrid’s k-means clusters are also more coherent at 3, 5 and 10 clusters (Table 6.16).
On Caco-2 and hERG Ki the result changes with the number of clusters; on Caco-2
the hybrid loses at 3 clusters and wins at 5 and 10.
   With the setting picked again, the hybrid retrieves neighbors with closer labels on
every antibody dataset except ∆∆G, where the label gap rises from 1.439 to 1.507
(Table 6.15). It is also worse on Buchwald–Hartwig. Principal components of all
representations retrieve better than the hybrid only on EGFR binding, and tie on CHO
polyreactivity. Clustering is mixed: at every cluster count the hybrid’s clusters are more
coherent on nanobody thermostability and CHO polyreactivity, and less coherent on
EGFR binding and SMAC colloidal stability (Table 6.16).
   Except on QM9 and Buchwald–Hartwig, the features a hybrid keeps carry no more
label information than principal components of the same number (Additional file 36).

6.4     Conclusions
Chapter 4 found that distance in standard representations barely rises with label dif-
ference. On the six small-molecule datasets, we found that distance in a hybrid built
for distance tracks label differences more closely than in the single representation. Pre-
diction from nearest neighbors improves on all six, both at the setting fixed before the
run and with the setting picked again. In addition, the neighbors retrieved in the hy-
brid have closer labels. On LogD, Caco-2, hERG Ki and hERG blocking, each model’s
first hybrid also predicts cliff molecules better than its best single representation. The
similarity hybrid tracks label differences more closely than the distance hybrid only on
Caco-2 and hERG blocking, of the nine datasets where it has a value (Table 6.6).
   Weighting features by importance does part of the same work without any selec-
tion. On LogD it lets the single representation track label differences better than the
unweighted hybrid. The hybrid’s distance does, however, track prediction error worse
on hERG blocking, and except on QM9 and Buchwald–Hartwig its kept features carry
no more label information than principal components of the same size.
   On the antibody datasets and Buchwald–Hartwig, distance in the hybrid also tracks
label differences more closely, but the methods built on it do not always gain. With

                                         168
Chapter 7

Detecting and handling label noise

7.1     Introduction
In Chapter 6, on the six small-molecule datasets, distance in a hybrid built for distance
tracked the label more closely than distance in a single representation. Prediction from
nearest neighbors also improved on all six. This chapter asks whether that helps with
label noise. Can we find the noisy labels in a QSAR dataset, and does acting on them
improve a model’s held-out score? We ask this on two kinds of labels. The first has
damage injected on purpose, so we know which labels were damaged and by how much.
The second is the labels as delivered, which carry assay error that nobody has marked.
   Every method in this chapter reads a feature space.          A detector compares a
molecule’s label with its neighbors’ labels, or with a model’s prediction, and both
depend on the features. The adaptive hybrid representation (Chapter 5) leaves out
features that do not relate to the label yet add a term to the distance (Chapter 4).
For every method we therefore ask whether it improves the held-out score and, where
it does, whether it improves the hybrid more than the best single representation.
   The methods fall into the three groups of Table 2.3. This chapter expands the
sweep of twelve published methods in Chapter 4 (Section 4.3.3). It also adds methods
that select molecules by a known flag, such as a label at an assay limit, which none of
the published methods uses. QM9, whose labels are computed rather than measured,
is the clean-label control (Section 7.2.5).


7.2     Methods
This chapter uses the datasets of Chapters 5 and 6 (Table 5.7), plus one more, hERG
IC50 (HIC50 in the figures, Table 5.8), which is used only here. Its labels are pIC50


                                              169
values for the hERG channel from ChEMBL. The IC50 is the concentration of a molecule
that gives half of the maximal inhibition in an assay, and pIC50 is its negative base-10
logarithm in molar units. hERG IC50 is the one dataset with real repeat measurements.
In this chapter, “hERG” on its own means the hERG blocking dataset, whose labels
are blocker or non-blocker. Folds and metrics are those of Chapter 5. Seven datasets
of Table 5.7 are not used here: rat microsomal clearance, anti-VEGF scan expression
and binding, EGFR binding, angiopoietin-2 binding, antibody humanness and HIC
retention.
   Each dataset has two downstream models. In each outer fold, a search on that
fold’s training molecules ranks each model’s two best single representations and two
best hybrids. Every method is tested on all four. In most experiments that search
sees the clean training labels. Only the hybrid’s features are then re-ranked, and its
budget re-split between base representations, on the damaged labels; in this way the
hybrid’s features move with the damage. The base representations and the way the
hybrid is built, however, are chosen with clean labels, which a real user would not have.
There are two exceptions. The expanded published sweep repeats the search for every
damage kind, level and draw, trained and scored on the damaged labels alone, and
the experiment on cleaning before the feature search (Section 7.2.3) compares cleaning
before the search with cleaning after it.
   We keep apart three tasks that are often treated as one: finding the damaged labels,
sizing the damage, and improving the model’s held-out score. A method can succeed
at the first and fail at the third. Figure 7.1 shows how one method is run and scored
in one outer fold.


7.2.1    Noise conditions
We damage a regression dataset in six ways (Table 7.1). Four are noise conditions from
Section 2.5.1: Gaussian, outlier, grouped-shifted and censoring. Censoring clips labels
at a fixed value that imitates an assay’s reporting limit, the edge of its measurable
range. The clip is at the top of the label range. For potency labels such as hERG Ki ,
real censoring falls mostly at the low end; the clip imitates the rarer case. Under outlier
noise, a random 5% of molecules get an error three times wider, where Chapter 3 used
10%. Because every other label still gets the ordinary error, the 5% carry about a
third of the squared error, not all of it. The last two kinds raise a random 10% of the
training labels by one fixed amount, in the two units of Table 7.1. The grouped-wider
condition and the Student-t and Laplace shapes of Chapter 3 are not used here.


                                            170
Figure 7.1: How a noise method is run and scored in one outer fold. Noise mitigation
selects molecules by a detector, a known flag or every molecule, then acts on them.
Noise-robust training selects nothing and changes the model. Dashed: the controls.


   A classification dataset has its labels flipped instead, and the level is the share of
training labels flipped. Six rules decide which labels flip. A label can flip uniformly,
more often in the rarer classes or more often in one direction. It can also flip at a rate
that varies by molecule, in proportion to class frequency, or along a confusion matrix.
With no matrix given, the last rule is the same as the first, which leaves five distinct
rules on a two-class dataset.
   Most experiments first run a screen of two levels (Table 7.2). The screen leaves out
the shift sized by model error. The held-out predictions it produces correlate 0.93 to
0.97 with those of the plain shift in 10%. Two draws of the plain shift correlate about
as closely with each other. An experiment that is taken further runs all six kinds at the

full-run levels, on datasets of 5,000 molecules or fewer. Each non-zero level is drawn
three times, except censoring, which involves no random draw and runs once. The
random seed depends on the damage kind and the draw, not on the level or the fold.
The levels therefore nest: every molecule’s error at level 1.0 is twice its error at 0.5,
and a molecule carries the same damage in every fold it trains in.

Table 7.2: Damage levels in the screen and in the full run. Regression levels are in the
units of Table 7.1.

   Only the training labels are damaged. As in Chapter 3, the held-out labels of the
outer fold are left as delivered: every score in this chapter is against the delivered
labels. Where a search runs on damaged labels, as in the expanded published sweep,
the inner folds of that search are scored on damaged labels too.
   Three settings involve no injection: the labels as delivered, whose errors nobody
has marked; hERG IC50 , where the disagreement between repeat measurements is real
noise; and QM9 with no noise added, whose labels are clean. Three experiments add

                                              172
damage of their own. The experiment on break-even precision gives a random 5% of
molecules wide errors and leaves every other label alone. The experiment that changes
the model adds Gaussian noise sized by a LightGBM model’s error. The experiment
on measuring sources simulates several measurements per molecule.


7.2.2     Noise detection
We group the detectors (Table 2.3) by what they read: an out-of-fold prediction, the
neighbors’ labels, or a model’s predicted spread (Table 7.3). Every detector of the first
two kinds reads the molecule’s own label. Three of the third kind, the heteroscedastic
GP, the variance-head network and NGBoost, predict a spread from the features alone
and never read it. Such a detector can only find a wrong label if the damage is tied
to structure. Most detectors only rank molecules, but a few of the third kind return a
spread in the label’s own units, which can feed a weight or a move.
   The gap over neighbor spread is the buddy check used to screen weather-station
readings, which divides a reading’s gap from its neighbors’ mean by their standard
deviation [262]. RegENN applies a similar threshold to filter noisy regression labels
[263], but we found no earlier use of either on molecular labels. The cliff-aware gap
divides by the spread of all labels plus that of the neighbors. A molecule among
neighbors that disagree with each other, as near an activity cliff, scores lower as a result.
SLOM lowers an outlier score in the same way where a neighborhood is unstable, but
for spatial data, where neighbors are found by location [264].
   The variance-head network predicts a mean µ(x) and a variance σ 2 (x) and is trained
with
                                       (y − µ)2
                                                        
                               1
                            L=                         2
                                                + log σ · σ 2β ,                       (7.1)
                               2          σ2
where β = 0.5 keeps the variance from hiding the errors in the mean [265].
   A neighbor detector needs a space to find neighbors in, a neighbor count k and a way
to weight the neighbors. We try k of 5, 10 and 20, and weight each neighbor by 1/rank
or by 1/d2 . The setting is chosen inside each fold, without the held-out molecules. We
hold back 20% of the training scaffold families and predict each held-back label as the
weighted mean of its neighbors’ labels among the other training molecules. The three
settings that predict best are kept. The space is a single representation, a hybrid, or
one of the similarity spaces of Chapter 6.
   Two experiments change the space itself. The first multiplies each z-scored feature
by the square root of its importance, rescaled so that the multipliers average 1, before
the neighbors are found. The importance is computed against the damaged training

deviation of the labels of molecule i’s k nearest training neighbors, and σy is the

labels, as feature-weighted nearest neighbors do [255]. The second compares four fami-
lies of distance for the neighbor detectors. They are the plain features under Euclidean
and Manhattan distance, the features weighted by importance raised to 0.5 or 1.0,
forest proximity, and the hybrid’s own features. The forest proximity of two molecules
is the share of trees in which they fall in the same leaf [266], and their distance is one
minus it. The forest has 100 trees with at least 5 molecules per leaf, fitted to the fold’s
damaged training labels.
   One experiment compares several further detectors with the out-of-fold residual.


                                            174
One is the value of each molecule to a nearest-neighbor predictor, as a Shapley value
[267, 268]. Another is the cleanlab regression score [269], which draws its internal folds
at random rather than by scaffold family. The rest divide the residual by a spread for
that molecule, from NGBoost, a quantile forest or a conformal interval.
       Two experiments test the out-of-fold LightGBM residual under the shift in 10% sized
by model error. The first ranks molecules by the residual itself. The second divides
each residual by a second LightGBM model’s out-of-fold estimate of how large that
molecule’s residual usually is. It then turns the result into conformal p-values against
a calibration set of training molecules [270]. Molecules are flagged with the Benjamini–
Hochberg rule [271], which bounds the expected share of false flags at 5%, 10% or 20%.
The bound holds only if the calibration labels are clean. The calibration set is a random
20% or 50% of the training molecules or, where a dataset has repeat measurements,
the molecules with three or more measurements that agree. Both experiments grade
the ranking by average precision.
       A detector can be graded only where the damage moved some labels and not others,
so that it is known which molecules were damaged. That is outlier noise, censoring,
the two shifts in 10% and every label flip. Under Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise
every label moves, and no detection score exists. We grade a detector by the average
precision of its suspicion scores against the set of damaged molecules. A detector that
ranks at random scores the damaged share of the training molecules; that share is the
chance level. Some experiments report the area under the ROC curve instead, where
chance is 0.5.


7.2.3        Noise mitigation
Table 7.4 describes each noise mitigation method by its two halves (Table 2.3). The
methods reduce to a few selectors crossed with three actions.
       Most methods read the suspicion score rescaled to [0, 1], s̃i = (si − min s)/(max s −
min s). Deleting drops the molecules above a quantile of s (Table 7.4). This is the
filtering of Brodley and Friedl [157], which Zhao et al. [272] tested on QSAR modeling
sets. Down-weighting gives molecule i the weight 1/(1 + αs̃i ), and all weights are then
divided by their mean. A proportional move takes the label part of the way to a target
ti ,
                                    yi′ = yi + α s̃i (ti − yi ),                       (7.2)

so the most suspect label moves a share α of the way and the least suspect does not
move. The target is the weighted mean of the molecule’s neighbors’ labels [273, 274],


                                               175
or an out-of-fold LightGBM prediction, which is close to the target blending of Reed
et al. [275]. One further rule, the score-scaled shrink, moves a share 0.5s̃i /(1 + 0.5s̃i )
of the way to the out-of-fold prediction. It is modeled on James–Stein shrinkage [276],
which pulls an estimate toward a common value by an amount set by its variance. Here
the variance cancels out of the share, so the share depends only on the score.


Selected by a detector

The main cleanup experiment takes the out-of-fold forest residual as its detector and
crosses it with each action above. The cliff-aware gap is graded as a detector beside it.
A second experiment asks whether to delete a flagged molecule or keep it, and keeps it
either with a lower weight or with a moved label. Its control deletes the same number of
molecules at random, at five seeds. A third experiment replaces the forest residual with
each of five detectors in turn: the cliff-aware gap, and the GP, the heteroscedastic GP,
the variance-head network and NGBoost. Each deletes its top 5%, 10% or 20%. Where
the detector has its own prediction, it also moves every label toward that prediction.
The share moved is given by Equation 7.2, or by ci = αsi /(1 + αsi ), which uses the
score in its own units.
   Four experiments weight molecules by a neighbor or model detector, with no dele-
tion. Three use the weight 1/(1 + 3s̃i ), under which the most suspect molecule counts
a quarter as much as the least suspect. They differ in where the neighbors are found:
in features scaled by importance on clean labels, in the importance-scaled space above,
and in each of the four families of distance. The fourth uses exp(−0.1 s̃i ) with the gap
over neighbor spread, the GP or NGBoost as the detector. With that weight, the most
suspect molecule counts about 0.9 times as much as the least suspect.
   Close-pair weights look only at pairs of near-identical molecules. A pair is close
if its distance is in the lowest 1% of all training pairs. The label noise is estimated
from the closest fifth of those pairs as σ̂ 2 = ∆y 2 /2, since two noisy copies of one value
differ by twice the noise variance. Both molecules of any close pair whose labels differ
                 √
by more than 3 2 σ̂ get weight 0.25. Residual flags give weight 0.25 to the top 5%,
10% or 20% of molecules by out-of-fold LightGBM residual. The same experiment also
asks how precise a detector has to be before acting on its flags helps. It flags sets of
molecules of the same size with a known share of truly damaged molecules, from none
to all, and finds the share at which the gain from acting crosses zero. We call that
share the break-even precision.
   Cliff put-back runs after a cleanup and undoes it on activity-cliff molecules: deleted
molecules return, lowered weights go back to 1 and moved labels go back to their

Table 7.4: Noise mitigation methods, by what selects the molecules. s̃i is the suspicion
score rescaled to [0, 1] and α is the strength. Weights are divided by their mean. The
four deleting methods of the published sweep are in Table 7.5.




                                                      177
training value. Here the cleanup acts on the top 1%, 5% or 20% of flagged molecules. A
training molecule is a cliff molecule if it has a partner with ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity
of at least 0.55 [148] whose label is far from its own [76, 77]. Far means more than twice
the median absolute deviation of the labels. On datasets whose label is not a biological
activity, such as LogD, the same test finds pairs that differ sharply in that property,
and we keep the name. The cliff set is rebuilt in every fold from the damaged training
labels. Its control puts back the same number of molecules drawn at random from
the non-cliff molecules. We found no earlier method that puts examples back after a
cleanup. Some cleaning methods exempt a whole class decided in advance [277, 278],
and Brodley and Friedl [157] name the risk of discarding correct exceptions without
giving a rule.
   The hybrid’s features are chosen against the labels, which means the labels can
be cleaned before the features are chosen or after. One experiment compares the two
orders, with nine cleanups that use the out-of-fold forest residual as the detector, under
injected damage and on labels as delivered. The search and the fit each use the labels
as given or the cleaned labels, four combinations in all. Down-weighting cannot reach
the search, because no weight enters the feature ranking or the budget split. Its two
cleaned-search arms consequently do not exist. No source we found crosses features
chosen on cleaned or recorded labels with a fit on cleaned or recorded labels. The
nearest design ranks features on one version of the data and fits on another, but its
second step is class-imbalance sampling, not label cleaning [279].
   On labels as delivered, nothing says which molecules are wrong. A gain can come
from removing hard molecules rather than wrong ones. One experiment runs seven
cleanups on delivered labels with the neighbor gap and the forest residual as detec-
tors. Its control shuffles each detector’s scores across the training molecules, at three
seeds, and reruns the cleanup with the same share and strength. The same number of
molecules is touched, but not the ones the detector picked.


Selected by a known flag

When a label is clipped at an assay limit, we know two things that a detector has to
guess: which labels are wrong, and in which direction. The limit L is read off the
clipped training labels as their largest value, if at least two molecules and 1% of the
training molecules sit on it. An out-of-fold LightGBM fit to the clipped labels gives
each molecule a prediction pi . Let σ be the standard deviation of yi − pi over the




                                           178
molecules below the limit, and mi the inverse Mills ratio,

                                      ϕ(ai )               L − pi
                             mi =              ,    ai =          ,                 (7.3)
                                    1 − Φ(ai )               σ

where ϕ and Φ are the standard normal density and distribution function. The normal
model for labels cut at a limit is that of Tobin [280]. The truncated-normal mean re-
places each clipped label by the mean of a normal distribution centered on its prediction
and cut at the limit,
                                 yi′ = max(L, pi + σmi ) .                          (7.4)

Censored EM repeats this for three rounds, refitting the downstream model out of fold
to the current labels each round, after the iterated estimator of Buckley and James
[281]. The constant lift raises every clipped label to L + σm, the mean of σmi over the
clipped molecules. That shared amount is larger than the truncated-normal mean’s
lift by L − pi on average. Two arms test what the flag is worth. Deleting the clipped
molecules uses the flag but throws the molecules away. A per-molecule lift raises
each clipped label by its own σmi , and its control deals the same lifts to the wrong
clipped molecules, at three seeds. Svensson et al. [83] fitted censored regression to
drug-discovery labels, which is the closest precedent.
       A scaffold family is also a known flag. Under grouped-shifted noise every family
carries its own offset. A family’s mean out-of-fold residual estimates that offset, and
subtracting it from every label in the family undoes it. Three versions differ in how
much they trust a small family. The first subtracts the whole mean residual from every
family, and the second only from families of three or more training molecules. The third
is a random-intercept model, fitted by up to five rounds of expectation maximization
in the style of mixed-effects random forests [282, 283]. It shrinks each family’s offset
toward zero, more for a family with few molecules.
       A source is a known flag when each molecule was measured by several of them. One
experiment simulates eight sources, and each molecule is measured by one to four of
them, drawn from hERG IC50 ’s real counts or uniformly. Measurement s of molecule
i is
                                            √                
                                                                                    (7.5)
                                                    p
                         yis = yi + bs + σe   ρ ui + 1 − ρ vis ,

where bs is the source’s offset, ui is an error shared by every measurement of the
molecule and vis is an error of its own. The value of ρ, 0, 0.3 or 0.7, sets how much
of the error the measurements share. The offsets have standard deviation 0 or 0.5 σe .
The training label is the mean of the molecule’s measurements. The methods remove


                                              179
each source’s offset, estimated by a mixed model with the source as a fixed effect and
the molecule as a random intercept. They also weight each molecule by the inverse of
its estimated error variance [284]. Two further arms use the true ρ and σe , which a
user never has; hence they are bounds and not methods.
   hERG IC50 carries its own known flag: how many times each molecule was mea-
sured, and how far apart those measurements are. A molecule is one canonical SMILES
string as given by ChEMBL. Its count is its number of ChEMBL IC50 records, and its
spread is the variance of their pIC50 values. Of the three methods that use the flag,
the first weights each molecule by its count. The second weights it by the inverse of
its estimated error variance, 1/(σu2 + s̃2i /ni ) [284]. Here ni is the number of measure-
ments behind the label, and σu2 is the error that remains however often a molecule is
measured. Its spread s̃2i is pulled toward the spread pooled over all molecules, as if the
pool were three more measurements. We do this because a variance from two or three
records is unreliable [285]. The third moves each label toward an out-of-fold LightGBM
prediction, further for a less precise label. The share moved is vi /(vi + τ 2 ), where vi is
the molecule’s spread over its count and τ 2 is the variance of the out-of-fold residual.
Held-out molecules are graded only if they have three or more measurements.


Every molecule

One method moves every label, with no selection at all. Each label moves halfway
toward the out-of-fold prediction of a LightGBM model fitted with a Huber loss [98],
whose threshold is 1.4826 times the median absolute out-of-fold residual. The Huber
loss keeps the outlying labels from pulling that prediction.


7.2.4     Noise-robust training
Five experiments test noise-robust training (Table 2.3). They change the downstream
model, rerun the twelve published methods, use a robust loss, exchange molecules
between two learners, or use repeat measurements.


Changing the model instead of the labels

A model can be fitted more carefully to noisy labels with no label changed, and that is
the comparison point for every mitigation result. One experiment changes the down-
stream model in four ways. Retuning searches 20 random settings of the model plus
its default, scores each on three inner folds split by scaffold family of the damaged
training labels, and refits the best. Its control runs the same search on the clean labels

                                            180
and fits the chosen setting to the damaged ones. No real user has that control, but it
tells us whether a gain comes from tuning against the noise or from tuning at all. Seed
averaging fits ten copies of LightGBM, XGBoost or the network with different random
seeds, each tree model on 80% of the molecules and features, and averages their predic-
tions. Stacking combines the dataset’s two downstream models by non-negative least
squares on their out-of-fold predictions [286]. The damage is each regression kind of
Table 7.1, and Gaussian noise whose variance is a multiple, 0.1, 0.3 or 1.0, of a Light-
GBM model’s out-of-fold mean squared error on the clean labels. This experiment ran
on Buchwald–Hartwig, Caco-2, hERG Ki , LogD and QM9, though not under every
damage kind on each.


The expanded published sweep

The twelve published methods of Chapter 4 are run again (Table 7.5). Every one uses
the same network as the plain model, with three hidden layers of 256, 128 and 64 units
and dropout 0.2. It is trained with the Adam optimizer at a learning rate of 10−3 for
up to 200 epochs, with early stopping. The code is a new implementation of the same
twelve, not a rerun of the earlier study. It is wider in four ways: it runs on many
datasets instead of QM9 alone, under every damage kind instead of Gaussian noise
alone, and on hybrids as well as single representations. It also scores each method
against the plain network on the same damaged labels in the same feature space. Four
of the twelve select molecules with a detector and delete them, so under Table 2.3
they are noise mitigation, and the table marks them so. Most were built for image
classification, and several of our versions differ from their source, as the table states.


Robust losses

The network is also trained with a Huber loss [98], whose threshold is c times 1.4826
times the median absolute out-of-fold residual, with c of 0.5, 1, 1.5 or 2. Under the shift
in 10% sized by model error, each model’s own robust loss is tried at c = 1.5. That is
a Huber loss for LightGBM and the network, a pseudo-Huber loss for XGBoost, and
absolute-error splits for the random forest.


Two learners

Two learners, LightGBM and an SVM, each score every molecule by its out-of-fold
error. For two rounds, each is refitted on the molecules the other finds easiest. Each
then keeps its own easiest 80%, 90% or 95%, and the downstream model is fitted on

the molecules both keep. This follows the exchange of co-teaching [154], done between
whole refits rather than inside one training loop. Its control deletes the same number
of molecules at random, at three seeds.


Repeat measurements

On hERG IC50 , four methods change what the model sees rather than the labels.
The first trains on one row per measurement instead of one row per molecule with the
mean label. The second adds the log of each molecule’s measurement count as an extra
feature, and the third gives NGBoost the same feature. The fourth fits an XGBoost
accelerated-failure-time model [293] to the interval between each molecule’s lowest and
highest measurement, instead of to one value.


7.2.5    Evaluation and controls
Each group has its own test, because each makes a different claim.

   • Noise detection. Is the suspicion score high on the damaged molecules? This
     can only be read where some labels were damaged and others were not (Sec-
     tion 7.2.2). On QM9 with no noise added, every flag is a false flag.

   • Noise mitigation, selected by a detector or a flag. The held-out score after
     the action, against the same model on the same features with no action. Where
     a control ran, the action must also beat the same action on molecules chosen at
     random.

   • Noise mitigation on every molecule. Against no action. No random selection
     is possible, so QM9 with no noise added is the only check.

   • Noise-robust training. Against the same model trained normally on the same
     labels. A gain on QM9 with no noise added comes from the training change
     alone, and we set it beside the gain under damage.

   Five controls ran, each described with its experiment. Random deletion is the
control for deleting flagged molecules and for the two learners, and random put-back
for cliff put-back. Shuffled detector scores are the control for cleaning the labels as
delivered, and shuffled lifts for the per-molecule lift. Settings tuned on clean labels
are the control for retuning. Each seed of a control is reported on its own. The other
methods have no control, which makes their gains weaker evidence.



                                          183
   QM9 is the clean-label control, and the most direct test in this chapter of whether
a method removes noise. Its HOMO–LUMO gaps are computed rather than measured
[109], so they carry no assay error. Every other dataset is measured, and its labels as
delivered carry noise that nobody has marked. A method that removes noise should
do nothing on QM9, or make it worse, because there is no noise to find. A gain on
QM9 with no noise added comes from a change that would help on any labels, such as
a better-trained model. The result we look for is a method that leaves QM9 unchanged
and improves a measured dataset’s labels as delivered. That is the strongest evidence
we have that a method found real noise. A gain on the labels as delivered that QM9
matches is not such evidence. The shuffled-score control cannot make this distinction
alone, because it asks whether the choice of molecules matters, not whether there
is noise. We therefore set each method’s result on QM9 beside its gains elsewhere
(Section 7.3.2).
   Where a clean score exists, we also report how much of the lost score a method
wins back. Each model m is fitted to the clean, the damaged and the treated training
                                clean , sdamaged and streated in the dataset’s own metric.
labels, giving held-out scores sm        m            m

The recovery is
                                        sm          m
                                         treated − sdamaged
                       Recoverym = 100 × m                  %,                       (7.6)
                                         sclean − sm
                                                   damaged

which reads the same whether higher or lower values of the metric are better. It is left
undefined when the gap between the clean and damaged score is 2% of the clean score
or less. We report it for single representations only. A hybrid’s features are re-ranked
on the damaged labels, so its clean score is not the score of the same features, and the
denominator would mix two changes.


7.3     Results
Three things hold for every result in this chapter. First, because of time and compute,
most results are on the first outer fold only (Section 5.2.5), and we give the fold count
wherever more exist. Second, where a method has several settings, such as a share
to delete or a strength, we report its best setting on the held-out score; because the
single representation and the hybrid are picked the same way, every reported gain is
optimistic. Third, the network’s score changes from one fit to the next. On QM9
its second hybrid scores between 0.348 and 0.362 RMSE on the same fold and labels,
depending on which experiment fitted it, and 0.364 in one run of the published sweep
(Table 7.33). LightGBM gives one score in every experiment. A network gain of up to


                                           184
about 0.016 RMSE is inside that spread.


7.3.1    Overall effect of noise handling
Additional file 38 counts how often each group of methods improved the held-out score
on the first outer fold. Mitigation helps most often under injected damage, and least
often on the labels as delivered.


7.3.2    The clean-label test: where noise is removed
QM9 separates the two things a gain can mean (Section 7.2.5). A method that re-
moves noise should leave QM9’s computed labels alone and improve measured labels
as delivered. A method that also helps QM9 is, at least in part, a better way to train.
We apply this test to each kind of method here, before the sections that go through
them one by one.
   Deletion passes the test. On QM9 with no noise added it made every model worse,
in every configuration of Table 7.11. Across the other QM9 runs it gained at most 0.010
RMSE on the best single representation or a hybrid, about the size of the network’s
spread between fits. Deleting 20% of the molecules flagged by the forest residual does
the same on QM9’s labels (Table 7.12). On SMAC colloidal stability and expression
titer, the same deletion improved the labels as delivered and beat every shuffled control,
and so did deletion on the weaker configurations of nanobody thermostability and AC-
SINS self-association. These are the clearest cases in the chapter of a method finding
noise that was already in the data. On the small-molecule labels as delivered, deletion
found almost nothing to remove (Section 7.3.3).
   Label smoothing and weighting pass the test only in part. With the network they
improve QM9 with no noise added (Tables 7.10 and 7.12, and the QM9 row of Fig-
ure 7.3). LightGBM, which gives the same score on every fit, barely moves on QM9.
On Caco-2, LogD and MDR1–MDCK efflux as delivered, the network gains about as
much as it does on QM9, so those gains are not evidence of noise. The support vector
regressor, which also gives the same score on every fit, gains almost nothing from any
cleanup on these labels. Label smoothing on hERG Ki is the exception among the
small molecules. Relative to its score, the network’s first single representation gains
several times what the same cleanup gives on QM9 (Table 7.12).
   QM9 also shows the limit of the shuffled-score control. With the network, label
smoothing on QM9’s first single representation beats all three shuffles, although there
is no noise to find (Table 7.12). The shuffles themselves move about as much as the

                                           185
network does between fits. Passing the shuffled control with the network is therefore
weak evidence by itself, and we read it beside QM9.
   Changing the model fails the test, so its gains are better training more than noise
handling. On QM9 with no noise added, stacking the two models improves every
configuration, and averaging network seeds improves most of them (Table 7.30). On
the labels as delivered of hERG Ki and LogD the same changes help by about as much.
Under injected Gaussian noise, seed averaging on hERG Ki gains more than on the
labels as delivered, in every configuration (Table 7.29). On QM9 it also gains more
under the same noise than with none added, with both models in every configuration.
Part of its gain under damage does respond to the noise.
   The twelve published methods pass the test in the other direction. On QM9 with
no noise added each one made the network worse in the first run (Table 7.33). In a
second run about half beat the plain network, by 0.001 to 0.021 RMSE, about the size
of the network’s own spread. Where one of them helps a measured dataset as delivered,
the gain is therefore more likely noise found. The heteroscedastic GP on ∆∆G is the
clearest case: it raises the best single representation’s Spearman and makes QM9 worse
(Tables 7.32 and 7.33).
   Noise is removed most clearly, then, where deletion or a published method helps an
antibody dataset as delivered and does nothing for QM9. On the small-molecule and
reaction labels as delivered, three results clear the bar: label smoothing on hERG Ki
and on hERG blocking, and deletion on Buchwald–Hartwig. Under injected damage,
moving every label toward a Huber-fitted prediction clears it too: it helped hERG Ki
under three kinds of damage and made QM9 worse (Section 7.3.5). The test rests on
one computed property of one dataset, on the first outer fold, and a few methods have
no QM9 result (Section 7.4).


7.3.3    Changing a label against deleting a molecule
Under hERG’s label flips every action helped, and deleting the flagged molecules helped
most (Table 7.6). This holds for gradient-boosted trees on both the hybrid and the best
single representation, with 10% of labels flipped along a confusion matrix. Deletion is
the one action here with a control, and it passes it. With the neighbor-label gap as the
detector, deleting takes the hybrid to 0.838 AUROC against 0.818 for the best of five
random deletions of the same size. With the forest residual as the detector, deletion
beats the best random deletion on all three folds that have results (Table 7.7).
   Figure 7.2 shows how this depends on the share flagged, on one outer fold. Un-
