der both flip kinds, deleting the flagged molecules beat the best random deletion at
every flagged share, on the hybrid and on the best single representation. The best
random deletion is picked on the held-out score, so the control is optimistic. Under the
confusion-matrix flips the hybrid started below the single representation and finished
above it after every action. On Caco-2, censored EM lowered the network’s RMSE
at every clipped share, and deleting the clipped molecules raised it. At 40% clipped,

   On a regression dataset the three actions differ less. On LogD with 5% outliers,
every action improved the network on the first single representation, and the score-
scaled shrink toward an out-of-fold prediction and deletion improved it most. On the
first hybrid, down-weighting is best, and the shrink makes it slightly worse (Table 7.8).
Deletion beats random deletion on the second hybrid but loses to it on the first hybrid.
On the second fold the same deletion makes the second hybrid worse (Table 7.7). On
the third and fourth folds it improves the second hybrid but loses to random deletion.
   Which detector selects the molecules to delete matters more than which action


                                          188
follows. We deleted the top 10% by each of five detectors under the same hERG flips.
The cliff-aware gap helped the hybrid most, then the GP, while the variance-head
network, NGBoost and the heteroscedastic GP helped it least (Table 7.6). On the
single representation the last three make things worse. The three that predict a spread
from the features alone never read a label (Section 7.2.2), so they cannot see a flipped
one. On LogD with outliers the GP was best for the network’s hybrid (Table 7.8). On
a second fold of the hERG flips, in its first noise draw, none of the five improved the
single representation, and only the cliff-aware gap improved the hybrid. Averaged over
all three draws of that fold, the cliff-aware gap improved both, and the three that never
read a label made both worse.

   A second test of whether the gain comes from the detector uses sets of flagged
molecules whose content we control. On hERG Ki with wide errors in 5% of molecules,
we down-weighted the 10% of molecules the residual flags. This takes the network on
the first single representation from 0.689 to 0.642 RMSE, and improves the hybrid by
a similar amount (Table 7.9). Down-weighting the same number of molecules, none of
them damaged, makes both worse, so the gain depends on which molecules the action
reaches. On QM9 with no noise added, down-weighting the residual’s top 10% made
both configurations slightly worse.
   Weighting keeps every molecule and every label, and it helped on hERG, LogD
and Buchwald–Hartwig (Table 7.10). Under the hERG flips it helped the hybrid and
barely moved the single representation. On QM9 with no noise added, neighbor weights

   Figure 7.3 shows, on the first outer fold only, how often each method improved on

   Additional file 39 counts, for each broad noise mitigation method, the conditions
where it improved the best single representation, the hybrid, both or neither.
   On the first outer fold, deleting almost never helped on clean labels. On QM9
with no noise added, the best deletion setting took the network’s hybrid from 0.355
to 0.393 RMSE, and LightGBM’s hybrid and every other configuration also got worse
(Table 7.11). Two further deletion experiments on QM9 gave the same result: at its
best setting, deletion made the best single representation and the best hybrid worse
with both models. In a third, deleting the top 10% by each of five detectors made the
first hybrid worse with both models and every detector. Its only gains were on single
representations, of 0.004 RMSE. When deleting helps on a damaged dataset and beats
random deletion, as it does under the hERG flips, the gain is noise removed.

The labels as delivered

On hERG Ki ’s labels as delivered, label smoothing passes the shuffled-score control
with both detectors (Table 7.12). Label smoothing with the neighbor gap takes the net-
work on the first single representation from 0.638 to 0.578 RMSE, while every shuffled
run makes it slightly worse. With the forest residual, label smoothing also improves
the hybrid by more than any of three shuffles. In a second experiment it improved the
network’s hybrid on each of folds 0, 1 and 2, while the second single representation got
worse on fold 0 and better on the other two (Table 7.13). On hERG blocking, label
smoothing beats all its shuffles with gradient-boosted trees on the first single repre-
sentation and on the second hybrid. Caco-2 and LogD pass the control too, but by
small amounts. With down-weighting on the network’s second single representation,
one shuffle wins back more than half of the gain on each. On MDR1–MDCK efflux,
label smoothing and down-weighting also beat all three shuffles with the network, and
the support vector regressor gains nothing from any cleanup.

   Deleting does not pass its control on the small-molecule labels as delivered. On
Caco-2, hERG Ki and LogD no deletion setting beat both no cleaning and every
random deletion. On MDR1–MDCK efflux one setting did: deleting the 5% with the
largest neighbor-label gap took the network’s first hybrid from 0.466 to 0.446 RMSE,
against 0.450 for the best random deletion. That margin is inside the network’s spread
between fits. Buchwald–Hartwig is different: deleting the 20% of reactions with the
largest neighbor-label gap improves LightGBM on the hybrid and on the second single
representation, and beats random deletion on both (Table 7.7). On the hybrid it scores
18.0 RMSE in percentage points of yield, against 19.9 for the best random deletion.
Random deletion alone also lowered the RMSE a little, from 20.1 to 19.9 on the hybrid
and from 20.2 to 19.9 on the second single representation.
   Putting activity-cliff molecules back after a cleanup was tested on hERG Ki and
QM9. On Caco-2, hERG and LogD more than half of the training molecules count
as cliff molecules, which leaves too few other molecules to draw a random set of the
same size. On hERG Ki ’s first outer fold, deleting the flagged molecules barely moves
the network’s hybrid. Putting the cliff molecules back takes it to 0.579 RMSE, against
0.595 with random molecules put back. Fold 2 agrees, and fold 1 does not (Table 7.14).
On QM9 with no noise added, 19 of 7,982 training molecules count as cliff molecules.
There, deleting the flagged molecules made the network’s hybrid worse, 0.349 to 0.382
RMSE, and putting the cliff molecules back recovered part of that, to 0.372. Putting
random molecules back left it at 0.382.

7.3.4     Detection of the suspect labels
This section uses injected damage alone (Section 7.2.2). The out-of-fold forest residual
finds outliers, shifted labels and flipped classes well above chance on every dataset it ran
on (Table 7.15). On Caco-2 under outlier noise its average precision is 0.338, against a
chance level of 0.051. Censoring is the exception on small molecules and reactions. At
a clipped share of 0.25 the residual falls below chance on Buchwald–Hartwig, LogD and
QM9, and is only a little above it on Caco-2 and hERG Ki . On the antibody datasets
it stays above chance, and well above it on SMAC colloidal stability. A model fitted

behind, and under censoring it trails the residual on Caco-2, hERG Ki and LogD at
every setting (Table 7.15). No detector is best everywhere: a neighbor detector is
better at a shift that moves a few labels a long way, and a model residual is as good
or better at outliers.
   The space in which the neighbors are found changes detection little (Section 7.2.2).
Taking the best setting in each family, the importance-weighted features were best
on Buchwald–Hartwig, Caco-2, hERG Ki , LogD, MDR1–MDCK efflux and SMAC
colloidal stability (Table 7.16). On Caco-2 under outliers the four families reach average
precisions close to each other, all far above chance. The hybrid’s own features were
the best family on the change in binding free energy (∆∆G) under outliers. They were
also best on SMAC colloidal stability under censoring, on QM9 under outliers and on
MDR1–MDCK efflux under the shift in 10%, but on no dataset overall. Averaged over
the detector settings instead of taking the best one, the hybrid’s features come out
ahead more often, so this ranking depends on how a detector’s setting is chosen.
   Scaling each feature by its importance before the neighbors are found raised detec-
tion under the shift in 10% on every dataset but hERG Ki and CHO polyreactivity,
where it was unchanged or slightly lower, and lowered it under censoring on every
dataset (Table 7.17). The largest gain was on QM9, 0.538 to 0.634 average precision,
then Buchwald–Hartwig, 0.465 to 0.512, with smaller gains on LogD and SMAC col-
loidal stability, while under outliers scaling barely changed detection. Importance is
computed against the damaged labels, so under censoring it likely rewards the fea-
tures that predict the clipped values, and the clipped molecules then look like their
neighbors.

uncertainty detector that could be graded. The other three predict a spread from
the features alone. Every damage kind that can be graded picks its molecules at ran-

the cleanlab score was well ahead of the residual on Buchwald–Hartwig. Each number
is a mean over the detector’s settings, and the Shapley value has many more settings
than the residual.
   Two more experiments graded the plain residual under damage sized by the model’s
own error. It ranked the shifted molecules above chance on every dataset there, for
example an average precision of 0.20 to 0.25 against a chance level of 0.106 on expression
titer. Turning such a ranking into a decision is harder. Conformal p-values with a false-
discovery-rate rule flagged no molecule at q = 0.05 or q = 0.1 when the calibration
set was drawn from the noisy training labels. With a clean calibration set, which a
user would not have, they flagged 10 molecules at q = 0.1 in one of 120 passes on the
measured datasets. On QM9 with outliers or a shift injected, the clean calibration set
flagged 23 to 485 molecules in 21 of 28 passes at q = 0.1, and flagged molecules in 9
passes at q = 0.05. Acting on those flags left the held-out score where it was in almost
every pass.
   The break-even precision explains when acting on a detector can work (Sec-
tion 7.2.3). With wide errors in 5% of molecules, the residual’s top 5% are mostly
damaged molecules, and its precision is above the break-even on Caco-2 and hERG Ki
(Table 7.18). Under the shift sized by model error, the residual’s precision falls below
the break-even for deletion. On Caco-2 the residual’s top 5% has a precision of 0.171,
and deleting them only pays off above 0.616. On hERG Ki under the plain shift in


                                           201
10%, the precision of the top 10% is also far below the break-even for deletion. A
detector that ranks the damaged labels above chance can still lead to a worse model:
acting on its flags helps only above the break-even, and the break-even depends on
the damage, the action and the model.

7.3.5    Effect by noise type
Labels clipped at an assay limit

Clipping at an assay limit is the damage we could most often undo, and undoing it
needs no detector. With a quarter of the training labels clipped (Figure 7.7), for every
model and configuration censored EM gives a lower held-out RMSE than training on
the clipped labels, but not as low as training on the labels before clipping. On LogD,
the network on the best single representation wins back about three quarters of the lost
RMSE on outer fold 0 (Equation 7.6). QM9 shows the same pattern for the network
and LightGBM, and Caco-2 at a smaller scale.
   The gain holds on the further folds: on LogD folds 0 to 4, censored EM improves
the network’s hybrid over the clipped labels on every fold, and stays short of the score
before clipping. It also beats the clipped labels on Caco-2 folds 1 and 2 (Table 7.19).
In this run hERG Ki and QM9 have only fold 0; a separate five-fold run on QM9 is
described at the end of this section.

brought the random forest back to about its score before clipping (Additional file 41).

   Deleting the clipped molecules is worse than leaving the labels clipped on Caco-2,
hERG Ki and QM9 (Figure 7.7), and on MDR1–MDCK efflux with both models in
every configuration. On LogD it beats the clipped labels with the network on the
hybrid, but stays far behind the lift. Without the clipped molecules, the top of the
label range is missing from training.
   The per-molecule lift tests whether the size of each lift matters. On LogD, the
network on the hybrid scores 0.499 RMSE with each molecule’s own lift, against 0.463
for the best of three shuffles. On LogD and Caco-2 every shuffle beats the molecule’s
own lift in every configuration, on hERG Ki in the second single representation, and
on MDR1–MDCK efflux in both hybrids (Table 7.21). On QM9 the first hybrid’s own
lift beats one shuffle of three and loses to the other two. What matters is knowing
which labels are clipped and raising them by about the right average amount, which is
what the constant lift does.

   In a separate run on all five outer folds, we also clipped a quarter of QM9’s training

A shared offset per scaffold group

Under grouped-shifted noise, subtracting each scaffold family’s estimated offset worked
on Caco-2, hERG Ki , LogD and QM9, the four datasets with all three noise draws.
On Caco-2, LogD and QM9 it improved the network on the hybrid on every damaged
draw, and stayed short of the score on clean labels (Table 7.22). On the first Caco-2
draw the random-intercept model took it from 0.321 to 0.280 RMSE, and it was best
on every Caco-2 and QM9 draw and on the first two LogD draws. On QM9 it took the
network’s hybrid from 0.909 to 0.507 RMSE on the first draw. On hERG Ki neither
of the two was best on every draw. On MDR1–MDCK efflux, with two draws, the
random-intercept model improved the hybrid on both, by less, and subtracting family
means made the second draw worse.

Several sources per molecule

With simulated sources, removing each source’s offset made little difference on small
molecules. On hERG Ki the network on the hybrid scored 0.742 RMSE with the plain
mean of the measurements and 0.748 after removing the offsets. Buchwald–Hartwig,
where the sources’ offsets were large against the label spread, is where removing them
helped most often. On the other datasets, QM9 among them, it helped in about half
the settings or fewer. Even on Buchwald–Hartwig the gain can be small, and the
random forest on the hybrid stayed far from its score on clean labels.


Gaussian noise, outliers and shifts in a few labels

Moving every label halfway toward a Huber-fitted prediction, with no detector at all,
helped the network on hERG Ki under each of the three kinds of damage it ran on.
Under Gaussian noise at level 1.0 the second hybrid went from 0.718 to 0.681 RMSE.
Under the scaffold-family shift the second hybrid improved too, and under outliers
the first single representation did. All three gains held on each of the three draws
(Table 7.24). On QM9 with no noise added the same blend made every configuration
worse with both models, for example the network’s second hybrid from 0.362 to 0.368
RMSE. By the clean-label test, its gains on hERG Ki are noise removed.


7.3.6    Effect by dataset
On five antibody datasets, cleaning the labels as delivered passes the shuffled-score
control in some configurations, and there it gave the largest gains in this chapter.

The antibody datasets

SMAC colloidal stability gives the strongest controlled result on any labels as delivered
(Table 7.12). Deleting the 20% of antibodies the forest residual flags takes the support
vector regressor on the first single representation from 0.274 to 0.403 Spearman, and
the same deletion also helps its second hybrid. Moving the flagged labels toward an out-
of-fold prediction helps the network on both the single representation and the hybrid.
In all four cases, the same cleanup driven by shuffled detector scores gives a lower score
on every shuffle. Fold 0 holds out only 53 antibodies, but the shuffles were fitted on
the same fold and stayed low.
   ∆∆G and expression titer also pass the shuffled control on their labels as delivered,
in all four cases below (Table 7.12). On ∆∆G, label smoothing takes the network’s
first single representation from 0.341 to 0.484 Spearman, and also helps its hybrid. On
expression titer, deletion helps the random forest’s second single representation and its
second hybrid. On both datasets the cleaned single representation still scores above the
cleaned hybrid. On nanobody thermostability and AC-SINS self-association, deletion
passed the shuffled control mainly where the starting score was low. On nanobody
thermostability it took the network’s second single representation from 0.548 to 0.691
Spearman. Where the starting score was highest, the gain was small or a shuffle did
as well, as for the GP on AC-SINS’s first single representation.
   Neighbor-based weights, which have no control, gave gains of the same size. On


                                           210
SMAC, weighting by agreement with the neighbors took the network on the first single
representation from 0.065 to 0.209 Spearman, and also helped it on the hybrid (Ta-
ble 7.25). CHO polyreactivity, the antibody set with the fewest noise results, has no
result for cleaning the labels as delivered, and we do not draw a conclusion from it.

                       Configuration    Unweighted       Weighted
                       First single             0.065      0.209
                       First hybrid              0.227      0.370

Table 7.25: Weighting by agreement with the neighbors on SMAC colloidal stability’s
labels as delivered. Network, outer fold 0. One row is one configuration. Held-out
Spearman, higher is better.

   The held-out folds of these datasets are small, 44 to 135 antibodies, and several start
from weak scores, so large gains are easier to see and weaker evidence. The shuffled
controls are why we still count SMAC, ∆∆G and expression titer as real results, and
nanobody thermostability and AC-SINS on their weaker configurations.

Small molecules and the reaction set

On the small-molecule labels as delivered, two label smoothing gains pass their control
and do not rest on the network’s spread between fits. One is the network’s on hERG
Ki , the other XGBoost’s on hERG blocking (Section 7.3.3). Buchwald–Hartwig is the
one dataset where deleting flagged reactions from the labels as delivered beats random
deletion.

Repeat measurements on hERG IC50

hERG IC50 is the one dataset whose noise was measured rather than injected (Sec-
tion 7.2). No noise is added here; each draw re-draws which measurements form each
held-out molecule’s label. Weighting each molecule by its number of measurements
took the support vector regressor on the hybrid from 0.760 to 0.728 RMSE on the
first draw, and helped on all three draws (Table 7.26). Weighting by the inverse of
the estimated error variance also helped it, by less. The same count weights made
the network worse. For the network, adding the log of the measurement count as
a feature helped instead, and so did shrinking each label toward a prediction by its
spread. NGBoost gained a little from the same extra feature, and the interval model
did slightly worse than the point model. Training on one row per measurement instead
of the mean barely changed the score for either model. ChEMBL curators flagged 301
of the 12,008 records, most of them as outside the typical range. Graded against these

                                          211
flags on the first outer fold, the neighbor-label gap and the forest residual ranked the
flagged records above chance in every configuration, with average precisions of 0.14 to
0.23 against a chance level of 0.036.

7.3.7    Cleaning inside the feature search
Which of the four combinations does best changes from row to row (Section 7.2.3;
Table 7.27). Cleaning both the search and the fit does best in about half the rows. On
expression titer’s labels as delivered, deleting the 20% of antibodies the forest residual
flags takes the network on the hybrid from 0.155 to 0.562 Spearman this way. Cleaning
only the fit, or only the search, also helps, by less. On SMAC colloidal stability with
a quarter of the labels clipped, deleting the flagged 5% also does best when it reaches
both, while cleaning the search alone does worse than no cleaning.
   The largest single gain is on SMAC colloidal stability’s labels as delivered (Ta-
ble 7.27). There the network on the second hybrid goes from −0.014 to 0.560 Spearman

                                           212
when the labels are shrunk toward an out-of-fold prediction for the search alone. That
starts from a score near zero, on one fold of 53 antibodies. Cleaning before the search
can also go the other way. Deleting flagged antibodies before the search made the
network’s first hybrid on SMAC worse. On hERG Ki under Gaussian noise, deleting
before the search helped the second hybrid on the first noise draw and hurt it on the
second. On hERG Ki ’s labels as delivered, the one dataset with further folds here,
label smoothing before the search beat no cleaning on folds 1 and 2 and lost on fold
0. This experiment has no random-flag control, and on QM9 with no noise added only
the fit was cleaned, never the search.

   A separate experiment searched the hybrid on clean or damaged labels, then fitted
both on labels with Gaussian noise at level 1.0 and scored them on held-out labels as
delivered. On Caco-2 the network’s second hybrid scores within a few thousandths of
an RMSE between the two arms on every fold, and neither arm is ahead on all five


                                          213
(Table 7.28). On LogD with the support vector regressor the two arms agree to the
third decimal on two of three folds. The largest loss was on hERG Ki with the network,
0.669 against 0.779 RMSE on the second fold. On SMAC colloidal stability the two
arms swap places from fold to fold. On ∆∆G they differ by up to 0.29 Spearman on
one fold, in both directions across folds, and on QM9 they stay within 0.013 RMSE of
each other. For most datasets, the search itself is not where Gaussian noise does its
damage.


7.3.8     Changing the model instead of the labels
On hERG Ki with Gaussian noise at level 1.0, averaging ten network seeds takes the
network on the second hybrid from 0.718 to 0.656 RMSE (Table 7.29). Stacking the
two downstream models and retuning the network on the damaged labels also help, by
less, and none of the three reaches the score on clean labels. Seed averaging won on
each of the three noise draws. These gains are as large as the best mitigation results
on the same dataset, and they need no detector and change no label. On QM9 with
the same noise, seed averaging takes the network’s second hybrid from 0.547 to 0.476,
a larger gain than the 0.357 to 0.346 it gives with no noise added (Table 7.30).
   On the labels as delivered they help too, but not everywhere (Table 7.30). They
help QM9 with no noise added by about as much, so on these labels their gains are
better training, not noise found (Section 7.3.2). On LogD, seed averaging improves
the network on both its best single representation and its best hybrid. On hERG Ki
the network’s best single representation, the second, does not improve with any of the
three, and retuning makes both hybrids worse. Stacking scores the same whichever
model it is compared with, because it combines both. Against the support vector
regressor it therefore compares two models with the weaker one alone, for example
0.495 to 0.408 RMSE on LogD’s second single representation.
   Under Gaussian noise on hERG Ki , retuning on the damaged labels mostly beats
reusing settings tuned on clean labels (Table 7.29). On the network’s second hybrid,
the setting tuned on damaged labels scores 0.691 and the setting tuned on clean labels
0.703. On its first hybrid the clean-label settings are slightly ahead, 0.709 against
0.712. With a quarter of the labels clipped, the clean-label settings did better in every
configuration. Retuning on the clipped labels also rarely beat the plain model.
   The other noise-robust training methods are in Additional file 42. A Huber loss
for the network helped under outliers on hERG Ki , and made Gaussian noise worse at
every threshold. Each model’s own robust loss helped under the shift sized by model

   The method that does best on the hybrid is rarely the one that does best on both
single representations. The three exceptions on the first hybrid are Caco-2 under the
shift in 10% (distance-based selection), hERG under class-dependent flips (confident
learning) and hERG under instance-dependent flips (distance-based selection). A rank-
ing of noise-handling methods made on one representation does not carry to another.


7.3.9    Effect by model and representation
Cleaning keeps the hybrid ahead on small molecules. Before any treatment the hybrid
already beats the best single representation in almost every small-molecule condition,
and after mitigation it still does. On LogD with outliers, for example, the network’s
cleaned hybrid scores 0.556 against 0.591 for the cleaned single representation (Ta-


                                          219
ble 7.10). Cleaning rarely changes which of the two wins, and when it does it more
often moves the win to the hybrid. On antibodies the hybrid is ahead in about half
the conditions, before and after cleaning. The reaction set sits between the two.
   The closest test of the question this thesis started from, whether a better feature set
makes noise handling work, is where the detector finds its neighbors. One experiment
down-weights molecules by the neighbor gap and finds the neighbors either in the
model’s own single representation or in the hybrid, with everything else held fixed.
Finding them in the hybrid helped the same model on the same single representation
slightly more often. The largest differences go both ways. On LogD under a scaffold-
family shift, the network’s single representation goes from 0.705 to 0.663 RMSE with
neighbors in the hybrid and gets worse with neighbors in its own space. On nanobody
thermostability with outliers, the network barely moves with its own neighbors and
gains with the hybrid’s. On expression titer under Gaussian noise the reverse happens,
and its own neighbors help more than the hybrid’s (Table 7.34). These are the largest
cases picked out of many, not typical ones, and this comparison has no control.

                                                        No       Own        Hybrid’s
   Dataset and damage            Configuration
                                                  weighting neighbors      neighbors
   RMSE (lower is better)
   LogD, scaffold-family shift   First single         0.705        0.753       0.663

   The network gains from cleaning more often than the support vector regressor on
Caco-2, hERG Ki and LogD. Part of that may be the network’s own spread between
fits, since a deterministic model with an unchanged score never counts as helped. On
∆∆G the support vector regressor gains more often, and on expression titer the random
forest gains more often than the network.
   On small molecules, a user who builds a hybrid loses nothing by cleaning afterwards.
Whether a noise-handling method works depends on the feature space it runs in, as
Chapter 4 found for distance.




                                          220
7.4      Conclusions
On the first outer fold, we found noisy labels in QSAR data and, in several settings,
corrected them. QM9, the one dataset with computed labels, gave the cleanest test
of whether a method removes noise or trains a better model (Section 7.3.2). Deletion
and the published methods helped QM9’s clean labels by at most about the network’s
own spread. Where they helped an antibody dataset as delivered, they found noise
that was already in the data. Label smoothing and weighting improved QM9 as well,
as did changes to the model. Their gains on measured labels as delivered are therefore
largely better training, with label smoothing on hERG Ki the exception. Moving every
label toward a Huber-fitted prediction helped hERG Ki under damage and made QM9
worse, so its gains are noise removed. Corrections worked best with a flag: raising labels
clipped at an assay limit won back about half to over four fifths of the lost RMSE on
the small-molecule datasets, with no detector. With a detector, deleting or relabeling
flagged molecules helped under label flips and outliers. Cleanups driven by a detector
passed random or shuffled controls under hERG’s label flips. They also passed on
the labels as delivered of hERG Ki , Buchwald–Hartwig and five antibody datasets, on
two of these only in their weaker configurations, though QM9 shows that the shuffled
control alone is weak evidence with the network. The hybrid stayed ahead of the
single representation on small molecules in almost every condition, before and after
treatment. Finding a detector’s neighbors in the hybrid rather than in the model’s
own single representation helped slightly more often, with large differences in both
directions. Which noise-handling method works best thus depends on the feature space
it runs in.
   Most results rest on the first outer fold; the further folds that exist mostly agree
with it, and the text gives the exceptions. The results to confirm first are, in order, the
cleaning of SMAC colloidal stability’s labels as delivered and relabeling and cliff put-
back on hERG Ki , then cleaning under hERG’s flips, the clipped-label corrections and
the scaffold-family offsets. Cleaning before the search gave large gains, but on QM9
with no noise added only the fit was cleaned, never the search, so its gains cannot be
split into noise removed and a better model. Chapter 8 uses these results to answer the
research questions of Chapter 1 and to set out how to choose a noise-handling method
for a new dataset.




                                           221
Chapter 8

Conclusions and Further Thoughts

This chapter answers the research questions of Chapter 1 on handling label noise in
molecular data, then sets out how to choose a noise-handling method and what the
thesis contributes.


8.1      Summary of findings
The first half of the thesis asked four questions about label noise, answered in Chapter 3.
First, model family decided noise robustness more than the representation did. On
QM9 under Gaussian noise, model family explained 47.2% of the variance in AUCnorm
and the representation far less, whereas the representation mattered more for predictive
performance. Label noise corrupts the labels and leaves the features untouched, so this
split is what one would expect. Second, random forests were the most consistent
model under noise. LightGBM and XGBoost predicted well on clean labels and lost
the most as noise rose, so a model’s score on clean labels says little about how it
holds up under noise. Third, the type of noise mattered more than the shape of
its distribution. Systematic noise, shared within a scaffold family or clipped at an
assay limit, did the most damage. A study that injects only Gaussian noise therefore
misses it. Fourth, mean predicted uncertainty rose with noise, but outside censoring
no model’s uncertainty identified the corrupted labels. Under censoring, dividing a
molecule’s error by its uncertainty picked out the clipped labels better than the error
alone.
   Chapter 4 asked why published methods for learning with noisy labels did poorly
in an earlier exploratory study. In that study’s QM9 sweep, the configurations with
the lowest retention all used a distance. The sweep added a distance to some methods
designed without one, so poor adaptation may explain part of this. Within one dataset,


                                           222
most features of a standard representation barely vary: on hERG Ki , 1,815 of the 2,048
ECFP4 features take one value in at least 95% of molecules. Constant features add
nothing to a distance, but every other feature adds a term. When only a few features
matter for the property, the rest dominate the distance. Any method that relies on
such a distance inherits the problem, which the adaptive hybrid representation was
built to repair.
   The second half asked whether the representation can be adapted to the task. First,
we found that the best set of features depends on the dataset and the downstream
model, and a search can find it (Chapter 5). The adaptive hybrid representation beat
the best single representation on every small-molecule dataset with both downstream
models. The method carried over from small molecules to antibodies, although there
the gain depended on the dataset. On the Buchwald–Hartwig reactions the hybrid
gained nothing beyond the spread across folds. On two antibody datasets the best
single representation was one built for this thesis, which reads only the regions or
mutations that vary. Second, distance in a hybrid tracked the label better than in the
single representation on all six small-molecule datasets, and predicting each label from
its five nearest neighbors was more accurate (Chapter 6). On antibodies and reactions,
distance tracked the label better too. However, prediction from neighbors was worse
on several of these datasets, so the benefit of a hybrid has to be measured on each one.
   Third, noisy labels could be found and corrected (Chapter 7). With a quarter of
LogD’s training labels clipped, censored EM removed most of the error that clipping
added to the neural network. Cleaning guided by a detector beat random deletion
or shuffled detector scores on hERG, hERG Ki , Buchwald–Hartwig and five antibody
datasets. On small molecules the hybrid stayed ahead of the single representation in
almost every condition, but not always with the network on hERG Ki .
   Fourth, feature importance helped in building the hybrid, but using the importance
scores again during training added little. The hybrid ranks features by importance
within each representation, and at the same number of features it beat recursive feature
elimination and random features at every budget on QM9, Caco-2, LogD and MDR1–
MDCK efflux. On hERG Ki with the DNN, both beat the hybrid at 1,000 and 2,000
features. For finding noisy labels, the best feature space for the neighbor detectors
depended on the dataset (Chapter 7).




                                          223
8.2     Choosing a noise-handling method
No single method handled label noise on every dataset. Under clipping, the forest
residual detector fell below chance on Buchwald–Hartwig, LogD and QM9, and acting
on the known flag did best. Subtracting scaffold-family offsets made the model worse
on Caco-2’s labels as delivered, and deleting the molecules the network fits worst helped
on the reactions but almost never on small molecules. Even the order of the methods
changed between the hybrid and the single representations, so a method chosen on one
representation may not be the best on another.
   We recommend modeling the noise in a dataset before choosing a method. Data
pooled from several laboratories can carry an offset per source, and an assay with a
reporting limit gives clipped labels; both come with a flag that a correction can use. A
method that assumes noise that is not there can do harm. Simple methods were often
as good as complex ones: random forests were the most consistent model, and on hERG
Ki one constant lift for every clipped label beat censored EM in most configurations.


8.3     Contributions
We make six contributions on label noise and three on the representation, in that order.

  1. NoiseInject, an open-source Python package for benchmarking noise robustness
      and uncertainty in QSAR models (Section 2.6.1).

  2. Noise conditions beyond Gaussian noise, including noise shared within scaffold
      families and labels clipped at an assay limit (Chapters 2 and 3).

  3. A split of the variance in performance and in noise robustness between model
      family and representation (Chapter 3).

  4. Published noise-handling methods adapted to QSAR data, for example censored
      EM with any downstream model, a random intercept per scaffold family, and the
      buddy check from meteorology as a detector of noisy labels (Chapter 7).

  5. Three methods for which we found no earlier use: the cliff-aware gap, cleaning
      the labels before the hybrid search, and returning activity-cliff molecules after a
      cleanup (Chapter 7).

  6. Tests of these methods on single representations and hybrids side by side, with
      three controls: QM9 with no noise added, random deletion and shuffled detector
      scores (Chapter 7).

                                          224
  7. The adaptive hybrid representation, which ranks features within each representa-
      tion, splits a budget of features among them, and searches the selection settings
      for each dataset and model (Chapter 5). A newly published embedding is added
      to the search as one more representation.

  8. Hybrids built for distance (Chapter 6), and hybrids built from data held at sep-
      arate sites (Chapter 5).

  9. Antibody representations built for this thesis: readouts of the complementarity-
      determining regions (CDRs) and of each region, mutation features, and the
      change in embedding from the parent antibody (Chapter 5).


8.4     Limitations
Most noise results rest on the first outer fold. QM9 is the only clean control, and most
noise methods have no control. With no ground truth for the labels as delivered, a
control can show that a gain beats chance but not that a label was corrected. The
hybrid needs data, and most antibody datasets here hold a few hundred antibodies.
We have not tested whether the best hybrid settings follow measurable properties of a
dataset (Section 5.3.1).


8.5     Future work
The hybrid search needs only several feature matrices for the same samples and a
supervised task. Gene-expression profiles and financial time series are natural next
domains, since each has many competing representations of the same samples, and so
are DNA promoter sequences. Beyond new domains, a hybrid could be the input to
the model that scores molecules for a generative model. Starting the search from the
settings found on a similar dataset may save compute, and a deployment across real
sites would test the separate-site results of Chapter 5.
   The noise methods need more clean controls: QM9 offers twelve computed prop-
erties, and only the HOMO–LUMO gap was used. In QSAR, a class label usually
comes from cutting a continuous measurement at a threshold, so measurement error
flips mostly the molecules near that threshold. Flips of that kind, on more classification
datasets, are the next test for the detectors.




                                           225
8.6     Concluding remarks
Label noise in molecular data can be handled. Which method handles it, however,
depends on the prediction task and the data, as well as on the model and the kind
of noise. No single model or representation was best on every dataset we tested,
and neither was any noise-handling method. A dataset’s noise should therefore be
understood before a method is chosen.
   The features matter as well: when the noise methods failed, we followed the failure
down to the distance between molecules and then to the features that distance is com-
puted over. The adaptive hybrid representation selects those features for each dataset
and model. On small molecules it predicted better than any single representation and
gave better neighbors. It stayed ahead, moreover, in almost every noise condition, but
not always with the network on hERG Ki .
   A new foundation model appears every few months in molecular and protein model-
ing, antibodies included. With the hybrid search, a researcher does not need to choose
between their current representation and a new one. They add the new embedding to
the search and rerun it, and the search decides whether the embedding contributes fea-
tures and which (tested on LogD and hERG blocking in Chapter 5). A choice usually
made by habit becomes one measured on each task.
